Freeness in higher order frame bundles
Scot Adams

Abstract
. We provide counterexamples to P. Olver’s freeness conjecture for C ∞ C^{\infty} actions. In fact, we show that a counterexample exists for any connected real Lie group with noncompact center, as well as for the additive group of the integers.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
57Sxx, 58A05, 58A20, 53A55

1.
Introduction

P. Olver’s freeness conjecture (in his words) asserts: “If a Lie
group acts effectively on a manifold, then, for some
n<\infty
, the
action is free on [a nonempty] open subset of the jet bundle of order
n
.”
There is some ambiguity in this wording:
No mention is made of connectedness of the group or manifold,
the particular choice of jet bundle isn’t made precise and
the smoothness of the action is left unspecified.

In this note, we provide counterexamples to one interpretation of the
freeness conjecture for
C^{\infty}
actions and higher order frame bundles.
Those who know Olver’s work will understand that there are a family
of associated jet bundles to which he generally refers,
and work in frame bundles then informs results in these jet bundles,
through the associated bundle construction.
In the
C^{\infty}
context, Olver has noted that,
to avoid elementary counterexamples,
“effective” must be strengthened to “fixpoint rare”,
which we define in §
2
below.
In Theorem
18.1
and Lemma
17.3
,
we show that a counterexample exists for any connected real Lie group
with noncompact center, as well as for the additive group of the integers.
We also prove (
[
Ad2
]
) the validity of the conjecture for
connected real Lie groups with compact center.
Finally, in
[
AO
]
,
we describe a certain “meager” modification of the
C^{\omega}
conjecture,
and prove it holds for all connected real Lie groups.

For any fixed group, the
C^{\infty}
conjecture implies the
C^{\omega}
conjecture,
so the
C^{\omega}
conjecture (on frame bundles) is now proved for connected real Lie groups with compact center.
In
[
Ad1
]
, we offer a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-action on a manifold with infinitely generated fundamental group
which, after induction of actions, provides a
C^{\omega}
counterexample for any connected Lie group with

noncompact
center.
There is the possibility that the construction in
Theorem
18.1
could be modified to make
counterexamples to the
C^{\omega}
conjecture on a contractible manifold,
e.g.
,
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
The main difficulty in such an extension appears to be technical, and revolves around
developing a good understanding of convergence of sequences in
C^{\omega}
with respect to some well-chosen topology.
For this, D. Morris’ unpublished note
[
M
]
may be useful.

The present writeup, also, is not intended for publication.

2.
Miscellaneous notation and terminology

A subset of a topological space is
meager
(a.k.a.
of first category
) if it is a countable union of nowhere dense sets.
A subset of a topological space is
nonmeager
(a.k.a.
of second category
) if it is not meager.
A subset of a topological space is
comeager
(a.k.a.
residual
) if its complement is meager.

Let
{\mathbb{N}}:=\{1,2,3,\ldots\}
.
Let
{\mathcal{I}}:=\{(-a,a)\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}\,|\,a\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
.
For every
I\in{\mathcal{I}}
, let
a_{I}:=\sup I
, so
a_{I}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
I=(-a_{I},a_{I})
.
For every
I\in{\mathcal{I}}
, for every integer
n\geq 1
, we define
nI:=(-na_{I},na_{I})\in{\mathcal{I}}
;
then
a_{nI}=na_{I}
.
For every
I\in{\mathcal{I}}
, let
{\overline{I}}:=[-a_{I},a_{I}]
be the closure in
{\mathbb{R}}
of
I
.
For every
I,J\in{\mathcal{I}}
, let
I+J:=(-a_{I}-a_{J},a_{I}+a_{J})\in{\mathcal{I}}
;
then
a_{I+J}=a_{I}+a_{J}
.
We define
I_{0}:=(-1,1)\in{\mathcal{I}}
; then
a_{I_{0}}=1
.
For all
I,J\in{\mathcal{I}}
, we have:

[\,(I\subseteq J)\,\,\Leftrightarrow\,\,(a_{I}\leq a_{J})\,]

and
[\,(\,{\overline{I}}\subseteq J)\,\,\Leftrightarrow\,\,(a_{I}<a_{J})\,]
.

For this entire note, fix a
C^{\infty}
function
\zeta:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that

•

\zeta=1
on
(-\infty,0]
,
\zeta=0
on
[1,\infty)
and

•

\zeta^{\prime}<0
on
(0,1)
.

Then
0<\zeta<1
on
(0,1)

and
\zeta
is decreasing on
(0,1)
.
Moreover,
\zeta
is nonincreasing on
{\mathbb{R}}
.
For every
I\in{\mathcal{I}}
, define
\zeta_{I}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

•

\zeta_{I}(x)=\zeta(-x-a_{I})
on
x\leq 0
and

•

\zeta_{I}(x)=\zeta(x-a_{I})
on
x\geq 0
.

Then, for all
I\in{\mathcal{I}}
, we have

•

\zeta_{I}=1
on
{\overline{I}}
,
\zeta_{I}=0
on
{\mathbb{R}}\backslash(I+I_{0})
,

•

0<\zeta_{I}<1
on
(I+I_{0})\backslash{\overline{I}}
,

•

\zeta_{I}^{\prime}>0
on
(-a_{I}-1,-a_{I})
and

•

\zeta_{I}^{\prime}<0
on
(a_{I},a_{I}+1)
.

For all integers
d\geq 1
, let
\hbox{Id}_{d}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be the identity map, defined by
\hbox{Id}_{d}(\sigma)=\sigma
.
For every subset
S\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
, for every integer
d\geq 1
, we define

S^{d}:=S\times S\times\cdots\times S\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.

Let
d\geq 1
be an integer.
A function
V:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
will be said to be
complete

if it is
C^{\infty}
and represents a complete vector field on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
For any complete
V:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, we will use the notation
\Phi_{t}^{V}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}

to denote the time
t
flow of
V
,
defined by the ODE
(d/dt)(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma))=V(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma))

and by the initial value condition
\Phi_{0}^{V}=\hbox{Id}_{d}
.

Let
d\geq 1
be an integer.
Let
V:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be complete.
For any
A\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
, for any
B\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, let
\Phi_{A}^{V}(B):=\{\Phi_{a}^{V}(b)\,|\,a\in A,b\in B\}
.
For any
A\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
, for any
b\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, let
\Phi_{A}^{V}(b):=\{\Phi_{a}^{V}(b)\,|\,a\in A\}
.
For any
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for any
B\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, let
\Phi_{a}^{V}(B):=\{\Phi_{a}^{V}(b)\,|\,b\in B\}
.

Let
d\geq 1
be an integer.
Let
V:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be complete.
Let
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
We say that
(V,\sigma)
is
periodic
if there exists an integer
n\neq 0

such that
\Phi_{n}^{V}(\sigma)=\sigma
.
For any integer
k\geq 0
,
we say that
(V,\sigma)
is
periodic to order
k
if there is an integer
n\neq 0

such that the map
\Phi_{n}^{V}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
agrees with the identity
\hbox{Id}_{d}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
to order
k
at
\sigma
.
We say that
(V,\sigma)
is
periodic to all orders
if there is an integer
n\neq 0

such that the map
\Phi_{n}^{V}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
agrees with
\hbox{Id}_{d}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
to all orders at
\sigma
.

Let
d\geq 1
be an integer.
Let
V:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be complete.
Let
S\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
We say
S
is
V
-invariant
if,
for all
\sigma\in S
,
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)\subseteq S
.
We say
S
is
locally
V
-invariant
if,
for all
\sigma\in S
, there is an open neighborhood
N
in
{\mathbb{R}}
of
0

such that
\Phi_{N}^{V}(\sigma)\subseteq S
.

For all integers
j\in[1,4]
, let
\Pi_{j}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be projection onto the
j
th coordinate,
defined by
\Pi_{j}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})=x_{j}
.
Let
{\mathcal{C}}
be the set of
C^{\infty}
maps
V:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}

such that
V({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\subseteq{\overline{I}}_{0}^{4}
.
Note that, for all
V\in{\mathcal{C}}
, the function
V:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
is complete.
We define
V_{0}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by the rule: for all
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,

V_{0}(\sigma)=(0,0,0,1)
.
Then
V_{0}\in{\mathcal{C}}
.
For all
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we define

{\mathcal{V}}(a):=\{V\in{\mathcal{C}}\,|\,V=V_{0}\hbox{ on }{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-\infty,-a)\}
.

For all
I\in{\mathcal{I}}
, we define

\displaystyle T(I)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\overline{I}}^{3}\times\{a_{I}\}\quad\subseteq\quad{\overline{I}}^{4},

\displaystyle B(I)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\overline{I}}^{3}\times\{-a_{I}\}\quad\subseteq\quad{\overline{I}}^{4},

\displaystyle T_{\circ}(I)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle I^{3}\times\{a_{I}\}\quad\subseteq\quad I^{3}\times{\overline{I}},

\displaystyle B_{\circ}(I)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle I^{3}\times\{-a_{I}\}\quad\subseteq\quad I^{3}\times{\overline{I}}\qquad\hbox{and}

\displaystyle\xi_{I}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle(0,0,0,-a_{I})\quad\in\quad B_{\circ}(I).

The
straight up map for
I
is the bijection
SU_{I}:B(I)\to T(I)

defined by
SU_{I}(w,x,y,-a_{I})=(w,x,y,a_{I})
.
Note that
SU_{I}(B_{\circ}(I))=T_{\circ}(I)
.

Let
V\in{\mathcal{C}}
.
We say
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
is
undeterred by
V
if

\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))={\mathbb{R}}
.
The
undeterred set for
V
is
the set
{\mathcal{U}}(V)
of all
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
such that
\sigma
is undeterred by
V
.
This set
{\mathcal{U}}(V)
is
V
-invariant.
We say
V
is
porous
if
{\mathcal{U}}(V)
is dense in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
For example,
{\mathcal{U}}(V_{0})={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, so
V_{0}
is porous.
By a
deterrence system
we mean an element
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{C}}\times{\mathcal{I}}

such that
V=V_{0}
on
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
.
Let
{\mathcal{D}}
be the set of all deterrence systems.
Note, for all
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
, that
V\in{\mathcal{V}}(a_{I})
.
For all
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
, we define

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{\circ}(V,I)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle({\mathcal{U}}(V))\cap(I^{4}),

\displaystyle{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}(V,I)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle({\mathcal{U}}(V))\cap(\,{\overline{I}}^{4}\,),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V,I)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle({\mathcal{U}}(V))\cap(B(I)),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V,I)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle({\mathcal{U}}(V))\cap(B_{\circ}(I)).

For any
I\in{\mathcal{I}}
, we define
{\mathcal{C}}_{I}:=\{P\in{\mathcal{C}}\,|\,P=V_{0}\hbox{ on }(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}\}
.
For any
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
, for any
P\in{\mathcal{C}}_{I}
,
we will denote by
{\mathcal{X}}_{I}(P,V)
the function
X\in{\mathcal{C}}
defined by:

X:=V
on
(2I)^{4}
and
X:=P
on
{\mathbb{R}}\backslash(\,{\overline{I}}^{4}\,)
.

The “
{\mathcal{X}}
” stands for “exchange”:
As
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
, we know that
V=V_{0}
on
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
.
As
P\in{\mathcal{C}}_{I}
, we know that
P=V_{0}
on
(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}
.
To construct
{\mathcal{X}}_{I}(P,V)
,
we start with
P
, and then “exchange”
V_{0}
for
V
on
I^{4}
.

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
For any
(W,J)\in{\mathcal{D}}
, we say

(W,J)
is a
modification of
(V,I)
if:   both   [
a_{I}<a_{J}
]   and   [
W=V
on
{\overline{I}}^{4}
].

We define

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}(V,I)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{\,(W,J)\in{\mathcal{D}}\,\,\,|\,\,(W,J)\hbox{ is a modification of }(V,I)\,\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{*}(V,I)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{\,(W,J)\in{\mathcal{M}}(V,I)\,\,\,|\,\,W\in{\mathcal{V}}(a_{I})\,\}.

Let
I\in{\mathcal{I}}
.
We define
{\mathcal{D}}_{I}^{\times}:=\{(P,K)\in{\mathcal{D}}\,|\,4I\subseteq K\hbox{ and }P\in{\mathcal{C}}_{I}\}
.
We denote by
{\mathcal{P}}_{I}
the set of
(P,K)\in{\mathcal{D}}_{I}^{\times}
such that,
for some integer
m>2a_{I}
, we have:

•

\Phi_{m}^{P}
agrees with
\hbox{Id}_{4}
to all orders at
\xi_{I}
and

•

for all
\tau\in B_{\circ}(I)
,   for all
t\in(0,m)
,

[\,\Phi_{t}^{P}(\tau)\in I^{4}\,]\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad[\,t<2a_{I}\,].

For any
C^{\infty}
manifold
M
, for any integer
k\geq 0
,
let
\pi_{k}^{M}:F_{k}M\to M
denote the
k
th order frame bundle of
M
.

An action of a group
G
on a topological space
X
is

fixpoint rare
if, for any nonempty open subset
U
of
X
,
for all
g\in G\backslash\{1_{G}\}
, there exists
u\in U
such that
gu\neq u
.
Any fixpoint rare action is effective.
For a
C^{\omega}
action on a connected manifold, fixpoint rare and effective are equivalent.
Any continuous transitive action of a real Lie group preserves a
C^{\omega}
structure,
from which it follows that:
By Lemma 6.1 of
[
Ad3
]
,
a continuous action of a connected real Lie group
G
on a topological space
X
is fixpoint rare iff,
for every nonempty
G
-invariant open subset
V
of
X
,
the
G
-action on
V
is effective.

3.
Miscellaneous results

LEMMA 3.1
.

Let
f:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be differentiable.
Assume, for all
t\geq 0
, that
f^{\prime}(t)\geq 0
.
Let
K
be be a compact subset of
{\mathbb{R}}
.
Assume that
f([0,\infty))\subseteq K
.
Let
a\geq 0
and
b>0
.
Then there exists
r\geq 0
such that
both
f^{\prime}(r)<b
and
f^{\prime}(r+a)<b
.

Proof.

Suppose, for a contradiction, that, for all
t\geq 0
, we have:

(*)

either
f^{\prime}(t)\geq b
or
f^{\prime}(t+a)\geq b
.

Define
g:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
g(t)=f(t+a)
.
Since
f^{\prime}\geq 0
on
[0,\infty)
, by the Mean Value Theorem,

f
is nondecreasing on
[0,\infty)
.
Then, because
a\geq 0
, we have
f\leq g
on
[0,\infty)
.
Let
h:=f+g
.
Then
h\leq 2g
on
[0,\infty)
.
For all
t\geq 0
, since
f^{\prime}(t)\geq 0
, since
f^{\prime}(t+a)\geq 0

and since
(*)
holds,
it follows that
(f^{\prime}(t))+(f^{\prime}(t+a))\geq b
,

i.e.
, that
h^{\prime}(t)\geq b
.

Let
c:=h(0)
.
Then, by the Mean Value Theorem,
for all
t>0
, we have
[(h(t))-c]/t\geq b
.
Then, for all
t\geq 0
,
h(t)\geq c+bt
.
Then, since
b>0
, we get
\displaystyle{\lim_{t\to\infty}[h(t)]=\infty}
.
So, because
h\leq 2g
on
[0,\infty)
,
it follows that
\displaystyle{\lim_{t\to\infty}[g(t)]=\infty}
.
Then
\displaystyle{\lim_{t\to\infty}[f(t)]=\lim_{t\to\infty}[g(t-a)]=\infty}
.
However,
K
is compact and
f([0,\infty))\subseteq K
, contradiction.
∎

LEMMA 3.2
.

Let
f:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be continuous.
Let
a,c,s,u\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Assume that
a<c
and that
s<u
.
Assume that
f^{-1}(a)=\{s\}
and that
f^{-1}(c)=\{u\}
.
Assume that
\displaystyle{\lim_{t\to-\infty}[f(t)]=-\infty}

and that
\displaystyle{\lim_{t\to\infty}[f(t)]=\infty}
.
Then
f^{-1}((a,c))=(s,u)
.

Proof.

Since
[f^{-1}(a)]\cap(s,u)=\emptyset=[f^{-1}(c)]\cap(s,u)
,
it follows that
a,c\notin f((s,u))
.
Because
f(s)=a<c
and
c\notin f((s,u))
, it follows,
from the Intermediate Value Theorem,
that
f([s,u))\subseteq(-\infty,c)
.
Because
f(u)=c>a
and
a\notin f((s,u))
, it follows,
from the Intermediate Value Theorem,
that
f((s,u])\subseteq(a,\infty)
.
Then

f((s,u))\qquad\subseteq\qquad(-\infty,c)\,\cap\,(a,\infty)\qquad=\qquad(a,c).

Then
(s,u)\subseteq f^{-1}((a,c))
.
It remains to show that
f^{-1}((a,c))\subseteq(s,u)
.
Let
t_{0}\in f^{-1}((a,c))
.
We wish to prove
s<t_{0}<u
.
We will show
s<t_{0}
; the proof of
t_{0}<u
is similar.

Since
\displaystyle{\lim_{t\to-\infty}[f(t)]=-\infty}
,
choose
p<t_{0}
such that
f(p)<a
.
Since
t_{0}\in f^{-1}((a,c))
,
it follows that
f(t_{0})>a
.
By the Intermediate Value Theorem,
choose
q\in(p,t_{0})
such that
f(q)=a
.
Then
q\in f^{-1}(a)=\{s\}
, so
q=s
.
Then
s=q<t_{0}
.
∎

4.
Results about
V_{0}

Recall, from §
2
, the definition of
V_{0}
.
For all
t,w,x,y,z\in{\mathbb{R}}
, if
\rho:=(w,x,y,z)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, then

\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}(\rho)=(w,x,y,z+t)
.

LEMMA 4.1
.

Let
S\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
.
Then all of the following are true:

(i)

\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}(S\times{\mathbb{R}})=S\times{\mathbb{R}}
.

(ii)

\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\forall a\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}(S\times[a,\infty))=S\times[a+t,\infty)
.

(iii)

\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\forall a\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}(S\times(-\infty,a])=S\times(-\infty,a+t]
.

Proof.

Straightforward.
∎

LEMMA 4.2
.

Let
I\in{\mathcal{I}}
.
Then

(i)

\Phi_{(0,2a_{I})}^{V_{0}}(B_{\circ}(I))=I^{4}
,

(ii)

\Phi_{(-2a_{I},0)}^{V_{0}}(T_{\circ}(I))=I^{4}
,

(iii)

\Phi_{[0,2a_{I}]}^{V_{0}}(T_{\circ}(I))\subseteq[(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}]\backslash[I^{4}]
,

(iv)

\Phi_{[-2a_{I},0]}^{V_{0}}(B_{\circ}(I))\subseteq[(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}]\backslash[I^{4}]
,

(v)

\Phi_{[-2a_{I},4a_{I}]}^{V_{0}}(B_{\circ}(I))\subseteq(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}
and

(vi)

\Phi_{[-2a_{I},2a_{I}]}^{V_{0}}\,(\,[(\,{\overline{I}}\,)^{3}\backslash(I^{3})]\,\times\,{\overline{I}}\,)\,\subseteq[(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}]\backslash[I^{4}]
.

Proof.

Proof of (i):

Since
B_{\circ}(I)=I^{3}\times\{-a_{I}\}
, it follows that

\Phi_{(0,2a_{I})}^{V_{0}}(B_{\circ}(I))\qquad=\qquad I^{3}\,\,\times\,\,(\,-a_{I}+0\,,\,-a_{I}+2a_{I}\,),

so
\Phi_{(0,2a_{I})}^{V_{0}}(B_{\circ}(I))=I^{3}\times(-a_{I},a_{I})=I^{3}\times I=I^{4}
.

End of proof of (i).

The proofs of (ii)-(vi) are similarly straightfoward.
∎

LEMMA 4.3
.

Let
I\in{\mathcal{I}}
.
Let
\rho_{1}\in T_{\circ}(I)
.
Let
\rho^{\prime}:=\Phi_{-2a_{I}}^{V_{0}}(\rho_{1})
.
Then
\rho^{\prime}\in B_{\circ}(I)
and
\rho_{1}=SU_{I}(\rho^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Straightforward.
∎

LEMMA 4.4
.

Let
I,J\in{\mathcal{I}}
.
Assume
J\subseteq I
.
Let
s:=a_{I}-a_{J}
.
Give
B_{\circ}(I)
and
B_{\circ}(J)
their relative topologies, inherited from
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Then

(i)

\Phi_{s}^{V_{0}}(\xi_{I})=\xi_{J}
,
\Phi_{-s}^{V_{0}}(\xi_{J})=\xi_{I}
,

(ii)

\Phi_{[0,s]}^{V_{0}}(\xi_{I})\,\,=\,\,\Phi_{[-s,0]}^{V_{0}}(\xi_{J})\,\,\subseteq\,\,\{(0,0,0)\}\,\times\,[-a_{I},-a_{J}]
and

(iii)

\tau\mapsto\Phi_{-s}^{V_{0}}(\tau):B_{\circ}(J)\to B_{\circ}(I)
is an open map.

Proof.

Straightforward.
∎

LEMMA 4.5
.

Let
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Let
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}(\sigma))=(\Pi_{4}(\sigma))+t
.

Proof.

Choose
w,x,y,z\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\sigma=(w,x,y,z)
.
Then
\Pi_{4}(\sigma)=z
.
Then
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}(\sigma))=\Pi_{4}((w,x,y,z+t))=z+t=(\Pi_{4}(\sigma))+t
.
∎

LEMMA 4.6
.

Let
I\in{\mathcal{I}}
and let
\rho\in({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
.
Then

(i)

\Pi_{4}(\rho)\geq 0\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\Phi_{[0,\infty)}^{V_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
and

(ii)

\Pi_{4}(\rho)\leq 0\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\Phi_{(-\infty,0]}^{V_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
.

Proof.

Fix
w,x,y,z\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\rho=(w,x,y,z)
.
If
(w,x,y)\notin I^{3}
, then
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\rho)=\{(w,x,y)\}\times{\mathbb{R}}\subseteq({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
;
in this case,

\hbox{both}\quad\Phi_{(-\infty,0]}^{V_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})\quad\hbox{and}\quad\Phi_{[0,\infty)}^{V_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})

are true, and we are done.
We therefore assume that
(w,x,y)\in I^{3}
.
Then, as
(w,x,y,z)=\rho\notin I^{4}
, we get
z\notin I
.
That is,
z\notin(-a_{I},a_{I})
.

If
\Pi_{4}(\rho)\geq 0
,
i.e.
, if
z\geq 0
, then, because
z\notin(-a_{I},a_{I})
, we get
z\geq a_{I}
,
so
\Phi_{[0,\infty)}^{V_{0}}(\rho)=\{(w,x,y)\}\,\times\,[z,\infty)\subseteq({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
,
proving (i).

If
\Pi_{4}(\rho)\leq 0
,
i.e.
, if
z\leq 0
, then, as
z\notin(-a_{I},a_{I})
, we get
z\leq-a_{I}
,
so
\Phi_{(-\infty,0]}^{V_{0}}(\rho)=\{(w,x,y)\}\,\times\,(-\infty,z]\subseteq({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
,
proving (ii).
∎

LEMMA 4.7
.

Let
I
and
J
be open subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let
c\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let
t_{0}\in J
.
Give
I^{3}\times\{c\}
the relative topology inherited from
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Let
N
be an open subset of
I^{3}\times\{c\}
.
Then
\Phi_{J}^{V_{0}}(N)
is an open subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.

Proof.

Fix an open subset
U
of
I^{3}
such that
N=U\times\{c\}
.
Then
\Phi_{J}^{V_{0}}(N)=U\times(c+J)
.
Since
U
is open in
I^{3}
,
U
is open in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
.
Then, since
c+J
is open in
{\mathbb{R}}
,
we see that
\Phi_{J}^{V_{0}}(N)
is open in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
∎

5.
Coincidence of orbits

LEMMA 5.1
.

Let
d\geq 1
be an integer.
Let
V,W:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be complete.
Assume, for all
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, that there exists
c\in{\mathbb{R}}

such that
W(\sigma)=c\cdot(V(\sigma))
.
Let
\sigma_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Then there exists
\delta>0
such that

\Phi_{(-\delta,\delta)}^{W}(\sigma_{0})\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma_{0})
.

Proof.

Define
\beta:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
by
\beta(t)=\Phi_{t}^{W}(\sigma_{0})
.
We wish to prove that there exists
\delta>0
such that,
for all
t\in(-\delta,\delta)
, we have

\beta(t)\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma_{0})
.

Let
{\bf 0}:=(0,\ldots,0)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
If
W(\sigma_{0})={\bf 0}
, then,
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\beta(t)=\Phi_{t}^{W}(\sigma_{0})=\sigma_{0}\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma_{0})
,
so, for any choice of
\delta>0
, we are done.
We therefore assume
W(\sigma_{0})\neq{\bf 0}
.
Fix
c_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
W(\sigma_{0})=c_{0}\cdot(V(\sigma_{0}))
.
Then
c_{0}\cdot(V(\sigma_{0}))\neq{\bf 0}
, so
V(\sigma_{0})\neq{\bf 0}
.

Let
M:=({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\backslash(V^{-1}({\bf 0}))
.
Since
V(\sigma_{0})\neq{\bf 0}
,
we see that
\sigma_{0}\in M
.
Since
V^{-1}({\bf 0})
is
V
-invariant,
it follows that
M
is
V
-invariant as well.
Then
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma_{0})\subseteq M
.
Define
f:M\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

\hbox{for all }\sigma\in M,\qquad\qquad W(\sigma)\,\,=\,\,[f(\sigma)]\,[V(\sigma)].

Then
f:M\to{\mathbb{R}}
is
C^{\infty}
.
Define
G:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
G(q)=f(\Phi_{q}^{V}(\sigma_{0}))
.
Then
G:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is also
C^{\infty}
.
By local existence of solutions of ODEs,
fix
\delta>0
and
s:(-\delta,\delta)\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that

s(0)\,=\,0\quad\qquad\hbox{and}\quad\qquad\forall t\in(-\delta,\delta),\quad s^{\prime}(t)\,=\,G(s(t)).

Let
I:=(-\delta,\delta)\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
.
We wish to show, for all
t\in I
, that
\beta(t)\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma_{0})
.

Define
\alpha:I\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by
\alpha(t)=\Phi_{s(t)}^{V}(\sigma_{0})
.
For all
t\in I
, we have
\alpha(t)\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma_{0})
.
So it suffices to show that, for all
t\in I
,
\beta(t)=\alpha(t)
.
We have
\beta(0)=\Phi_{0}^{W}(\sigma_{0})=\sigma_{0}=\Phi_{0}^{V}(\sigma_{0})=\alpha(0)
.
Also, for all
t\in I
, we have
\beta^{\prime}(t)=W(\beta(t))
.
Then, by uniqueness of solutions of ODEs,
it suffices to show, for all
t\in I
, that
\alpha^{\prime}(t)=W(\alpha(t))
.
Fix
t_{0}\in I
.
We wish to prove that
\alpha^{\prime}(t_{0})=W(\alpha(t_{0}))
.

Let
q_{0}:=s(t_{0})
.
Then
\alpha(t_{0})=\Phi_{q_{0}}^{V}(\sigma_{0})
.
Then
\alpha(t_{0})\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma_{0})\subseteq M
.
Then
W(\alpha(t_{0}))=[f(\alpha(t_{0}))][V(\alpha(t_{0}))]
.
By the Chain Rule, we have

\alpha^{\prime}(t_{0})\quad=\quad[\,\,s^{\prime}(t_{0})\,\,]\,\,[\,\,(d/dq)_{q=q_{0}}\,(\,\Phi_{q}^{V}(\sigma_{0})\,)\,\,].

We have
s^{\prime}(t_{0})=G(s(t_{0}))=G(q_{0})=f(\Phi_{q_{0}}^{V}(\sigma_{0}))=f(\alpha(t_{0}))
.
Also,

(d/dq)_{q=q_{0}}\,(\,\Phi_{q}^{V}(\sigma_{0})\,)\quad=\quad V(\Phi_{q_{0}}^{V}(\sigma_{0}))\quad=\quad V(\alpha(t_{0})).

Then
\alpha^{\prime}(t_{0})=[f(\alpha(t_{0}))][V(\alpha(t_{0}))]=W(\alpha(t_{0}))
, as desired.
∎

LEMMA 5.2
.

Let
d\geq 1
be an integer.
Let
V,W:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be complete.
Assume, for all
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, that there exists
c\in{\mathbb{R}}

such that
W(\sigma)=c\cdot(V(\sigma))
.
Then, for all
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, we have
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{W}(\sigma)\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)
.

Proof.

Fix
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}
. We will show that
\Phi_{[0,\infty)}^{W}(\sigma)\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)
;
the proof that
\Phi_{(-\infty,0]}^{W}(\sigma)\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)
is similar.
Let
S:=\{t\in[0,\infty)\,|\,\Phi_{t}^{W}(\sigma)\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)\}
.
Assume, for a contradiction, that
S\subsetneq[0,\infty)
.

Because
\Phi_{0}^{W}(\sigma)=\sigma=\Phi_{0}^{V}(\sigma)\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)
, we see that
0\in S
.

Let
t_{0}:=\inf[0,\infty)\backslash S
.
Let
\sigma_{0}:=\Phi_{t_{0}}^{W}(\sigma)
.
By Lemma
5.1
,
fix
\delta>0
such that

\Phi_{(-\delta,\delta)}^{W}(\sigma_{0})\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma_{0})
.
Because
t_{0}=\inf[0,\infty)\backslash S
, we have

t_{0}\geq 0,\qquad[0,t_{0})\,\,\,\subseteq\,\,\,S\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad[t_{0},t_{0}+\delta)\,\,\,\not\subseteq\,\,\,S.

Let
b:=\min\{\delta/2,t_{0}\}
.
Then
b\in[0,\delta)
, so
-b\in(-\delta,0]\subseteq(-\delta,\delta)
.

Either
t_{0}=0
or
t_{0}>0
.
If
t_{0}=0
, then
b=0
, in which case
t_{0}-b=0\in S
.
If
t_{0}>0
, then
0<b\leq t_{0}
, so
t_{0}-b\in[0,t_{0})\subseteq S
.
In either case,
t_{0}-b\in S
.
That is,
\Phi_{t_{0}-b}^{W}(\sigma)\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)
.
Also,

\Phi_{t_{0}-b}^{W}(\sigma)\quad=\quad\Phi_{-b}^{W}(\sigma_{0})\quad\in\quad\Phi_{(-\delta,\delta)}^{W}(\sigma_{0})\quad\subseteq\quad\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma_{0}).

Then
\Phi_{t_{0}-b}^{W}(\sigma)\in(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))\cap(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma_{0}))
,
so
\emptyset\neq(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))\cap(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma_{0}))
,
so
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma_{0})
.
Then

\Phi_{[t_{0},t_{0}+\delta)}^{W}(\sigma)\,\,=\,\,\Phi_{[0,\delta)}^{W}(\sigma_{0})\,\,\subseteq\,\,\Phi_{(-\delta,\delta)}^{W}(\sigma_{0})\,\,\subseteq\,\,\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma_{0})\,\,=\,\,\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma),

so
[t_{0},t_{0}+\delta)\subseteq S
, contradiction.
∎

COROLLARY 5.3
.

Let
d\geq 1
be an integer.
Let
V,W:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be complete.
Assume, for all
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, that there exists
c\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}

such that
W(\sigma)=c\cdot(V(\sigma))
.
Then, for all
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, we have
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{W}(\sigma)=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.2
, for all
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,
we have
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{W}(\sigma)\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)
;
we need to show that
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{W}(\sigma)
.

For all
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, there exists
c\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}
such that
V(\sigma)=(1/c)\cdot(W(\sigma))
.
So, interchanging
V
and
W
and replacing
c
with
1/c
, Lemma
5.2
shows,
for all
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, that
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{W}(\sigma)
,
as desired.
∎

6.
Coincidence of flows

LEMMA 6.1
.

Let
N
be an interval in
{\mathbb{R}}
.
Assume that
0\in N
.
Let
d\geq 1
be an integer.
Let
V,W:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be complete.
Let
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Assume, for all
t\in N
, that
W(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma))=V(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma))
.
Then, for all
t\in N
, we have
\Phi_{t}^{W}(\sigma)=\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma)
.

Proof.

Define
a,b:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
by

a(t)=\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma)
and
b(t)=\Phi_{t}^{W}(\sigma)
.
We wish to show, for all
t\in N
, that
b(t)=a(t)
.

We have
a(0)=\sigma=b(0)
.
Also, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
b^{\prime}(t)=W(b(t))
.
So, by uniqueness of solutions of ODEs,
it suffices to show, for all
t\in N
, that
a^{\prime}(t)=W(a(t))
.

For all
t\in N
, we have

W(a(t))=W(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma))=V(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma))=V(a(t))
;
then
a^{\prime}(t)=V(a(t))=W(a(t))
, as desired.
∎

COROLLARY 6.2
.

Let
N
be an interval in
{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let
{\overline{N}}
denote the closure in
{\mathbb{R}}
of
N
.
Assume that
0\in{\overline{N}}
.
Let
d\geq 1
be an integer and let
A,B\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Let
V,W:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be complete.
Assume
W=V
on
B
.
Assume
\Phi_{N}^{V}(A)\subseteq B
.
Let
{\overline{A}}
denote the closure in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
of
A
.
Then, for all
t\in{\overline{N}}
, we have
\Phi_{t}^{W}=\Phi_{t}^{V}
on
{\overline{A}}
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma\in{\overline{A}}
.
We wish to show, for all
t\in{\overline{N}}
, that

\Phi_{t}^{W}(\sigma)=\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma)
.

Let
{\overline{B}}
be the closure in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
of
B
.
By assumption,
\Phi_{N}^{V}(A)\subseteq B
,
so, by continuity,
\Phi_{\overline{N}}^{V}(\,{\overline{A}}\,)\subseteq{\overline{B}}
.
Also, since
W=V
on
B
, continuity yields

W=V
on
{\overline{B}}
.
For all
t\in{\overline{N}}
, we have
\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma)\in\Phi_{{\overline{N}}}^{V}(\,{\overline{A}}\,)\subseteq{\overline{B}}
.
Then, for all
t\in{\overline{N}}
, we have
W(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma))=V(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma))
.

Then, by Lemma
6.1
,
for all
t\in{\overline{N}}
, we have
\Phi_{t}^{W}(\sigma)=\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma)
.
∎

Let
d\geq 1
be an integer and let
M:={\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Let
k\geq 0
be an integer.
Let
\pi:=\pi_{k}^{M}:F_{k}M\to M
be the
k
th order frame bundle of
M
.
Any diffeomorphism
g:M\to M
induces a bundle diffeomorphism
F_{k}g:F_{k}M\to F_{k}M
.
A complete
X:M\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
therefore induces, for all
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
a bundle diffeomorphism
F_{k}\Phi_{s}^{X}:F_{k}M\to F_{k}M
.
For any two diffeomorphisms
g,h:M\to M
, for any
\lambda\in F_{k}M
, we have:

[
g
and
h
agree to order
k
at
\pi(\lambda)
]   iff   [
(F_{k}g)(\lambda)=(F_{k}h)(\lambda)
].

The tangent bundle of
F_{k}M
is denoted
TF_{k}M
.
The bundle of
k
-jets of germs of vector fields on
M
is denoted
J_{k}TM
.
Any
C^{\infty}
function
X:M\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
represents a vector field
M\to TM
,
which “prolongs” to a section
J_{k}X:M\to J_{k}TM
.
If
X:M\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is complete and if
\mu\in F_{k}M
,
then someone who knows both
\mu
and

the
k
-jet of the germ of
X
at
\pi(\mu)

has enough information to compute

the tangent vector at
0
of the curve
s\mapsto(F_{k}\Phi_{s}^{X})(\mu):{\mathbb{R}}\to F_{k}M
.

More succinctly, there is a bundle map

\Psi_{d}^{k}\qquad:\qquad F_{k}M\,\,\,\times_{M}\,\,\,J_{k}TM\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad TF_{k}M

such that, for any complete
X:M\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, for any
\mu\in F_{k}M
,

\Psi_{d}^{k}\,\,(\,\,\mu\,,\,(J_{k}X)(\pi(\mu))\,\,)\quad=\quad(d/ds)_{s=0}\,\,[\,\,(F_{k}\Phi_{s}^{X})(\mu)\,\,].

LEMMA 6.3
.

Let
N
be an interval in
{\mathbb{R}}
.
Assume that
0\in N
.
Let
d\geq 1
be an integer.
Let
V,W:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be complete.
Let
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Assume, for all
t\in N
, that
W
agrees with
V
to all orders at
\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma)
.
Then, for all
t\in N
,
\Phi_{t}^{W}
agrees with
\Phi_{t}^{V}
to all orders at
\sigma
.

Proof.

Fix an integer
k\geq 0
.
We wish to show, for all
t\in N
,
that
\Phi_{t}^{W}
and
\Phi_{t}^{V}
agree at
\sigma
to order
k
.

Let
M:={\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Let
\pi:=\pi_{k}^{M}:F_{k}M\to M
be the
k
th order frame bundle of
M
.
Fix
\sigma^{*}\in\pi^{-1}(\sigma)
.
We wish to show, for all
t\in N
, that

(F_{k}\Phi_{t}^{W})(\sigma^{*})=(F_{k}\Phi_{t}^{V})(\sigma^{*})
.
For all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, let

\alpha_{t}:=F_{k}\Phi_{t}^{V}:F_{k}M\to F_{k}M\quad\hbox{and}\quad\beta_{t}:=F_{k}\Phi_{t}^{W}:F_{k}M\to F_{k}M.

We wish to show, for all
t\in N
, that
\beta_{t}(\sigma^{*})=\alpha_{t}(\sigma^{*})
.

Let
a,b:{\mathbb{R}}\to F_{k}M
be defined by

a(t)=\alpha_{t}(\sigma^{*})\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad b(t)=\beta_{t}(\sigma^{*}).

We wish to show, for all
t\in N
, that
b(t)=a(t)
.

Let
V^{*},W^{*}:F_{k}M\to TF_{k}M
be the vector fields on
F_{k}M
defined by

V^{*}(\rho)\,\,=\,\,(d/ds)_{s=0}\,[\,\alpha_{s}(\rho)\,],\qquad W^{*}(\rho)\,\,=\,\,(d/ds)_{s=0}\,[\,\beta_{s}(\rho)\,].

Then, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\displaystyle W^{*}(b(t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(d/ds)_{s=0}[\beta_{s}(b(t))]\,=\,(d/ds)_{s=0}[\beta_{s}(\beta_{t}(\sigma^{*}))]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(d/ds)_{s=0}[\beta_{t+s}(\sigma^{*})]\,=\,(d/ds)_{s=0}[b(t+s)]=b^{\prime}(t).

Also,
a(0)=\alpha_{0}(\sigma^{*})=\sigma^{*}=\beta_{0}(\sigma^{*})=b(0)
.
So, by uniqueness of solutions of ODEs,
we wish to prove: for all
t\in N
,
a^{\prime}(t)=W^{*}(a(t))
.
Fix
t_{0}\in N
and let
\mu:=a(t_{0})
.
We wish to prove
a^{\prime}(t_{0})=W^{*}(\mu)
.

We have
\mu=a(t_{0})=\alpha_{t_{0}}(\sigma^{*})
and
\alpha_{t_{0}}=F_{k}\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}
and
\pi(\sigma^{*})=\sigma
, so

\pi(\mu)\,=\,\pi(\alpha_{t_{0}}(\sigma^{*}))\,=\,\pi((F_{k}\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V})(\sigma^{*}))\,=\,\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\pi(\sigma^{*}))\,=\,\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\sigma).

Then, by assumption,
W
and
V
agree at
\pi(\mu)
to all orders and, in particular, to order
k
.
Then
(J_{k}V)(\pi(\mu))=(J_{k}W)(\pi(\mu))
.
Also, by definition of
\Psi_{d}^{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\Psi_{d}^{k}\,\,(\,\,\mu\,,\,(J_{k}V)(\pi(\mu))\,\,)\,\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\,(d/ds)_{s=0}\,\,[\,\,(F_{k}\Phi_{s}^{V})(\mu)\,\,]\qquad\hbox{and}

\displaystyle\Psi_{d}^{k}\,\,(\,\,\mu\,,\,(J_{k}W)(\pi(\mu))\,\,)\,\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\,(d/ds)_{s=0}\,\,[\,\,(F_{k}\Phi_{s}^{W})(\mu)\,\,].

Then

(d/ds)_{s=0}[(F_{k}\Phi_{s}^{V})(\mu)]=(d/ds)_{s=0}[(F_{k}\Phi_{s}^{W})(\mu)]
.
For all
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
F_{k}\Phi_{s}^{V}=\alpha_{s}
and
F_{k}\Phi_{s}^{W}=\beta_{s}
.
Then
(d/ds)_{s=0}[\alpha_{s}(\mu)]=(d/ds)_{s=0}[\beta_{s}(\mu)]
.

For all
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\alpha_{s}(\mu)=\alpha_{s}(\alpha_{t_{0}}(\sigma^{*}))=\alpha_{t_{0}+s}(\sigma^{*})=a(t_{0}+s)
.
Therefore

(d/ds)_{s=0}[\alpha_{s}(\mu)]=a^{\prime}(t_{0})
.
By definition of
W^{*}
, we have

W^{*}(\mu)=(d/ds)_{s=0}[\beta_{s}(\mu)]
.

Then
a^{\prime}(t_{0})=(d/ds)_{s=0}[\alpha_{s}(\mu)]=(d/ds)_{s=0}[\beta_{s}(\mu)]=W^{*}(\mu)
.
∎

COROLLARY 6.4
.

Let
d\geq 1
be an integer.
Let
{\bf 0}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be defined by:
for all
\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,
{\bf 0}(\tau)=(0,\ldots,0)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Let
V:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be complete.
Let
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Assume that
V
agrees with
{\bf 0}
to all orders at
\sigma
.
Then, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\Phi_{t}^{V}
agrees with
\hbox{Id}_{d}
to all orders at
\sigma
.

7.
Results about invariance and local invariance

Recall, from §
2
, the definitions of

V
-invariant
and
locally
V
-invariant
.

LEMMA 7.1
.

Let
V,W:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
both be complete.
Let
U
be an open subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Assume that
V=W
on
U
.
Let
S\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be
V
-invariant.
Then
S\cap U
is locally
W
-invariant.

Proof.

Fix
\sigma\in S\cap U
.
We wish to show that there is an open neighborhood
N
in
{\mathbb{R}}
of
0

such that
\Phi_{N}^{W}(\sigma)\subseteq S\cap U
.

Define
f:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
by
f(r)=\Phi_{r}^{W}(\sigma)
.
We have
f(0)=\sigma\in U
, so
0\in f^{-1}(U)
.
By continuity of
f
,
f^{-1}(U)
is open in
{\mathbb{R}}
,
so each connected component of
f^{-1}(U)
is open in
{\mathbb{R}}
as well.
Let
N
be the connected component of
f^{-1}(U)
satisfying
0\in N
.
Then

\Phi_{N}^{W}(\sigma)\quad=\quad f(N)\quad\subseteq\quad f(f^{-1}(U))\quad\subseteq\quad U.

It remains to show that
\Phi_{N}^{W}(\sigma)\subseteq S
.

Since
S
is
V
-invariant, and since
\sigma\in S
,
it follows that
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)\subseteq S
.
Since
W=V
on
U
and since
\Phi_{N}^{W}(\sigma)\subseteq U
,
it follows, from Lemma
6.1
,
that
\Phi_{N}^{V}(\sigma)=\Phi_{N}^{W}(\sigma)
.
Then
\Phi_{N}^{W}(\sigma)=\Phi_{N}^{V}(\sigma)\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)\subseteq S
.
∎

LEMMA 7.2
.

Let
W:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be complete.
Let
Z\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be a locally
W
-invariant, meager subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Then
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{W}(Z)
is meager in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.

Proof.

Since
Z
is meager in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, it follows, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, that
\Phi^{W}_{t}(Z)
is meager as well.
So, since
\displaystyle{\Phi_{\mathbb{Q}}^{W}(Z)=\bigcup_{q\in{\mathbb{Q}}}\,\left(\Phi^{W}_{q}(Z)\right)}
,
the set
\Phi_{\mathbb{Q}}^{W}(Z)
is also meager in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
It therefore suffices to show that
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{W}(Z)=\Phi_{\mathbb{Q}}^{W}(Z)
.

Since
{\mathbb{Q}}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
, it follows that
\Phi_{\mathbb{Q}}^{W}(Z)\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{W}(Z)
,
and it remains to prove that
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{W}(Z)\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{Q}}^{W}(Z)
.
Let
\zeta\in Z
and let
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. We wish to show that
\Phi_{t}^{W}(\zeta)\in\Phi_{\mathbb{Q}}^{W}(Z)
.

As
\zeta\in Z
and
Z
is locally
W
-invariant, fix an open neighborhood
N
in
{\mathbb{R}}
of
0

such that
\Phi_{N}^{W}(\zeta)\subseteq Z
.
By density of
{\mathbb{Q}}
in
{\mathbb{R}}
, choose
q\in{\mathbb{Q}}\cap(t-N)
.
Choose
r\in N
such that
q=t-r
. Then
t=q+r
.

Then
\Phi_{t}^{W}(\zeta)=\Phi_{q}^{W}(\Phi_{r}^{W}(\zeta))\in\Phi_{\mathbb{Q}}^{W}(\Phi_{N}^{W}(\zeta))\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{Q}}^{W}(Z)
.
∎

8.
Results about deterrence systems

LEMMA 8.1
.

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Then all of the following are true:

(i)

For all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\Phi_{t}^{V}=\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}
on
[({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\backslash(I^{3})]\times{\mathbb{R}}
.

(ii)

For all
t\geq 0
,
\Phi_{t}^{V}=\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times[a_{I},\infty)
.

(iii)

For all
t\leq 0
,
\Phi_{t}^{V}=\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-\infty,-a_{I}]
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.1
(i), for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}\,(\,[({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\backslash(I^{3})]\times{\mathbb{R}}\,)\quad\subseteq\quad[({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\backslash(I^{3})]\,\times\,{\mathbb{R}}.

Also,
V=V_{0}
on
[({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\backslash(I^{3})]\times{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then Lemma
6.1
yields (i).

By Lemma
4.1
(ii), for all
t\geq 0
, we have

\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}\,(\,{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times[a_{I},\infty)\,)\quad\subseteq\quad{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\,\times\,[a_{I},\infty).

Also,
V=V_{0}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times[a_{I},\infty)
.
Then Lemma
6.1
yields (ii).

By Lemma
4.1
(iii), for all
t\leq 0
, we have

\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}\,(\,{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-\infty,-a_{I}]\,)\quad\subseteq\quad{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\,\times\,(-\infty,-a_{I}].

Also,
V=V_{0}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-\infty,-a_{I}]
.
Then Lemma
6.1
yields (iii).
∎

LEMMA 8.2
.

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Then
I^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}
is
V
-invariant.

Proof.

Let
P:=[({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\backslash(I^{3})]\times{\mathbb{R}}
.
By Lemma
4.1
(i),
P
is
V_{0}
-invariant
So, by Lemma
8.1
(i),
P
is
V
-invariant.
Then
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash P
is
V
-invariant.
So, since
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash P=I^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}
, we are done.
∎

LEMMA 8.3
.

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
,
\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
The following are equivalent:

(a)

\rho\in{\mathcal{U}}(V)
,   i.e.,
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\rho))={\mathbb{R}}
.

(b)

(-\infty,-a_{I})\,\cap\,[\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\rho))]\,\,\,\neq\,\,\,\emptyset\,\,\,\neq\,\,\,(a_{I},\infty)\,\cap\,[\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\rho))]
.

(c)

\displaystyle{\lim_{t\to\infty}\,[\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\rho))]=\infty}

and
\displaystyle{\lim_{t\to-\infty}\,[\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\rho))]=-\infty}
.

Proof.

The implication (a
\Rightarrow
b) is immediate.

Proof of (b
\Rightarrow
c):

Let
s_{0},u_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
assume
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{s_{0}}^{V}(\rho))<-a_{I}
and
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{u_{0}}^{V}(\rho))>a_{I}
.
We wish to show

\hbox{that}\quad\lim_{t\to\infty}\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\rho))=\infty\quad\hbox{and that}\quad\lim_{t\to-\infty}\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\rho))=-\infty.

We will prove the former; the latter is similar.
As
\displaystyle{\lim_{t\to\infty}(a_{I}+t-u_{0})=\infty}
,
it suffices to show, for all
t\geq u_{0}
,
that
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\rho))>a_{I}+t-u_{0}
.
So fix
t\geq u_{0}
.
We wish to prove that
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\rho))>a_{I}+t-u_{0}
.

Let
\tau:=\Phi_{u_{0}}^{V}(\rho)
.
Then
\Pi_{4}(\tau)=\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{u_{0}}^{V}(\rho))>a_{I}
, so
\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(a_{I},\infty)
.
Then, as
t-u_{0}\geq 0
, by Lemma
8.1
(ii),
we have
\Phi_{t-u_{0}}^{V}(\tau)=\Phi_{t-u_{0}}^{V_{0}}(\tau)
.

Then
\Phi_{t}^{V}(\rho)=\Phi_{t-u_{0}}^{V}(\Phi_{u_{0}}^{V}(\rho))=\Phi_{t-u_{0}}^{V}(\tau)=\Phi_{t-u_{0}}^{V_{0}}(\tau)
.
By Lemma
4.5
,

\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t-u_{0}}^{V_{0}}(\tau))=(\Pi_{4}(\tau))+t-u_{0}
,
so, because
\Phi_{t}^{V}(\rho)=\Phi_{t-u_{0}}^{V_{0}}(\tau)
and
\Pi_{4}(\tau)>a_{I}
,
we get
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\tau))>a_{I}+t-u_{0}
.

End of proof of (b
\Rightarrow
c).

The Intermediate Value Theorem yields (c
\Rightarrow
a).
∎

COROLLARY 8.4
.

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Give
B(I)
and
B_{\circ}(I)
their relative topologies inherited from
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Then

(i)

{\mathcal{U}}(V)
is open in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,

(ii)

{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V,I)
is open in
B(I)
and

(iii)

{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V,I)
is open in
B_{\circ}(I)
.

Proof.

Since (ii) and (iii) follow from (i), we need only prove (i).

Let
\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}(V)
.
We wish to show that there is an open subset
U
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}

such that
\sigma\in U\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}(V)
.

Since
\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}(V)
, we get
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))={\mathbb{R}}
.
Fix
s_{0},u_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that

\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{s_{0}}^{V}(\sigma))<-a_{I}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{u_{0}}^{V}(\sigma))>a_{I}.

Let

U:=\{\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\,|\,\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{s_{0}}^{V}(\rho))<-a_{I}\hbox{ and }\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{u_{0}}^{V}(\rho))>a_{I}\}
.
By Lemma
8.3
(b
\Rightarrow
a),
we have
U\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}(V)
.
Then
U
is an open subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
and
\sigma\in U\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}(V)
, as desired
∎

COROLLARY 8.5
.

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Then

V
is porous   iff
{\mathcal{U}}(V)
is comeager in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.

Proof.

By definition,
V
is porous iff
{\mathcal{U}}(V)
is dense in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
By the Baire Category Theorem, an open subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
is dense iff it is comeager.
Thus this result follows from Corollary
8.4
(i).
∎

LEMMA 8.6
.

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Let
\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}(V)
.
Define
\psi:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by
\psi(s)=\Phi_{s}^{V}(\sigma)
.
Let
\psi(r),\psi(t)\in I^{4}
.
Say
r\leq t
.
Then
\psi([r,t])\subseteq I^{4}
.

Proof.

Let
s_{0}\in[r,t]

and assume, for a contradiction, that
\psi(s_{0})\notin I^{4}
.

Let
\rho:=\psi(s_{0})=\Phi_{s_{0}}^{V}(\sigma)
.
Then
\rho\notin I^{4}
.
By Lemma
4.6
,
either

(i)

\Phi_{[0,\infty)}^{V_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
or

(ii)

\Phi_{(-\infty,0]}^{V_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
.

Since
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
, we have
V=V_{0}
on
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
.

Assume (i).
Then, by Lemma
6.1
, we have

\Phi_{t-s_{0}}^{V}(\rho)=\Phi_{t-s_{0}}^{V_{0}}(\rho)
.
Then
\psi(t)=\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma)=\Phi_{t-s_{0}}^{V}(\rho)=\Phi_{t-s_{0}}^{V_{0}}(\rho)\in\Phi_{[0,\infty)}^{V_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
.
However,
\psi(t)\in I^{4}
, contradiction.

Assume (ii).
Then, by Lemma
6.1
, we have

\Phi_{r-s_{0}}^{V}(\rho)=\Phi_{r-s_{0}}^{V_{0}}(\rho)
.
Then
\psi(r)=\Phi_{r}^{V}(\sigma)=\Phi_{r-s_{0}}^{V}(\rho)=\Phi_{r-s_{0}}^{V_{0}}(\rho)\in\Phi_{(-\infty,0]}^{V_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
.
However,
\psi(r)\in I^{4}
, contradiction.
∎

LEMMA 8.7
.

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Let
\sigma\in I^{4}
,
s_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
W\in{\mathcal{C}}
.
Assume
\Phi_{s_{0}}^{V}(\sigma)\in I^{4}
.
Assume
W=V
on
I^{4}
.
Then
\Phi_{s_{0}}^{W}(\sigma)=\Phi_{s_{0}}^{V}(\sigma)
.

Proof.

Let
r:=\min\{0,s_{0}\}
,
t:=\max\{0,s_{0}\}
.
Then
\{0,s_{0}\}=\{r,t\}
.

Define
\psi:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by
\psi(s)=\Phi_{s}^{V}(\sigma)
.
Then
\psi(0)=\sigma\in I^{4}

and
\psi(s_{0})=\Phi_{s_{0}}^{V}(\sigma)\in I^{4}
.
Then
\psi(0),\psi(s_{0})\in I^{4}
, so
\psi(r),\psi(t)\in I^{4}
.
So, by Lemma
8.6
,
we have
\psi([r,t])\subseteq I^{4}
,
i.e.
,
we have
\Phi_{[r,t]}^{V}(\sigma)\subseteq I^{4}
.
So, because
s_{0}\in\{0,s_{0}\}=\{r,t\}\subseteq[r,t]
,
and because
W=V
on
I^{4}
, by Lemma
6.1
,
we conclude that
\Phi_{s_{0}}^{W}(\sigma)=\Phi_{s_{0}}^{V}(\sigma)
,
as desired.
∎

LEMMA 8.8
.

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Let
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Let
b\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash I
.
Then there is at most one
t_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\sigma))=b
.

Proof.

Let
t_{0},t^{\prime}_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Assume
t_{0}\leq t^{\prime}_{0}
and

\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\sigma))=b=\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t^{\prime}_{0}}^{V}(\sigma))
.
We wish to show that
t^{\prime}_{0}=t_{0}
.

Since
b\notin I=(-a_{I},a_{I})
,
we know either that
b\leq-a_{I}
or that
b\geq a_{I}
.
We will assume that
b\geq a_{I}
;
the proof in the other case is similar.

Let
\sigma_{0}:=\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\sigma)
.
Then
\Pi_{4}(\sigma_{0})=b
.
Let
t_{1}:=t^{\prime}_{0}-t_{0}
.
Then
\Phi_{t_{1}}^{V}(\sigma_{0})=\Phi_{t^{\prime}_{0}}^{V}(\sigma)
,
so
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{1}}^{V}(\sigma_{0}))=b
.
We have
\Pi_{4}(\sigma_{0})=b\geq a_{I}
,
i.e.
,
\sigma_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times[a_{I},\infty)
.
So, since
t_{1}\geq 0
, by Lemma
8.1
(ii),
we conclude that
\Phi_{t_{1}}^{V}(\sigma_{0})=\Phi_{t_{1}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma_{0})
.
Then
b=\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{1}}^{V}(\sigma_{0}))=\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{1}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma_{0}))
,
so, by Lemma
4.5
,

b=(\Pi_{4}(\sigma_{0}))+t_{1}
.
Then
b=b+t_{1}
, so
t_{1}=0
, so
t^{\prime}_{0}=t_{0}
.
∎

LEMMA 8.9
.

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Let
\sigma\in{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}(V,I)
.
Then
(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))\cap(T(I))
and
(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))\cap(B(I))

both have exactly one element.

Proof.

We will show that
(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))\cap(B(I))

has exactly one element;
the proof for
(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))\cap(T(I))
is similar.
Let
b:=-a_{I}
.
Then
b\notin I
and
B(I)={\overline{I}}^{3}\times\{b\}
.
Since
\sigma\in{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}(V,I)\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}(V)
,
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))={\mathbb{R}}
,
so fix
t_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\sigma))=b
.
Let
\rho:=\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\sigma)
.
Then
\rho\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)
.
Also,
\Pi_{4}(\rho)=b
,
i.e.
,
\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times\{b\}
.

By Lemma
8.2
,

I^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}
is
V
-invariant.
Then, by continuity,
{\overline{I}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}
is also
V
-invariant.
So, since
\sigma\in{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}(V,I)\subseteq{\overline{I}}^{4}\subseteq{\overline{I}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}
,
we see that
\rho\in{\overline{I}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then
\rho\in(\,{\overline{I}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}})\cap({\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times\{b\})={\overline{I}}^{3}\times\{b\}=B(I)
.
Thus
\rho\in(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))\cap(B(I))
,
so
(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))\cap(B(I))
has at least one element.
Assume
\tau\in(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))\cap(B(I))
.
We wish to show that
\tau=\rho
.

Choose
t_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\tau=\Phi_{t_{1}}^{V}(\sigma)
.
We have
\rho,\tau\in B(I)\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times\{b\}
.
So
\Pi_{4}(\rho)=b=\Pi_{4}(\tau)
.
That is,
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{1}}^{V}(\sigma))=b=\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\sigma))
.
So, by Lemma
8.8
,
t_{1}=t_{0}
.
Then
\tau=\Phi_{t_{1}}^{V}(\sigma)=\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\sigma)=\rho
.
∎

LEMMA 8.10
.

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Let
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Let
b\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash I
.
Let
t_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Assume
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\sigma))=b
.
Then both of the following are true:

(i)

For all
t>t_{0}
, we have
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma))>b
.

(ii)

For all
t<t_{0}
, we have
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma))<b
.

Proof.

We prove only (i); the proof of (ii) is similar.
Define
f:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
f(t)=\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma))
.
We wish to show that
f((t_{0},\infty))\subseteq(b,\infty)
.

We have
f(t_{0})=b
.
By Lemma
8.8
,
we get
f({\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{t_{0}\})\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{b\}
.
Then
f((t_{0},\infty))\subseteq f({\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{t_{0}\})\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{b\}=(-\infty,b)\cup(b,\infty)
.
Then, because
f((t_{0},\infty))
is connected, we have

\hbox{either}\qquad f((t_{0},\infty))\subseteq(-\infty,b)\qquad\hbox{or}\qquad f((t_{0},\infty))\subseteq(b,\infty).

It therefore suffices to prove
f((t_{0},\infty))\not\subseteq(-\infty,b)
.

Let
\tau:=\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\sigma)
.
Then
\Pi_{4}(\tau)=f(t_{0})=b\notin I
.
It follows that
\tau\in({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
,
and so
V(\tau)=V_{0}(\tau)
.
Thus
V(\tau)=(0,0,0,1)
.
Then

(d/dt)_{t=t_{0}}[\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma)]\quad=\quad V(\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\sigma))\quad=\quad V(\tau)\quad=\quad(0,0,0,1).

We have
f^{\prime}(t_{0})=(d/dt)_{t=t_{0}}[\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma))]=\Pi_{4}((d/dt)_{t=t_{0}}[\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma)])
.
It follows that
f^{\prime}(t_{0})=\Pi_{4}(0,0,0,1)=1
.

Since
f(t_{0})=b
and
f^{\prime}(t_{0})>0
, it follows, for some
t>t_{0}
, that
f(t)>b
.
That is,
f((t_{0},\infty))\not\subseteq(-\infty,b)
.
∎

COROLLARY 8.11
.

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Let
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Let
b\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash I
.
Let
t,t_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Assume
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\sigma))=b
.
Then both of the following are true:

(i)

t>t_{0}\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma))>b

(ii)

t<t_{0}\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma))<b
.

Proof.

We prove (i);
the proof of (ii) is similar.
By (i) of Lemma
8.10
,
we have
\Rightarrow
of (i).
We wish to prove
\Leftarrow
of (i).
Assume
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma))>b

and assume, for a contradiction, that
t\leq t_{0}
.

Since
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma))\neq b=\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\sigma))
,
we get
t\neq t_{0}
.
Then
t<t_{0}
.
Then, by (ii) of Lemma
8.10
,

\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma))<b
, contradiction.
∎

LEMMA 8.12
.

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Let
b\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash I
.
Let
H:={\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-\infty,b)
.
Then
\Phi_{(-\infty,0]}^{V}(H)\subseteq H
.

Proof.

Let
\tau\in H
,
t_{1}\leq 0
,
\rho:=\Phi_{t_{1}}^{V}(\tau)
.
We wish to prove that
\rho\in H
.

If
t_{1}=0
, then
\rho=\tau\in H
, and we are done, so we assume
t_{1}<0
.

We have
\tau\in H
, so
\Pi_{4}(\tau)<b
.
If
b\notin\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\tau))
,
then, by the Intermediate Value Theorem,
we have
\Pi_{4}(\phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\tau))\subseteq(-\infty,b)
,
from which we get

\rho\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\tau)\subseteq\Pi_{4}^{-1}((-\infty,b))=H
,
and we are done.
We therefore assume that
b\in\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\tau))
.

Fix
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
b=\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{s}^{V}(\tau))
.
Let
\sigma:=\Phi_{s}^{V}(\tau)
.
Let
t_{0}:=0
.
Then
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\sigma))=\Pi_{4}(\sigma)=\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{s}^{V}(\tau))=b
.
Also,
\Phi_{-s}^{V}(\sigma)=\tau
.

We have
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{-s}^{V}(\sigma))=\Pi_{4}(\tau)<b
.
Then, by Corollary
8.11
(ii), we get
-s\leq t_{0}
.
So
-s+t_{1}\leq t_{0}+t_{1}
.
As
t_{1}<0
,
t_{0}+t_{1}<t_{0}
.
Then
-s+t_{1}\leq t_{0}+t_{1}<t_{0}
.
Then, by Corollary
8.11
(i),

\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{-s+t_{1}}^{V}(\sigma))<b
.
Then
\rho=\Phi_{t_{1}}^{V}(\tau)=\Phi_{t_{1}}^{V}(\Phi_{-s}^{V}(\sigma))=\Phi_{-s+t_{1}}^{V}(\sigma)\in\Pi_{4}^{-1}((-\infty,b))=H
.
∎

LEMMA 8.13
.

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
and let
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Assume that
(V,\sigma)
is periodic.
Then
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)\subseteq I^{4}
.

Proof.

Fix
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let
\rho:=\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma)
.
We wish to show that
\rho\in I^{4}
.

As
(V,\sigma)
is periodic,
fix an integer
n\neq 0
such that
\Phi_{n}^{V}(\sigma)=\sigma
.
Then

\Phi_{n}^{V}(\rho)\,\,=\,\,\Phi_{n}^{V}(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma))\,\,=\,\,\Phi_{t}^{V}(\Phi_{n}^{V}(\sigma))\,\,=\,\,\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma)\,\,=\,\,\rho.

Then
\rho=\Phi_{-n}^{V}(\Phi_{n}^{V}(\rho))=\Phi_{-n}^{V}(\rho)
.
Since
n\neq 0\neq-n
, by Lemma
4.5
,
we have
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{n}^{V_{0}}(\rho))\neq\Pi_{4}(\rho)\neq\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{-n}^{V_{0}}(\rho))
.
Then
\Phi_{n}^{V_{0}}(\rho)\neq\rho\neq\Phi_{-n}^{V_{0}}(\rho)
.

Then

\Phi_{n}^{V}(\rho)=\rho\neq\Phi_{n}^{V_{0}}(\rho)

and

\Phi_{-n}^{V}(\rho)=\rho\neq\Phi_{-n}^{V_{0}}(\rho)
.
Then

\Phi_{n}^{V}(\rho)\neq\Phi_{n}^{V_{0}}(\rho),\qquad\Phi_{|n|}^{V}(\rho)\neq\Phi_{|n|}^{V_{0}}(\rho),\qquad\Phi_{-|n|}^{V}(\rho)\neq\Phi_{-|n|}^{V_{0}}(\rho).

Let
S_{1}:=I^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}
.
If
\rho\notin S_{1}
, then, by Lemma
8.1
(i), for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\Phi_{t}^{V}(\rho)=\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}(\rho)
,
and, in particular,
\Phi_{n}^{V}(\rho)=\Phi_{n}^{V_{0}}(\rho)
, contradiction.
Thus
\rho\in S_{1}
.
Let
S_{2}:={\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-\infty,a_{I})
.
If
\rho\notin S_{2}
, then, by Lemma
8.1
(ii), for all
t\geq 0
,

\Phi_{t}^{V}(\rho)=\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}(\rho)
,
and, in particular,
\Phi_{|n|}^{V}(\rho)=\Phi_{|n|}^{V_{0}}(\rho)
, contradiction.
Thus
\rho\in S_{2}
.
Let
S_{3}:={\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-a_{I},\infty)
.
If
\rho\notin S_{3}
, then, by Lemma
8.1
(iii), for all
t\leq 0
,

\Phi_{t}^{V}(\rho)=\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}(\rho)
,
and, in particular,
\Phi_{-|n|}^{V}(\rho)=\Phi_{-|n|}^{V_{0}}(\rho)
, contradiction.
Thus
\rho\in S_{3}
.

Then
\rho\in S_{1}\cap S_{2}\cap S_{3}=I^{4}
.
∎

9.
Downflow, upflow and timeflow

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
By Corollary
8.4
(i),
{\mathcal{U}}(V)
is open in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Also, as
I\in{\mathcal{C}}
,
I^{4}
is open in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Then, since
{\mathcal{U}}_{\circ}(V,I)=({\mathcal{U}}(V))\cap(I^{4})
, we see that

{\mathcal{U}}_{\circ}(V,I)
is open in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
By Lemma
8.9
, for all
\sigma\in{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}(V,I)
,
each of

(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))\,\cap\,(B(I))\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))\,\cap\,(T(I))

has exactly one element.
The
downflow map
of
(V,I)
is the function

DF_{I}^{V}:{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}(V,I)\to B(I)

which maps each
\sigma\in{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}(V,I)
to the unique element in the set

(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))\cap(B(I))
.
This map is a kind of projection, in the sense that,
for all
\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V,I)
, we have
DF_{I}^{V}(\sigma)=\sigma
.
The
upflow map
of
(V,I)
is the function

UF_{I}^{V}:{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}(V,I)\to T(I)

which maps each
\sigma\in{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}(V,I)
to the unique element in the set

(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))\cap(T(I))
.

By the definitions of
DF_{I}^{V}
and
UF_{I}^{V}
,
for each
\sigma\in{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}(V,I)
,
there exists
t_{\sigma}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that

\Phi_{t_{\sigma}}^{V}(DF_{I}^{V}(\sigma))=UF_{I}^{V}(\sigma)
;
moreover, by Lemma
8.8
, this
t_{\sigma}
is unique.
By Corollary
8.11
(i),
for all
\sigma\in{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}(V,I)
,
t_{\sigma}>0
.
The
timeflow map
of
(V,I)
is the function

TF_{I}^{V}:{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}(V,I)\to(0,\infty)

defined by
TF_{I}^{V}(\sigma)=t_{\sigma}
.
Then
TF_{I}^{V}
is
C^{0}
on
{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}(V,I)

and is
C^{\infty}
on
{\mathcal{U}}_{\circ}(V,I)
.

LEMMA 9.1
.

Let
I\in{\mathcal{I}}
.
Then

(i)

{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V_{0},I)=B(I)
and

(ii)

on
B(I)
, we have
SU_{I}=\Phi_{2a_{I}}^{V_{0}}=UF_{I}^{V_{0}}
.

Proof.

Since
{\mathcal{U}}(V_{0})={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, we have

{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V_{0},I)=({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\cap(B(I))=B(I)
.
Let
\rho\in B(I)
.
We wish to prove that

SU_{I}(\rho)=\Phi_{2a_{I}}^{V_{0}}(\rho)=UF_{I}^{V_{0}}(\rho)
.

Choose
w,x,y\in{\overline{I}}
such that
\rho=(w,x,y,-a_{I})
.
Then we have
SU_{I}(\rho)=(w,x,y,a_{I})
.
Also, we have

\Phi_{2a_{I}}^{V_{0}}(\rho)\quad=\quad(w,x,y,-a_{I}+2a_{I})\quad=\quad(w,x,y,a_{I}).

It therefore remains to prove that

UF_{I}^{V_{0}}(\rho)=(w,x,y,a_{I})
.

We have

(w,x,y,a_{I})=\Phi_{2a_{I}}^{V_{0}}(\rho)\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\rho)

and
(w,x,y,a_{I})\in T(I)
.
Then
(w,x,y,a_{I})\in(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\rho))\cap(T(I))
,
so
UF_{I}^{V_{0}}(\rho)=(w,x,y,a_{I})
.
∎

LEMMA 9.2
.

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
, let
\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V,I)

and let
t_{0}:=TF_{I}^{V}(\sigma)
.
Then, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have:

[\,\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma)\in I^{4}\,]\,\,\Leftrightarrow\,\,[\,0<t<t_{0}\,]
.

Proof.

Define
\psi:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by
\psi(t)=\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma)
.
We wish to prove that
\psi^{-1}(I^{4})=(0,t_{0})
.
Define
f:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
f(t)=\Pi_{4}(\psi(t))
.
Because
\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V,I)\subseteq B_{\circ}(I)\subseteq I^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}
,
by Lemma
8.2
, we get

\psi({\mathbb{R}})\subseteq I^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then
\psi^{-1}(I^{4})=f^{-1}(I)
.
We wish to prove that
f^{-1}(I)=(0,t_{0})
.

We have
\sigma\in B_{\circ}(I)\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times\{-a_{I}\}
,
so
\Pi_{4}(\sigma)=-a_{I}
.
Also,
\psi(0)=\sigma
.
Then
f(0)=\Pi_{4}(\psi(0))=\Pi_{4}(\sigma)=-a_{I}
.

We have

\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V,I)\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V,I)
,
so
\sigma=DF_{I}^{V}(\sigma)
.
Therefore, because
t_{0}=TF_{I}^{V}(\sigma)
,
we get
\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\sigma)=UF_{I}^{V}(\sigma)
.
It follows that
\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\sigma)\in T(I)\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times\{a_{I}\}
.
Then
f(t_{0})=\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\sigma))=a_{I}
.

By Lemma
8.8
,

f^{-1}(-a_{I})
and
f^{-1}(a_{I})
both have at most one element.
So, since
0\in f^{-1}(-a_{I})
and since
t_{0}\in f^{-1}(a_{I})
,
it follows that
f^{-1}(-a_{I})=\{0\}
and
f^{-1}(a_{I})=\{t_{0}\}
.
We have
\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V,I)\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}(V)
, so,
by Lemma
8.3
(a
\Rightarrow
c), we have

\displaystyle{\lim_{t\to\infty}\,[f(t)]=\infty}
and

\displaystyle{\lim_{t\to-\infty}\,[f(t)]=-\infty}
.
Then, by Lemma
3.2
,
we have
f^{-1}((-a_{I},a_{I}))=(0,t_{0})
.
That is, we have
f^{-1}(I)=(0,t_{0})
, as desired.
∎

LEMMA 9.3
.

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
and let
W\in{\mathcal{C}}
.
Assume that
W=V
on
I^{4}
.
Let
\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V,I)
.
Let
t_{0}:=TF_{I}^{V}(\sigma)
.
Then, for all
t\in[0,t_{0}]
, we have
\Phi_{t}^{W}(\sigma)=\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
9.2
,
for all
t\in(0,t_{0})
, we have

\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma)\subseteq I^{4}
.
The result then follows from Corollary
6.2
.
∎

LEMMA 9.4
.

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

UF_{I}^{V}=SU_{I}
on
{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V,I)
.

(b)

For all
\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V,I)
,
(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))\cup(I^{4})\,=\,(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma))\cup(I^{4})
.

(c)

For all
\sigma\in({\mathcal{U}}(V))\backslash(I^{4})
,
(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))\cup(I^{4})\,=\,(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma))\cup(I^{4})
.

Proof.

Proof of (a
\Rightarrow
b):

Assume that (a) is true.
Fix
\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V,I)
.
Let
S:=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)
and
S_{0}:=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)
.
We wish to prove
S\cup(I^{4})=S_{0}\cup(I^{4})
.

We have
\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V,I)\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V,I)

and
\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V,I)\subseteq B(I)
.
Let

\tau:=SU_{I}(\sigma),\qquad t:=TF_{I}^{V}(\sigma),\qquad t_{0}:=TF_{I}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)

By (a),
\tau=UF_{I}^{V}(\sigma)
.
By Lemma
9.1
, we have
SU_{I}(\sigma)=UF_{I}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)
,

i.e.
,
\tau=UF_{I}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)
.
Since
\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V,I)
, we get
\sigma=DF_{I}^{V}(\sigma)=DF_{I}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)
.

We have
\sigma=DF_{I}^{V}(\sigma)
and
\tau=UF_{I}^{V}(\sigma)

and
t=TF_{I}^{V}(\sigma)
.
It follows that
\tau=\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma)
.
We have
\sigma=DF_{I}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)
and
\tau=UF_{I}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)
.
Moreover, we have
t_{0}=TF_{I}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)
.
It follows that
\tau=\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)
.
Let

A:=\Phi_{(-\infty,0]}^{V}(\sigma)
,
B:=\Phi_{(0,t)}^{V}(\sigma)
,
C:=\Phi_{[t,\infty)}^{V}(\sigma)
,

A_{0}:=\Phi_{(-\infty,0]}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)
,
B_{0}:=\Phi_{(0,t_{0})}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)
,
C_{0}:=\Phi_{[t_{0},\infty)}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)
.

By Lemma
9.2
, we have
B\subseteq I^{4}
, so,
since
S=A\cup B\cup C
,

S\,\cup\,(I^{4})\quad=\quad(A\,\cup\,B\,\cup\,C)\,\cup\,(I^{4})\quad=\quad A\,\cup\,C\,\cup\,(I^{4}).

By Lemma
9.2
, we have
B_{0}\subseteq I^{4}
, so,
since
S_{0}=A_{0}\cup B_{0}\cup C_{0}
,

S_{0}\,\cup\,(I^{4})\quad=\quad(A_{0}\,\cup\,B_{0}\,\cup\,C_{0})\,\cup\,(I^{4})\quad=\quad A_{0}\,\cup\,C_{0}\,\cup\,(I^{4}).

It therefore suffices to show that
A=A_{0}
and that
C=C_{0}
.

We have
\sigma\in B(I)\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-\infty,a_{I}]
.
Thus, by Lemma
8.1
(iii),
we have
\Phi_{(-\infty,0]}^{V}(\sigma)=\Phi_{(-\infty,0]}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)
,
i.e.
,
A=A_{0}
.

We have
\tau=UF_{I}^{V}(\sigma)\in T(I)\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times[a_{I},\infty)
.
Thus, by Lemma
8.1
(ii),
we have
\Phi_{[0,\infty)}^{V}(\tau)=\Phi_{[0,\infty)}^{V_{0}}(\tau)
.
So, since
\tau=\Phi_{t}^{V}(\sigma)=\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)
, this yields

\Phi_{[t,\infty)}^{V}(\sigma)=\Phi_{[t_{0},\infty)}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)
,
i.e.
,
C=C_{0}
.

End of proof of (a
\Rightarrow
b).

Proof of (b
\Rightarrow
c):

Assume that (b) is true.
Let
\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}(V)
and assume that
\sigma\notin I^{4}
.
We wish to show that

(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))\cup(I^{4})=(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma))\cup(I^{4})
.

If
\sigma\notin I^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}
,

i.e.
, if
\sigma\in[({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\backslash(I^{3})]\times{\mathbb{R}}
,
then, by Lemma
8.1
(i),
we have
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)
,
and so
(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))\cup(I^{4})=(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma))\cup(I^{4})
,
and we are done.
We therefore assume that
\sigma\in I^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}
.

Since
\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}(V)
, we have
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))={\mathbb{R}}
.
Choose
t_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\sigma))=-a_{I}
.
Let
\sigma_{0}:=\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\sigma)
.
Then
\Pi_{4}(\sigma_{0})=-a_{I}
.
That is,
\sigma_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times\{-a_{I}\}
.
Since
\sigma\in I^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}
,
by Lemma
8.2
,

\sigma_{0}\in I^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then

\sigma_{0}\,\,\,\in\,\,\,(I^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}})\,\cap\,({\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times\{-a_{I}\})\,\,\,=\,\,\,I^{3}\,\times\,\{-a_{I}\}\,\,\,=\,\,\,B_{\circ}(I).

Since
\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}(V)
and
\sigma_{0}\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)
,
and since
{\mathcal{U}}(V)
is
V
-invariant,
it follows that
\sigma_{0}\in{\mathcal{U}}(V)
.
Then
\sigma_{0}\in({\mathcal{U}}(V))\cap(B_{\circ}(I))={\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V,I)
.
By (b),
(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma_{0}))\cup(I^{4})=(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma_{0}))\cup(I^{4})
.
It therefore suffices to show that

\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma_{0})=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)
and that
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma_{0})=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)
.
Because we have
\sigma_{0}\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)
,
it follows that
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma_{0})=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)
.
It remains to show that
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma_{0})=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)
.

We have

\sigma=\Phi_{-t_{0}}^{V}(\sigma_{0})\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma_{0})\subseteq(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma_{0}))\cup(I^{4})=(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma_{0}))\cup(I^{4})
.
So, since
\sigma\notin I^{4}
,
it follows that
\sigma\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma_{0})
.
Then
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma_{0})=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)
.

End of proof of (b
\Rightarrow
c).

Proof of (c
\Rightarrow
a):

Assume that (c) is true.
Let
\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V,I)
.
We wish to show that
UF_{I}^{V}(\sigma)=SU_{I}(\sigma)

We have
\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V,I)\subseteq B(I)

and
(B(I))\cap(I^{4})=\emptyset
.
Then
\sigma\notin I^{4}
.
So, since
\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V,I)\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}(V)
,
we get
\sigma\in({\mathcal{U}}(V))\backslash(I^{4})
.
By (c),

(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))\,\cup\,(I^{4})\quad=\quad(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma))\,\cup\,(I^{4}).

So, intersecting with
T(I)
, we get

[\,(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))\cup(I^{4})\,]\,\,\cap\,\,[\,T(I)\,]\,\,=\,\,[\,(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma))\cup(I^{4})\,]\,\,\cap\,\,[\,T(I)\,].

So, because
I^{4}\cap(T(I))=\emptyset
,
we get

[\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)]\,\cap\,[T(I)]\quad=\quad[\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)]\,\cap\,[T(I)].

Thus
UF_{I}^{V}(\sigma)=UF_{I}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)
.
By Lemma
9.1
,

UF_{I}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)=SU_{I}(\sigma)
.
Then
UF_{I}^{V}(\sigma)=UF_{I}^{V_{0}}(\sigma)=SU_{I}(\sigma)
.

End of proof of (c
\Rightarrow
a).

∎

LEMMA 9.5
.

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Then
DF_{I}^{V}({\mathcal{U}}_{\circ}(V,I))\subseteq B_{\circ}(I)
.

Proof.

Because
{\mathcal{U}}_{\circ}(V,I)\subseteq I^{4}\subseteq I^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}
,
by Lemma
8.2
, it follows that

\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}({\mathcal{U}}_{\circ}(V,I))\subseteq I^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then

DF_{I}^{V}({\mathcal{U}}_{\circ}(V,I))\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}({\mathcal{U}}_{\circ}(V,I))\subseteq I^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}
.

We have
{\mathcal{U}}_{\circ}(V,I)\subseteq{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}(V,I)
and
DF_{I}^{V}(\,{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}(V,I))\subseteq B(I)
.
Then

DF_{I}^{V}({\mathcal{U}}_{\circ}(V,I))\quad\subseteq\quad DF_{I}^{V}(\,{\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}}(V,I))\quad\subseteq\quad B(I).

Then

DF_{I}^{V}({\mathcal{U}}_{\circ}(V,I))\subseteq(I^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}})\cap(B(I))=B_{\circ}(I)
.
∎

10.
Vector fields
V_{\#}^{s}
on
{\mathbb{R}}
with variable travel time

For this section, let
I:=(-95,95)\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
and
define
W:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
W(y)=1-[1/2][\zeta_{I}(y)]
.
For all
y\in{\overline{I}}
, we have
W(y)=1/2
.
Then
\Phi_{380}^{W}(-95)=95
.
For all
y\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have
1/2\leq W(y)\leq 1
.
For this section, fix
C>380
such that
\Phi_{C}^{W}(-100)=100
.

For all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, define
V_{*}^{x}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

V_{*}^{x}(y)\quad=\quad 1\,\,-\,\,[1/2][\zeta_{I}(y)][1-(\zeta(x))];

then
(x,y)\mapsto V_{*}^{x}(y):{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is
C^{\infty}
.
Moreover,

•

\forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\forall y\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash(-96,96)
,
V_{*}^{x}(y)=1
.

•

\forall x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
1/2\leq V_{*}^{x}(y)\leq 1
.

Moreover,

(i)

\forall x\leq 0
,
\forall y\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
V_{*}^{x}(y)=1
and

(ii)

\forall x\geq 1
,
\forall y\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
V_{*}^{x}(y)=W(y)
.

By (i), for all
x\leq 0
, we have
\Phi_{200}^{V_{*}^{x}}(-100)=100
.
By (ii), for all
x\geq 1
, we have
\Phi_{C}^{V_{*}^{x}}(-100)=\Phi_{C}^{W}(-100)=100
.

For all
y\in{\mathbb{R}}
, define
\beta_{y}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
\beta_{y}(x)=V_{*}^{x}(y)
.
Then we have
\beta_{y}=1-[1/2][\zeta_{I}(y)][1-\zeta]
,
so
\beta_{y}^{\prime}:=[\zeta_{I}(y)][(\zeta^{\prime})/2]
.
Then

(iii)

for all
y\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\beta_{y}^{\prime}\leq 0
on
(0,1)
and

(iv)

for all
y\in{\overline{I}}
,
\beta_{y}^{\prime}<0
on
(0,1)
.

For all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, let
\gamma(x)\in[200,C]
be the unique real number
such that
\Phi_{\gamma(x)}^{V_{*}^{x}}(-100)=100
.
Define
F:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
F(x,t)=\Phi_{t}^{V_{*}^{x}}(-100)
.
Then, for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\gamma(x)
is defined implicitly by
F(x,\gamma(x))=100
.
Let
\partial_{1},\partial_{2}
be the standard framing of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Then, for all
(x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, we have
(\partial_{2}F)(x,t)=V_{*}^{x}(F(x,t))\geq 1/2
.
That is,
\partial_{2}F\geq 1/2
.
Then, by the Implicit Function Theorem,

\gamma:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is
C^{\infty}
, and, moreover,

(v)

\forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\gamma^{\prime}(x)\,\,\,=\,\,\,-\,\,[\,(\partial_{1}F)(x,\gamma(x))\,]\,\,/\,\,[\,(\partial_{2}F)(x,\gamma(x))\,]
.

From (iii) and (iv) above, we can prove:

(vi)

\forall(x,t)\in(0,1)\times(5,\infty)
,
(\partial_{1}F)(x,t)<0
.

[Thanks to R. Moeckel for showing me the following elementary proof of (vi):
Define
U:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
by
U(x,y)=(0,V_{*}^{x}(y))
.
Then
U
is
C^{\infty}
and bounded, and, therefore, complete.
The defining equation for
\Phi_{t}^{U}(x,y)
is
(\partial/\partial t)(\Phi_{t}^{U}(x,y))=U(\Phi_{t}^{U}(x,y))
.
Differentiating this equation with respect to
x
and
y
,
we obtain the defining equation for the induced flow in the tangent bundle
T{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
We identify
T{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
with
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
This induced flow then identifies with a flow on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}

that combines
\Phi_{t}^{U}
, operating on the first two coordinates,
with a time-dependent vector field on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, whose flow operates on the last two.
For each
x,y,t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, that time-dependent vector field is linear
and is represented by the Jacobian

J_{t}(x,y):=(DU)(\Phi_{t}^{U}(x,y)):{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
For all
x,y,t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

(\,J_{t}(x,y)\,)\,(\,\{0\}\,\times\,{\mathbb{R}}\,)\qquad\subseteq\qquad\{0\}\,\times\,{\mathbb{R}}.

Geometrically, this says: For all
x,y,t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
the vector field represented by
J_{t}(x,y)
,
when restricted to the line
\{0\}\times{\mathbb{R}}
,
consists of vectors that all vanish or point straight upward or point straight downward.
Using (iii) above, we see that, on
0<x<1
,
y=-100
,
0\leq t\leq 5
,

(\,J_{t}(x,y)\,)\,(\,(0,\infty)\,\times\,\{0\}\,)\qquad\subseteq\qquad\{0\}\,\times\,(-\infty,0].

Geometrically, this says: On
0<x<1
,
y=-100
,
0\leq t\leq 5
,
the vector field represented by
J_{t}(x,y)
,
when restricted to the ray
(0,\infty)\times\{0\}
,
consists of vectors that all either vanish or point straight downward.
Using (iv) above, we see that, on
0<x<1
,
y=-100
,
t>5
,

(\,J_{t}(x,y)\,)\,(\,(0,\infty)\,\times\,\{0\}\,)\qquad\subseteq\qquad\{0\}\,\times\,(-\infty,0).

Geometrically, this says: On
0<x<1
,
y=-100
,
t>5
,
the vector field represented by
J_{t}(x,y)
,
when restricted to the ray
(0,\infty)\times\{0\}
,
consists of vectors that all point straight downward.
Let
E
denote the restriction of the vector field
\partial_{1}
to the segment
0<x<1
,
y=-100
.
The three geometric observations above show that, for time
t>5
,
any tangent vector in
E
is constrained to flow to a vector pointing into
the open fourth quadrant
(0,\infty)\times(-\infty,0)
.
On
(x,t)\in(0,1)\times(5,\infty)
,
each value of
(\partial_{1}F)(x,t)
is exactly the slope of such a vector.
A vector that points into the open fourth quadrant has negative slope.]

For all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have
\gamma(x)\geq 200
.
Then, for all
x\in(0,1)
, we have

(x,\gamma(x))\in(0,1)\times(5,\infty)
,
so, by (vi) above,
(\partial_{1}F)(x,\gamma(x))<0
.
Then, because
\partial_{2}F\geq 1/2
, by (v) above,
we see, for all
x\in(0,1)
, that
\gamma^{\prime}(x)>0
.
Also,
\gamma
is
C^{0}
on
[0,1]
.
Then, by the Mean Value Theorem,
\gamma
is increasing on
[0,1]
.
Also,

\gamma=200
on
(-\infty,0]
and
\gamma=C
on
[1,\infty)
.

It follows, for all
s\in[200,C]
, that there is a unique
x_{s}\in[0,1]
such that
\gamma(x_{s})=s
.
By the Inverse Function Theorem, we have

•

s\mapsto x_{s}:[200,C]\to{\mathbb{R}}
is
C^{0}
and

•

s\mapsto x_{s}:(200,C)\to{\mathbb{R}}
is
C^{\infty}
.

Since
\gamma(0)=200
, it follows that
x_{200}=0
.
Recall that
C>380
.
For all
s\in[200,380]
, define
V_{\#}^{s}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
V_{\#}^{s}(y):=V_{*}^{x_{s}}(y)
.
Then

•

(s,y)\,\,\mapsto\,\,V_{\#}^{s}(y)\,:\,[200,380]\,\,\times\,\,{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is
C^{0}
,

•

(s,y)\,\,\mapsto\,\,V_{\#}^{s}(y)\,:\,(200,380)\,\,\times\,\,{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is
C^{\infty}
,

•

\forall s\in[200,380]
,
\forall y\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
1/2\leq V_{\#}^{s}(y)\leq 1
,

•

\forall s\in[200,380]
,
\forall y\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash(-96,96)
,
V_{\#}^{s}(y)=1
,

•

\forall y\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
V_{\#}^{200}(y)=1
and

•

\forall s\in[200,380]
,
\Phi_{s}^{V_{\#}^{s}}(-100)=100
.

11.
Definitions of and results about
{\mathcal{D}}_{+}
,
{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{+}^{\#}
and
{\mathcal{D}}_{*}

Recall, from §
9
,
the definitions of
DF_{I}^{V}
,
UF_{I}^{V}
and
TF_{I}^{V}
.
Let

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{+}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}\,|\,UF_{I}^{V}=SU_{I}\hbox{ on }{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V,I)\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}\,|\,\xi_{I}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V,I)\}\qquad\hbox{and}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{+}^{\#}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle({\mathcal{D}}_{+})\,\cap\,({\mathcal{D}}^{\#}).

For all
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
, give
{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V,I)
and
B_{\circ}(I)

their relative topologies, inherited from
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
;
then, by Corollary
8.4
(iii),
{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V,I)
is open in
B_{\circ}(I)
,
so the open sets of
{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V,I)
are all open in
B_{\circ}(I)
, as well.
Finally, let
{\mathcal{D}}_{*}
denote the set of all
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}_{+}^{\#}
such that

for some integer
j\geq 1
and some open neighborhood
N
in
{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V,I)

of
\xi_{I}
, we have:
TF_{I}^{V}=j
on
N
.

LEMMA 11.1
.

Let
(V_{1},I_{1})\in{\mathcal{D}}
and let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{M}}(V_{1},I_{1})
.
Assume
V(({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I_{1}^{4}))\subseteq\{(0,0,0)\}\times(0,\infty)
.
Then all of the following are true:

(i)

For all
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, we have
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{1}}(\sigma)=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma)
.

(ii)

{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})={\mathcal{U}}(V)
.

(iii)

If
V_{1}
is porous, then
V
is porous.

(iv)

If
(V_{1},I_{1})\in{\mathcal{D}}_{+}
, then
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}_{+}
.

(v)

If
(V_{1},I_{1})\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}
, then
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}
.

Proof.

Let
S:=\{(0,0,0)\}\times(0,\infty)\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Then
V(({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I_{1}^{4}))\subseteq S
.
Also, since
(V_{1},I_{1})\in{\mathcal{D}}
, we get

V_{1}=V_{0}
on
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I_{1}^{4})
, so

V_{1}(({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I_{1}^{4}))\quad=\quad\{(0,0,0,1)\}\quad\subseteq\quad S.

For all
s,s^{\prime}\in S
, there exists
c>0
such that
s^{\prime}=cs
.
Then, for all
\sigma\in({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I_{1}^{4})
,
there exists
c>0
such that
V_{1}(\sigma)=c\cdot(V(\sigma))
.
Since
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{M}}(V_{1},I_{1})
,
it follows that
V=V_{1}
on
I_{1}^{4}
.
Then, for all
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, there exists
c>0
such that
V_{1}(\sigma)=c\cdot(V(\sigma))
.
Then Corollary
5.3
yields (i).
From (i), we see, for all
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, that

[\,\,\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{1}}(\sigma))\,=\,{\mathbb{R}}\,\,]\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad[\,\,\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))\,=\,{\mathbb{R}}\,\,],

i.e.
, that

[\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})]\Leftrightarrow[\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}(V)]
.
This proves (ii).
If
V_{1}
is porous, then
{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})
is dense in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,
so, by (ii),
{\mathcal{U}}(V)
is dense in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, which means that
V
is porous.
This proves (iii).

Proof of (iv):

Assuming
(V_{1},I_{1})\in{\mathcal{D}}_{+}
, it follows that
UF_{I_{1}}^{V_{1}}=SU_{I_{1}}
on
{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V_{1},I_{1})
.
Then, by Lemma
9.4
(a
\Rightarrow
c), we have

for all
\sigma\in({\mathcal{U}}(V_{1}))\backslash(I_{1}^{4})
,
(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{1}}(\sigma))\cup(I_{1}^{4})\,=\,(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma))\cup(I_{1}^{4})
.

We have
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{M}}(V_{1},I_{1})
, so
I_{1}\subseteq I
.
Then

for all
\sigma\in({\mathcal{U}}(V_{1}))\backslash(I^{4})
,
(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{1}}(\sigma))\cup(I^{4})\,=\,(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma))\cup(I^{4})
.

So, by (i) and (ii), we conclude that

for all
\sigma\in({\mathcal{U}}(V))\backslash(I^{4})
,
(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma))\cup(I^{4})\,=\,(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{0}}(\sigma))\cup(I^{4})
.

Also,
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{M}}(V_{1},I_{1})\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}
.
Then, by Lemma
9.4
(c
\Rightarrow
a),
we see that
UF_{I}^{V}=SU_{I}
on
{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V,I)
.
Then
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}_{+}
.

End of proof of (iv).

Proof of (v):

Assuming
(V_{1},I_{1})\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}
, we have
\xi_{I_{1}}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V_{1},I_{1})
.
Let
t_{0}:=a_{I}-a_{I_{1}}
.
By Lemma
8.1
(iii),

\Phi_{-t_{0}}^{V_{1}}(\xi_{I_{1}})=\Phi_{-t_{0}}^{V_{0}}(\xi_{I_{1}})
.
By Lemma
4.4
(i),

\Phi_{-t_{0}}^{V_{0}}(\xi_{I_{1}})=\xi_{I}
.
Then
\Phi_{-t_{0}}^{V_{1}}(\xi_{I_{1}})=\Phi_{-t_{0}}^{V_{0}}(\xi_{I_{1}})=\xi_{I}
.
So, because
\xi_{I_{1}}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V_{1},I_{1})\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})
,
and because
{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})
is
V_{1}
-invariant,
we see that
\xi_{I}\in{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})
.
So, by (ii), we conclude that
\xi_{I}\in{\mathcal{U}}(V)
.
Moreover,
\xi_{I}\in B(I)

and
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{M}}(V_{1},I_{1})\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}
.
Then

\xi_{I}\quad\in\quad({\mathcal{U}}(V))\,\cap\,(B(I))\quad=\quad{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V,I).

Then
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}
, as desired.

End of proof of (v).

∎

LEMMA 11.2
.

Let
(W,J)\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}
.
Assume that
W
is porous.
Then there exists
(V_{1},I_{1})\in({\mathcal{M}}_{*}(W,J))\cap({\mathcal{D}}_{+}^{\#})

such that
V_{1}
is porous.

Proof.

Define
\lambda:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by
\lambda(w,x,y,z)=(-w,-x,-y,z)
.
Let
c:=a_{J}+1
.
Define a reflection
R:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by

R\,(\,w\,,\,x\,,\,y\,,\,z\,)\quad=\quad(\,\,w\,\,,\,\,x\,\,,\,\,y\,\,,\,\,2c-z\,\,).

For any
X:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, we define
{\overline{X}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by
{\overline{X}}(\tau)=\lambda(X(R(\tau)))
.
For any
\gamma:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, we define
{\overline{\gamma}}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by
{\overline{\gamma}}(t)=R(\gamma(-t))
.
If
\gamma:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
and
X:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
are both
C^{\infty}
,
and if
\gamma
is a flowline of
X
,
then
{\overline{\gamma}}
is a flowline of
{\overline{X}}
.

Let
S:={\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times[c-1,c+1]\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Then
S\subseteq({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(J^{4})
and
R(S)=S
.
We have
\lambda(0,0,0,1)=(0,0,0,1)
, so
{\overline{V}}_{0}=V_{0}
.
Since
(W,J)\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}
, we have
W=V_{0}
on
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(J^{4})
.
Then
W=V_{0}
on
S
, so
{\overline{W}}={\overline{V}}_{0}
on
R(S)
,
i.e.
, on
S
.
Then, on
S
, we have
{\overline{W}}={\overline{V}}_{0}=V_{0}=W
.

Let
H:={\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-\infty,c+1)
.
Then
R(H)={\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(c-1,\infty)
.
Then
H\cup(R(H))={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
and
H\cap(R(H))\subseteq S
.
Define
V_{1}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by

V_{1}:=W
on
H
and
V_{1}:={\overline{W}}
on
R(H)
.

Then
V_{1}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
is
C^{\infty}
.
Moreover,
{\overline{V}}_{1}=V_{1}
, so, for any flowline
\gamma:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
of
V_{1}
,
we see that
{\overline{\gamma}}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
is also a flowline of
V_{1}
.

For all
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, if
\Pi_{4}(\sigma)=c
, then
R(\sigma)=\sigma
.
Therefore, for any map
\gamma:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, if
\Pi_{4}(\gamma(0))=c
, then
{\overline{\gamma}}(0)=R(\gamma(0))=\gamma(0)
.
Consequently, if
\gamma:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
is a flowline of
V_{1}
and if
\Pi_{4}(\gamma(0))=c
,
then, by uniqueness of solutions of ODEs, we see that
{\overline{\gamma}}=\gamma
.

Since
(W,J)\in{\mathcal{D}}
, it follows that
W\in{\mathcal{C}}
, so
W({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\subseteq{\overline{I}}_{0}^{4}
.
So, since
\lambda(\,{\overline{I}}_{0}^{4}\,)={\overline{I}}_{0}^{4}
,
we see that
{\overline{W}}({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\subseteq{\overline{I}}_{0}^{4}
.
Then
V_{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\subseteq{\overline{I}}_{0}^{4}
.
Then
V_{1}\in{\mathcal{C}}
.

Let
I_{1}:=3J+3I_{0}
.
Then
a_{I_{1}}=3a_{J}+3a_{I_{0}}=3a_{J}+3=3c
.

As
W=V_{0}
on
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(J^{4})
,
we see that
{\overline{W}}={\overline{V}}_{0}=V_{0}
on
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(R(J^{4}))
.
Then
V_{1}=V_{0}
on
[{\mathbb{R}}^{4}]\backslash[(J^{4})\cup(R(J^{4}))]
.
So, since
(J^{4})\cup(R(J^{4}))\subseteq I_{1}^{4}
,
it follows that
V_{1}=V_{0}
on
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I_{1}^{4})
.
Then
(V_{1},I_{1})\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
As
J^{4}\subseteq H
, we get
V_{1}=W
on
J^{4}
.
Also,
a_{J}<3a_{J}+3=a_{I_{1}}
.
Then
(V_{1},I_{1})\in{\mathcal{M}}(W,J)
.

Since
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-\infty,-a_{J})\subseteq H
,
we see that
V_{1}=W
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-\infty,-a_{J})
.
As
(W,J)\in{\mathcal{D}}
, we get
W\in{\mathcal{V}}(a_{J})
.
That is,
W=V_{0}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-\infty,-a_{J})
.
Therefore
V_{1}=W=V_{0}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-\infty,-a_{J})
,
and so we have
V_{1}\in{\mathcal{V}}(a_{J})
.
So, because
(V_{1},I_{1})\in{\mathcal{M}}(W,J)
, we conclude that
(V_{1},I_{1})\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}(W,J)
.
It remains to prove that
(V_{1},I_{1})\in{\mathcal{D}}_{+}^{\#}

and that
V_{1}
is porous.

Claim 1:
Let
\tau\in H
and let
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Assume that
\Phi_{t}^{W}(\tau)\in H
.
Then
\Phi_{t}^{V_{1}}(\tau)=\Phi_{t}^{W}(\tau)
.

Proof of Claim 1:

Let
s:=\max\{0,t\}
.
Let
\sigma:=\Phi_{s}^{W}(\tau)
.
As
s\in\{0,t\}
, we get
\sigma\in\{\tau,\Phi_{t}^{W}(\tau)\}\subseteq H
.
We have
c+1=a_{J}+2>a_{J}
, so
c+1\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash J
.
Also, recall that
(W,J)\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Then, by Lemma
8.12
,

\Phi_{(-\infty,0]}^{W}(\sigma)\subseteq H
.
So, as
V_{1}=W
on
H
,
we see, from Lemma
6.1
,
that
\Phi_{-|t|}^{V_{1}}(\sigma)=\Phi_{-|t|}^{W}(\sigma)
.
If
t\leq 0
, then
\sigma=\tau
and
-|t|=t
,
and so
\Phi_{t}^{V_{1}}(\tau)=\Phi_{-|t|}^{V_{1}}(\sigma)=\Phi_{-|t|}^{W}(\sigma)=\Phi_{t}^{W}(\tau)
, as desired.
We therefore assume that
t>0
.
Then
\sigma=\Phi_{t}^{W}(\tau)
and
|t|=t
.
Then

\Phi_{-t}^{V_{1}}(\sigma)\,=\,\Phi_{-|t|}^{V_{1}}(\sigma)\,=\,\Phi_{-|t|}^{W}(\sigma)\,=\,\Phi_{-t}^{W}(\sigma)\,=\,\Phi_{-t}^{W}(\Phi_{t}^{W}(\tau))\,=\,\tau.

Applying
\Phi_{t}^{V_{1}}
to this yields
\sigma=\Phi_{t}^{V_{1}}(\tau)
.
That is,
\Phi_{t}^{W}(\tau)=\Phi_{t}^{V_{1}}(\tau)
, as desired.

End of proof of Claim 1.

Claim 2:

({\mathcal{U}}(W))\cap H\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})
.

Proof of Claim 2:

Fix
\tau\in{\mathcal{U}}(W)
, and assume that
\tau\in H
.
We wish to show that
\tau\in{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})
.

Since
\tau\in{\mathcal{U}}(W)
, we have
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{W}(\tau))={\mathbb{R}}
.
Fix
t_{0},t_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}

such that
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{0}}^{W}(\tau))=c

and such that
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{1}}^{W}(\tau))=-4c
.
Then
