\Phi_{t_{0}}^{W}(\tau),\,\,\Phi_{t_{1}}^{W}(\tau)\quad\in\quad\Pi_{4}^{-1}((-\infty,c+1))\quad=\quad H.

Then, by Claim 1, we get

\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V_{1}}(\tau)=\Phi_{t_{0}}^{W}(\tau)

and
\Phi_{t_{1}}^{V_{1}}(\tau)=\Phi_{t_{1}}^{W}(\tau)
.
Then
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V_{1}}(\tau))=\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{0}}^{W}(\tau))=c

and
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{1}}^{V_{1}}(\tau))=\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{1}}^{W}(\tau))=-4c
.

Define
\gamma_{0}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by
\gamma_{0}(t)=\Phi_{t_{0}+t}^{V_{1}}(\tau)
.
Then
\gamma_{0}(0)=\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V_{1}}(\tau)
and
\gamma_{0}(t_{1}-t_{0})=\Phi_{t_{1}}^{V_{1}}(\tau)

and
\gamma_{0}(t_{0}-t_{1})=\Phi_{2t_{0}-t_{1}}^{V_{1}}(\tau)
.
As
\gamma_{0}
is a flowline of
V_{1}
and

\Pi_{4}(\gamma_{0}(0))=\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V_{1}}(\tau))=c
,
it follows that
\gamma_{0}={\overline{\gamma}}_{0}
.
Then
\gamma_{0}(t_{0}-t_{1})={\overline{\gamma}}_{0}(t_{0}-t_{1})=R(\gamma_{0}(t_{1}-t_{0}))
.
So, by definition of
R
, we get

\Pi_{4}(\gamma_{0}(t_{0}-t_{1}))=2c-[\Pi_{4}(\gamma_{0}(t_{1}-t_{0}))]
.
Also, we have

\Pi_{4}(\gamma_{0}(t_{1}-t_{0}))\,\,=\,\,\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{1}}^{V_{1}}(\tau))\,\,=\,\,-4c.

Then

\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{2t_{0}-t_{1}}^{V_{1}}(\tau))=\Pi_{4}(\gamma_{0}(t_{0}-t_{1}))=2c-[-4c]=6c
.
Thus

•

\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{2t_{0}-t_{1}}^{V_{1}}(\tau))\,\,=\,\,6c\,\,>\,\,3c\,\,=\,\,a_{I_{1}}
and

•

\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{1}}^{V_{1}}(\tau))\,\,=\,\,-4c\,\,<\,\,-3c\,\,=\,\,-a_{I_{1}}
.

So, since
(V_{1},I_{1})\in{\mathcal{D}}
,
by Lemma
8.3
(b
\Rightarrow
a),
we get
\tau\in{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})
, as desired.

End of proof of Claim 2.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be the set of all flowlines
\gamma:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
of
V_{1}
such that
\Pi_{4}(\gamma(0))=c

and
\Pi_{4}(\gamma({\mathbb{R}}))={\mathbb{R}}
.
Then, for all
\gamma\in{\mathcal{F}}
, we have
{\overline{\gamma}}=\gamma
.
Let
\displaystyle{U:=\bigcup_{\gamma\in{\mathcal{F}}}\,[\gamma({\mathbb{R}})]}
and
U_{0}:={\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})
.

Claim 3:

U\subseteq U_{0}
.

Proof of Claim 3:

Fix
\gamma\in{\mathcal{F}}
and
t_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let
\sigma_{1}:=\gamma(t_{1})
.
We wish to show that
\sigma_{1}\in{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})
.

Since
\gamma\in{\mathcal{F}}
,
we see that
\gamma
is a flowline of
V_{1}
and
that
\Pi_{4}(\gamma({\mathbb{R}}))={\mathbb{R}}
.
Let
\sigma_{0}:=\gamma(0)
.
Because
\gamma
is a flowline of
V_{1}
,
it follows, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, that
\gamma(t)=\Phi_{t}^{V_{1}}(\sigma_{0})
.
Then
\Phi_{t_{1}}^{V_{1}}(\sigma_{0})=\gamma(t_{1})=\sigma_{1}
.

Then, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\Phi_{t}^{V_{1}}(\sigma_{1})=\Phi_{t+t_{1}}^{V_{1}}(\sigma_{0})=\gamma(t+t_{1})
.
Then
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{1}}(\sigma_{1})=\gamma({\mathbb{R}})
,
so
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{1}}(\sigma_{1}))=\Pi_{4}(\gamma({\mathbb{R}}))={\mathbb{R}}
.
Then
\sigma_{1}\in{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})
, as desired.

End of proof of Claim 3.

Claim 4:

U_{0}\subseteq U
.

Proof of Claim 4:

Fix
\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})
.
We wish to show that there exists
\gamma\in{\mathcal{F}}
such that
\sigma\in\gamma({\mathbb{R}})
.

Because
\sigma\in{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})
,
it follows that
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{1}}(\sigma))={\mathbb{R}}
.
Choose
t_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{1}}^{V_{1}}(\sigma))=c
.
Define
\gamma:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by
\gamma(t)=\Phi_{t+t_{1}}^{V_{1}}(\sigma)
.

Then
\gamma
is a flowline of
V_{1}
and
\Pi_{4}(\gamma(0))=\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{1}}^{V_{1}}(\sigma))=c
.
Moreover,
\Pi_{4}(\gamma({\mathbb{R}}))=\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{1}}(\sigma))={\mathbb{R}}
.
Then
\gamma\in{\mathcal{F}}
.

Also,
\sigma=\Phi_{0}^{V_{1}}(\sigma)=\gamma(-t_{1})\in\gamma({\mathbb{R}})
.

End of proof of Claim 4.

For any
\gamma\in{\mathcal{F}}
, we have
{\overline{\gamma}}=\gamma
, so
R(\gamma({\mathbb{R}}))={\overline{\gamma}}({\mathbb{R}})=\gamma({\mathbb{R}})
.
Then
\displaystyle{R(U)=\bigcup_{\gamma\in{\mathcal{F}}}\,[R(\gamma({\mathbb{R}}))]=\bigcup_{\gamma\in{\mathcal{F}}}\,[\gamma({\mathbb{R}})]=U}
.
By Claim 3 and Claim 4, we have
U=U_{0}
.
Then
R(U_{0})=R(U)=U=U_{0}
, so
U_{0}\cup(R(U_{0}))=U_{0}
.

Let
U_{1}:=({\mathcal{U}}(W))\cap H
.
By Claim 2, we have
U_{1}\subseteq U_{0}
.
Then
U_{1}\cup(R(U_{1}))\subseteq U_{0}\cup(R(U_{0}))=U_{0}
.
Since
W
is porous,
{\mathcal{U}}(W)
is dense in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Then, since
U_{1}=({\mathcal{U}}(W))\cap H
, it follows that
U_{1}
is dense in
H
.
So, since
H\cup(R(H))={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,
we conclude that
U_{1}\cup(R(U_{1}))
is dense in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
So, since
U_{1}\cup(R(U_{1}))\subseteq U_{0}
,
we see that
U_{0}
is dense in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
So, as
U_{0}={\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})
,
V_{1}
is porous.
It remains to show that
(V_{1},I_{1})\in{\mathcal{D}}_{+}^{\#}
.

Claim 5:

(V_{1},I_{1})\in{\mathcal{D}}_{+}
.

Proof of Claim 5:

Recall that
(V_{1},I_{1})\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Let
\rho\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V_{1},I_{1})
.
Let
\sigma:=SU_{I_{1}}(\rho)
.
We wish to prove

UF_{I_{1}}^{V_{1}}(\rho)=\sigma
.
As
\rho\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V_{1},I_{1})\subseteq B(I_{1})
,
we get
\sigma=SU_{I_{1}}(\rho)\in SU_{I_{1}}(B(I_{1}))=T(I_{1})
.
So, as
\{UF_{I_{1}}^{V_{1}}(\rho)\}=(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{1}}(\rho))\cap(T(I_{1}))
,
we wish to prove
\sigma\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{1}}(\rho)
.

We have
\rho\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V_{1},I_{1})\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})
,
so
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{1}}(\rho))={\mathbb{R}}
.
Fix
t_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V_{1}}(\rho))=c
.
Define
\gamma_{1}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by
\gamma_{1}(t)=\Phi_{t_{0}+t}^{V_{1}}(\rho)
.
Then
\gamma_{1}(0)=\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V_{1}}(\rho)

and
\gamma_{1}(2c-t_{0})=\Phi_{2c}^{V_{1}}(\rho)
.
As
\gamma_{1}
is a flowline for
V_{1}
and
\Pi_{4}(\gamma_{1}(0))=\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V_{1}}(\rho))=c
,
we see that
{\overline{\gamma}}_{1}=\gamma_{1}
.
Then
{\overline{\gamma}}_{1}({\mathbb{R}})=\gamma_{1}({\mathbb{R}})
.

We have
\rho\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V_{1},I_{1})\subseteq B(I_{1})\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times\{-a_{I_{1}}\}={\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times\{-3c\}
.
Fix
w,x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\rho=(w,x,y,-3c)
.
Then
\Phi_{2c}^{V_{0}}(\rho)=(w,x,y,-c)
and

\sigma=SU_{I_{1}}(\rho)=(w,x,y,3c)=R(w,x,y,-c)
.
Moreover, because
\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times\{-3c\}
, we get

\Phi_{[0,2c]}^{V_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times[-3c,-c]
.
Also, because
c=a_{J}+1
, it follows that

{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times[-3c,-c]\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-\infty,-a_{J})
.
Therefore

\Phi_{[0,2c]}^{V_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times[-3c,-c]\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-\infty,-a_{J})
.
So, since
V_{1}=V_{0}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-\infty,-a_{J})
, by Lemma
6.1
, we get

\Phi_{2c}^{V_{1}}(\rho)=\Phi_{2c}^{V_{0}}(\rho)
.
It follows that

(w,x,y,-c)=\Phi_{2c}^{V_{0}}(\rho)=\Phi_{2c}^{V_{1}}(\rho)=\gamma_{1}(2c-t_{0})
.
Then
\sigma=R(w,x,y,-c)=R(\gamma_{1}(2c-t_{0}))={\overline{\gamma}}_{1}(t_{0}-2c)
,
and so we have
\sigma\in{\overline{\gamma}}_{1}({\mathbb{R}})=\gamma_{1}({\mathbb{R}})=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{1}}(\rho)
,
as desired.

End of proof of Claim 5.

Recall that
V_{1}=V_{0}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-\infty,-a_{J})
.
Then, by continuity,
V_{1}=V_{0}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-\infty,-a_{J}]
.
As
(W,J)\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}
, we get
\xi_{J}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(W,J)\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}(W)
.
Also, we have
\Pi_{4}(\xi_{J})=-a_{J}<a_{J}+2=c+1
, so
\xi_{J}\in H
.
It follows that
\xi_{J}\in({\mathcal{U}}(W))\cap H
.
Then, by Claim 2, we conclude that
\xi_{J}\in{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})
.
Let
a:=a_{I_{1}}-a_{J}
.
By Lemma
4.4
(ii), we have

\Phi_{[-a,0]}^{V_{0}}(\xi_{J})\,\,\subseteq\,\,\{(0,0,0)\}\,\times\,[-a_{I_{1}},-a_{J}].

Then
\Phi_{[-a,0]}^{V_{0}}(\xi_{J})\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-\infty,-a_{J}]
.
So, as
V_{1}=V_{0}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-\infty,-a_{J}]
,
by Lemma
6.1
,
we get
\Phi_{-a}^{V_{1}}(\xi_{J})=\Phi_{-a}^{V_{0}}(\xi_{J})
.
By Lemma
4.4
(i), we have
\Phi_{-a}^{V_{0}}(\xi_{J})=\xi_{I_{1}}
.
Then
\Phi_{-a}^{V_{1}}(\xi_{J})=\Phi_{-a}^{V_{0}}(\xi_{J})=\xi_{I_{1}}
.
So, since
\xi_{J}\in{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})
and since
{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})
is
V_{1}
-invariant,
we conclude that
\xi_{I_{1}}\in{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})
.
So, because
\xi_{I_{1}}\in B(I_{1})
and because
(V_{1},I_{1})\in{\mathcal{D}}
,
it follows that

\xi_{I_{1}}\qquad\in\qquad({\mathcal{U}}(V_{1}))\,\,\cap\,\,(B(I_{1}))\qquad=\qquad{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V_{1},I_{1}).

Then
(V_{1},I_{1})\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}
.
So, by Claim 5,

(V_{1},I_{1})\in({\mathcal{D}}_{+})\cap({\mathcal{D}}^{\#})={\mathcal{D}}_{+}^{\#}
.
∎

LEMMA 11.3
.

Let
(W,J)\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}
.
Assume that
W
is porous.
Then there exists
(V_{*},I_{*})\in({\mathcal{M}}_{*}(W,J))\cap({\mathcal{D}}_{*})

such that
V_{*}
is porous.

Proof.

By Lemma
11.2
,
choose
(V_{1},I_{1})\in({\mathcal{M}}_{*}(W,J))\cap({\mathcal{D}}^{\#}_{+})
such that
V_{1}
is porous.
Then
(V_{1},I_{1})\in{\mathcal{D}}_{+}^{\#}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}
.
As
(V_{1},I_{1})\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}
,
we get
\xi_{I_{1}}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V_{1},I_{1})
.
Then
\xi_{I_{1}}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V_{1},I_{1})\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})

and
\xi_{I_{1}}\in B_{\circ}(I_{1})
, so

\xi_{I_{1}}\in({\mathcal{U}}(V_{1}))\cap(B_{\circ}(I_{1}))={\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V_{1},I_{1})
.

Because
(V_{1},I_{1})\in{\mathcal{D}}
,
by Corollary
8.4
(iii),

{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V_{1},I_{1})
is an open subset of
B_{\circ}(I_{1})
.
Define
\Psi:{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V_{1},I_{1})\to(0,\infty)
by
\Psi(\tau)=TF_{I_{1}}^{V_{1}}(\tau)
.
By continuity of
\Psi
at
\xi_{I_{1}}
,
fix an open neighborhood
N_{1}

in
{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V_{1},I_{1})
of
\xi_{I_{1}}
such that,
for all
\tau\in N_{1}
,
|(\Psi(\tau))-(\Psi(\xi_{I_{1}}))|<1
.
Since
\Psi(\xi_{I_{1}})>0
, fix an integer
m\geq 1
such that
|(\Psi(\xi_{I_{1}}))-m|<1
.
Then, by the Triangle Inequality, we have:
for all
\tau\in N_{1}
,
|(\Psi(\tau))-m|<2
.
Define
f:N_{1}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
f(\tau)=m-(\Psi(\tau))+250
.
Then
248<f<252
.

Fix an open neighborhood
N
in
B_{\circ}(I_{1})
of
\xi_{I_{1}}

such that the closure
{\overline{N}}
in
B_{\circ}(I_{1})
of
N

is compact and satisfies
{\overline{N}}\subseteq N_{1}
.
Fix an open neighborhood
N_{0}
in
B_{\circ}(I_{1})
of
\xi_{I_{1}}

such that the closure
{\overline{N}}_{0}
in
B_{\circ}(I_{1})
of
N

satisfies
{\overline{N}}_{0}\subseteq N
.
Then
N_{0}\subseteq N_{1}
.
Fix
g:B_{\circ}(I_{1})\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that

•

(w,x,y)\mapsto g(w,x,y,-a_{I_{1}})\,:\,I_{1}^{3}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is
C^{\infty}
,

•

0\leq g\leq 1
on
B_{\circ}(I_{1})
,

•

g=1
on
{\overline{N}}_{0}
and

•

g=0
on
(B_{\circ}(I_{1}))\backslash N
.

Define
h:B_{\circ}(I_{1})\to{\mathbb{R}}
by:

•

\forall\tau\in N_{1}
,
h(\tau):=[f(\tau)][g(\tau)]+[200][1-(g(\tau))]
and

•

\forall\tau\in(B_{\circ}(I_{1}))\backslash{\overline{N}}
,
h(\tau):=200
.

Then
(w,x,y)\mapsto h(w,x,y,-a_{I_{1}})\,:\,I_{1}^{3}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is
C^{\infty}
, and

h=f
on
{\overline{N}}_{0}
and
200\leq h<252
.

For
s\in[200,380]
, define
V_{\#}^{s}
as in §
10
.
For all
\tau=(w,x,y,z)\in I_{1}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}
,

•

let
\tau_{\#}:=(w,x,y,-a_{I_{1}})\in B_{\circ}(I_{1})
and

•

let
v_{\tau}:=V_{\#}^{h(\tau_{\#})}(z-a_{I_{1}}-101)
.

Then
\tau\mapsto v_{\tau}:I_{1}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is
C^{\infty}
.
By the properties of
V_{\#}^{s}
,
for all
\tau\in I_{1}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}
, we have
1/2\leq v_{\tau}\leq 1
,
i.e.
,
v_{\tau}\in[1/2,1]
.
Let

Q_{1}:=I_{1}^{3}\,\times\,(a_{I_{1}}+1,a_{I_{1}}+5),\quad Q_{2}:=I_{1}^{3}\,\times\,(a_{I_{1}}+197,a_{I_{1}}+201).

For all
\tau=(w,x,y,z)\in Q_{1}\cup Q_{2}
,
we have

z-a_{I_{1}}-101\quad\in\quad(-100,-96)\cup(96,100)\quad\subseteq\quad{\mathbb{R}}\backslash(-96,96),

so
v_{\tau}=1
.
Let
K:=\{(w,x,y)\in I_{1}^{3}\,|\,(w,x,y,-a_{I_{1}})\in{\overline{N}}\,\}
.
Then, because
{\overline{N}}
is compact, it follows that
K
is a compact subset of
I_{1}^{3}
.
Define
{\widehat{I}}:=(a_{I_{1}}+1,a_{I_{1}}+201)
and

Q_{3}:=[(I_{1}^{3})\backslash K]\times{\widehat{I}}
.
Then, for all
\tau\in Q_{3}
, we have
\tau_{\#}\in(B_{\circ}(I_{1}))\backslash{\overline{N}}
,

h(\tau_{\#})=200
, so
v_{\tau}=1
.

Let
A:=I_{1}^{3}\times{\widehat{I}}

and let

be defined by
Y(\tau)=(0,0,0,v_{\tau})
.
Then
Y:A\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
is

and

•

Y(A)\quad\subseteq\quad\{(0,0,0)\}\times[1/2,1]
and

•

Y=V_{0}
on
Q_{1}\cup Q_{2}\cup Q_{3}
.

From the definitions of
A
and
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
and
Q_{3}
, we have both

A\,\,\backslash\,\,(Q_{1}\,\cup\,Q_{2})\qquad=\qquad I_{1}^{3}\quad\times\quad[\,\,a_{I_{1}}+5\,\,,\,\,a_{I_{1}}+197\,\,],

.

.

is a compact subset of

.

, and so

.

.

.

Define

on

on

Then

Recall that

.
Because of this, and because

•

(V_{*},I_{*})\,\,\in\,\,{\mathcal{M}}(V_{1},I_{1})
,

•

V_{1}
is porous     and

(V_{1},I_{1})\,\,\in\,\,{\mathcal{D}}_{+}^{\#}\,\,=\,\,({\mathcal{D}}_{+})\,\cap\,({\mathcal{D}}^{\#})
,

we see, from Lemma
11.1
(ii-v), that

•

{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})={\mathcal{U}}(V_{*})
,

•

V_{*}
is porous     and

•

(V_{*},I_{*})\,\,\in\,\,({\mathcal{D}}_{+})\,\cap\,({\mathcal{D}}^{\#})\,\,=\,\,{\mathcal{D}}_{+}^{\#}
.

It remains to show,

in

of
\xi_{I_{*}}
,

on

.

Let

and let

.

,
we conclude that
\Phi_{[-202,0]}^{V_{0}}(N_{0})\subseteq H
.

on

on

.

on
N_{0}
.
Then

.
So, because

is
V_{1}
-invariant,

\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{1}}(N_{0})\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})

{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})={\mathcal{U}}(V_{*})
.
Then we have

Recall that

.

N_{*}

\xi_{I_{*}}

.

Claim 1:

Proof of Claim 4:

Claim 5:

\omega=(w,x,y,a_{I_{1}}+202)
.

Proof of Claim 5:

.

12.
The hyperbolic vector field
H
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}

Let
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be the coordinate projection
maps defined by
\pi_{1}(w,x)=w
and
\pi_{2}(w,x)=x
.
Let
H_{0}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be defined by

H_{0}(w,x)\,\,=\,\,(d/dt)_{t=0}[(e^{t}w,e^{-t}x)]\,\,=\,\,(w,-x).

Then
H_{0}
is complete and, for all
w,x,t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\Phi_{t}^{H_{0}}(w,x)=(e^{t}w,e^{-t}x)
.

Let
Z:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be the zero function defined by
Z(u)=0
.
Let
c_{0}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a
C^{\infty}
function satisfying

•

0<c_{0}<1
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\backslash\{(0,0)\}
,

•

\forall u\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
[c_{0}(u)]\,[H(u)]\,\,\in\,\,{\overline{I}}_{0}^{2}
and

•

c_{0}
agrees with
Z
to all orders at
(0,0)
.

We define
H:=c_{0}H_{0}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Then
H:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is
C^{\infty}
and
H({\mathbb{R}}^{2})\subseteq{\overline{I}}_{0}^{2}
.
It follows that
H
is complete.

I refer to
H_{0}
and
H
as “hyperbolic”
because any orbit of either is contained in a level set of
the quadratic form
(w,x)\mapsto wx:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,
and because a generic level set of this quadratic form is a hyperbola.

LEMMA 12.1
.

Let
u\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Then there is an increasing
C^{\infty}
diffeomorphism

g:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\Phi_{t}^{H}(u)=\Phi_{g(t)}^{H_{0}}(u)
.

Proof.

If
u=(0,0)
, then, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\Phi_{t}^{H}(u)=(0,0)=\Phi_{t}^{H_{0}}(u)
,
and, in this case, we can define
g:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
g(t)=t
.
We may therefore assume that
u\neq(0,0)
.
Then, because
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\backslash\{(0,0)\}
is
H_{0}
-invariant,
it follows that
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{H_{0}}(u)\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\backslash\{(0,0)\}
.

Define
W:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
W(s)=c_{0}(\Phi_{s}^{H_{0}}(u))
.
Recall that
0<c_{0}<1
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\backslash\{(0,0)\}
.
Then
0<W<1
.
Then
W:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is
C^{\infty}
and bounded, and, therefore, complete.
Define
g:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
g(t)=\Phi_{t}^{W}(0)
.
Then
g:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is
C^{\infty}
and satisfies both
g(0)=0
and,
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
g^{\prime}(t)=W(g(t))
.
So, because
W>0
, we see that
g^{\prime}>0
.
So, by the Mean Value Theorem,
g
is increasing.
Then
g:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is injective.

Define a constant function
V:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by: for all
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
V(s)=1
.
For all
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
W(s)>0
, so
W(s)\neq 0
.
Then, by Corollary
5.3
,
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{W}(0)=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(0)
.
For all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\Phi_{t}^{V}(0)=t
, so
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(0)={\mathbb{R}}
.
Therefore
g({\mathbb{R}})=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{W}(0)=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(0)={\mathbb{R}}
.
Then
g:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is surjective.
Then
g:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is injective and surjective, hence bijective.
So, as
g^{\prime}>0
and as
g:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is
C^{\infty}
,
by the Inverse Function Theorem,
g^{-1}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is
C^{\infty}
.
Then
g:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is an increasing
C^{\infty}
diffeomorphism.
It remains to prove, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, that
\Phi_{g(t)}^{H_{0}}(u)=\Phi_{t}^{H}(u)
.

Define
\gamma:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
by
\gamma(t)=\Phi_{g(t)}^{H_{0}}(u)
.
We wish to show, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, that
\gamma(t)=\Phi_{t}^{H}(u)
.
We have
\gamma(0)=\Phi_{g(0)}^{H_{0}}(u)=\Phi_{0}^{H_{0}}(u)=u=\Phi_{0}^{H}(u)
.
Therefore, by uniqueness of solutions of ODEs,
it suffices to prove, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, that

\gamma^{\prime}(t)=H(\gamma(t))
.

Differentiating the definition of
\gamma
,
and using the Chain Rule, we see, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
that
\gamma^{\prime}(t)=[g^{\prime}(t)][H_{0}(\Phi_{g(t)}^{H_{0}}(u))]
.
Fix
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and let
u_{1}:=\gamma(t)=\Phi_{g(t)}^{H_{0}}(u)
.
We wish to prove that
[g^{\prime}(t)][H_{0}(u_{1})]=H(u_{1})
.

By definition of
W
, we have
W(g(t))=c_{0}(\Phi_{g(t)}^{H_{0}}(u))
.
Then

g^{\prime}(t)\quad=\quad W(\,g(t)\,)\quad=\quad c_{0}(\,\Phi_{g(t)}^{H_{0}}(u)\,)\quad=\quad c_{0}(u_{1}).

Then
[g^{\prime}(t)][H_{0}(u_{1})]=[c_{0}(u_{1})][H_{0}(u_{1})]=(c_{0}H_{0})(u_{1})=H(u_{1})
.
∎

LEMMA 12.2
.

Both of the following are true:

(i)

H({\mathbb{R}}\times\{0\})\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}\times\{0\}
.

(ii)

H(\{0\}\times{\mathbb{R}})\subseteq\{0\}\times{\mathbb{R}}
.

Proof.

We only prove (i). The proof of (ii) is similar.

Let
w\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
We wish to prove that
H(w,0)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times\{0\}
.

Let
a:=c_{0}(w,0)
.
As
H_{0}(w,0)=(w,0)

and
H(w,0)=a\cdot[H_{0}(w,0)]
,
it follows that
H(w,0)=(aw,0)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times\{0\}
, as desired.
∎

LEMMA 12.3
.

All of the following are true:

(i)

For all
u\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\backslash[\{0\}\times{\mathbb{R}}]
,
|\pi_{1}(\Phi_{t}^{H}(u))|\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
.

(ii)

For all
u\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\backslash[{\mathbb{R}}\times\{0\}]
,
|\pi_{2}(\Phi_{t}^{H}(u))|\to\infty
as
t\to-\infty
.

(iii)

For all
u\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
t\mapsto|\pi_{1}(\Phi_{t}^{H}(u))|:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is nondecreasing.

(iv)

For all
u\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
t\mapsto|\pi_{2}(\Phi_{t}^{H}(u))|:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is nonincreasing.

Proof.

We only prove (i) and (iii). The proofs of (ii) and (iv) are similar.

Fix
u=(w,x)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}={\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
We wish to prove both

•

if
w\neq 0
,    then
|\pi_{1}(\Phi_{t}^{H}(u))|\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
and

•

t\,\mapsto\,|\pi_{1}(\Phi_{t}^{H}(u))|\,\,:\,\,{\mathbb{R}}\,\to\,{\mathbb{R}}
is nondecreasing.

By Lemma
12.1
,
let
g:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be an increasing
C^{\infty}
diffeomorphism
such that, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\Phi_{t}^{H}(u)=\Phi_{g(t)}^{H_{0}}(u)
.
For all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have
\pi_{1}(\Phi_{t}^{H}(u))=\pi_{1}(\Phi_{g(t)}^{H_{0}}(u))=e^{g(t)}w
.
We therefore wish to prove both

•

if
w\neq 0
,    then
e^{g(t)}|w|\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
and

•

t\,\mapsto e^{g(t)}|w|\,\,\,:\,\,{\mathbb{R}}\,\to\,{\mathbb{R}}
is nondecreasing.

Because
g:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is an increasing
C^{\infty}
diffeomorphism,
it follows that
g(t)\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
.
So, if
w\neq 0
, then
e^{g(t)}|w|\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
.

Since both
g:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and
t\mapsto e^{t}|w|:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
are nondecreasing,
the composite
t\mapsto e^{g(t)}|w|:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is nondecreasing as well.
∎

LEMMA 12.4
.

Let
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Then the map
\Phi_{t}^{H}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
agrees
with the identity
\hbox{Id}_{2}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
to all orders at
(0,0)
.

Proof.

Define
{\bf 0}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
by: for all
u\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
{\bf 0}(u)=(0,0)
.
Because
c_{0}
agrees with
Z
agree to all orders at
(0,0)
,
because
H=c_{0}H_{0}
and because
{\bf 0}=ZH_{0}
, it follows that

H
agrees with
{\bf 0}
to all orders at
(0,0)
.
Then, by Corollary
6.4
,

\Phi_{t}^{H}
agrees with
\hbox{Id}_{2}
to all orders at
(0,0)
.
∎

13.
The “racetrack” vector field
Q
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}

For this section, let
S:=(4I_{0})\times(12I_{0})
,
let
B:=(4I_{0})\times\{-12\}
and let

T:=(4I_{0})\times\{12\}
.
Let
R
be an open subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
such that

•

R
is diffeomorphic to an open annulus in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and

•

S\,\cup\,B\cup\,T\quad\subseteq\quad R\quad\subseteq\quad(50I_{0})^{2}
.

Let
Q_{0}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be the constant map defined by
Q_{0}(y,z)=(0,1)
.
Let
Q:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be
C^{\infty}
and satisfy

•

Q({\mathbb{R}}^{2})\subseteq{\overline{I}}_{0}^{2}
,

•

R
is
Q
-invariant,

•

Q=Q_{0}
on
S
and

•

Q=Q_{0}
on
[{\mathbb{R}}^{2}]\,\backslash\,[(50I_{0})^{2}]
.

Then
Q
is
C^{\infty}
and bounded, so
Q
is complete.

Fix an integer
m>24
.
Throughout this section, we assume:

•

\forall v\in R
,
\Phi_{m}^{Q}(v)=v
and

•

\forall v\in B
,
\forall t\in(0,m)
,
[\Phi_{t}^{Q}(v)\in S]\Leftrightarrow[t<24]
.

I picture
R
in the shape of a racetrack, with
B
as the starting line,
with
T
as a checkpoint that occurs just before the first turn of the track,
and with
S
as the portion of the track between
B
and
T
.
While in
S
, the runners all travel straight upward in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, with unit speed.
Using seconds as our units of time, there exists an integer
m>24
, such that
every runner takes exactly
m
seconds to complete one lap around the track,
no matter what the runner’s starting point is.
Also, each runner starting on the starting line,
B
, spends exactly
24
seconds in
S
,
and then doesn’t return to
S
in the following
m-24
seconds.

LEMMA 13.1
.

Let
v=(y,z)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}={\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Assume that
v\in S
.
Then, all of the following are true:

(i)

\forall t\in[-12,12]
,
\Phi_{t-z}^{Q}(v)=\Phi_{t-z}^{Q_{0}}(v)=(y,t)
.

(ii)

\forall t\in[12,m-12]
,
\Phi_{t-z}^{Q}(v)\in({\mathbb{R}}^{2})\backslash S
.

(iii)

\forall t\in[m-12,m+12]
,
\Phi_{t-z}^{Q}(v)=\Phi_{t-m-z}^{Q_{0}}(v)=(y,t-m)
.

Proof.

Since
(y,z)=v\in S=(4I_{0})\times(12I_{0})
, we get
y\in 4I_{0}
and
z\in 12I_{0}
.
That is,
-4<y<4
and
-12<z<12
.

Proof of (i):

For all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\Phi_{t-z}^{Q_{0}}(v)=(y,t)
.
From this and continuity, we need only show, for all
t\in(-12,12)
, that
\Phi_{t-z}^{Q}(v)=\Phi_{t-z}^{Q_{0}}(v)
.

We have
-12-z<0<12-z
,
i.e.
,
0\in(-12-z,12-z)
.
For all
t\in(-12-z,12-z)
, we have
t+z\in(-12,12)=12I_{0}
, and so

\Phi_{t}^{Q_{0}}(v)\quad=\quad(y,t+z)\quad\in\quad(4I_{0})\,\times\,(12I_{0})\quad\subseteq\quad S.

So, because
Q=Q_{0}
on
S
,
we conclude, from Lemma
6.1
,
that, for all
t\in(-12-z,12-z)
,
we have
\Phi_{t}^{Q}(v)=\Phi_{t}^{Q_{0}}(v)
.
Equivalently, for all
t\in(-12,12)
,
we have
\Phi_{t-z}^{Q}(v)=\Phi_{t-z}^{Q_{0}}(v)
.

End of proof of (i).

Proof of (ii):

Let
v_{0}:=\Phi_{-12-z}^{Q}(v)
.
Then, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\Phi_{t+12}^{Q}(v_{0})=\Phi_{t-z}^{Q}(v)
.
By (i), we have
v_{0}=(y,-12)
.
It follows that
v_{0}=(y,-12)\in(4I_{0})\times\{-12\}=B
.
So, by definition of
m
, we see, for all
t\in(0,m)
, that:
[\Phi_{t}^{Q}(v_{0})\in S]\Leftrightarrow[t<24]
.

So, for all
t\in[24,m)
, we have

\Phi_{t}^{Q}(v_{0})\in({\mathbb{R}}^{2})\backslash S
.
So, since
({\mathbb{R}}^{2})\backslash S
is closed,
by continuity, for all
t\in[24,m]
, we get

\Phi_{t}^{Q}(v_{0})\in({\mathbb{R}}^{2})\backslash S
.
Equivalently, for all
t\in[12,m-12]
,
we have
\Phi_{t+12}^{Q}(v_{0})\in({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash S
,
so
\Phi_{t-z}^{Q}(v)=\Phi_{t+12}^{Q}(v_{0})\in({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash S
, as desired.

End of proof of (ii).

Proof of (iii):

Fix
t\in[m-12,m+12]
.
Since
\Phi_{t-m-z}^{Q_{0}}(v)=(y,t-m)
,
it suffices to show that
\Phi_{t-z}^{Q}(v)=\Phi_{t-m-z}^{Q_{0}}(v)
.

Since
t-m\in[-12,12]
, by (i), we see that
\Phi_{t-m-z}^{Q}(v)=\Phi_{t-m-z}^{Q_{0}}(v)
.
By definition of
m
, because
v\in S\subseteq R
, we see that
\Phi_{m}^{Q}(v)=v
.
Applying
\Phi_{t-m-z}^{Q}
to this equation gives us
\Phi_{t-z}^{Q}(v)=\Phi_{t-m-z}^{Q}(v)
.
Then
\Phi_{t-z}^{Q}(v)=\Phi_{t-m-z}^{Q}(v)=\Phi_{t-m-z}^{Q_{0}}(v)
, as desired.

End of proof of (iii).

∎

LEMMA 13.2
.

Let
a\in(0,4]
.
Let
I:=(-a,a)
.
Let
y\in I
,
z\in 12I_{0}
.
Let
v:=(y,z)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Then, for all
t\in[a,m-a]
,

\Phi_{t-z}^{Q}(v)\in[{\mathbb{R}}^{2}]\backslash[I^{2}]
.

Proof.

Since
0<a\leq 4
, we have
I\subseteq 4I_{0}
and
[a,12]\subseteq[-12,12]

and
[m-12,m-a]\subseteq[m-12,m+12]
.
Since
y\in I\subseteq 4I_{0}
and
z\in 12I_{0}
,
we see that
v=(y,z)\in(4I_{0})\times(12I_{0})=S
.
Also, because
I\subseteq 4I_{0}\subseteq 12I_{0}
,
we get
I^{2}\subseteq(4I_{0})\times(12I_{0})=S
, so

({\mathbb{R}}^{2})\backslash S\subseteq[{\mathbb{R}}^{2}]\backslash[I^{2}]
.

For all
t\in[a,12]
, we have
t\geq a
, so
t\notin I
, so
(y,t)\in[{\mathbb{R}}^{2}]\backslash[I^{2}]
.
For all
t\in[m-12,m-a]
, we have
t-m\leq-a
, so
t-m\notin I
, so
(y,t-m)\in[{\mathbb{R}}^{2}]\backslash[I^{2}]
.

Combining all these observations with Lemma
13.1
, we conclude:

(a)

\forall t\in[a,12]
,
\Phi_{t-z}^{Q}(v)=(y,t)\in[{\mathbb{R}}^{2}]\backslash[I^{2}]
.

(b)

\forall t\in[12,m-12]
,

\Phi_{t-z}^{Q}(v)\in({\mathbb{R}}^{2})\backslash S\subseteq[{\mathbb{R}}^{2}]\backslash[I^{2}]
.

(c)

\forall t\in[m-12,m-a]
,

\Phi_{t-z}^{Q}(v)=(y,t-m)\in[{\mathbb{R}}^{2}]\backslash[I^{2}]
.

Since
[a,m-a]=[a,12]\cup[12,m-12]\cup[m-12,m-a]
, by (a) and (b) and (c),
we see, for all
t\in[a,m-a]
, that
\Phi_{t-z}^{Q}(v)\in[{\mathbb{R}}^{2}]\backslash[I^{2}]
.
∎

We record the special cases
a=1
and
a=4
of Lemma
13.2
:

COROLLARY 13.3
.

Let
y\in I_{0}
,
z\in 12I_{0}
.
Let
v:=(y,z)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Then, for all
t\in[1,m-1]
, we have

\Phi_{t-z}^{Q}(v)\in[{\mathbb{R}}^{2}]\backslash[I_{0}^{2}]
.

COROLLARY 13.4
.

Let
y\in 4I_{0}
,
z\in 12I_{0}
.
Let
v:=(y,z)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Then, for all
t\in[4,m-4]
, we have

\Phi_{t-z}^{Q}(v)\in[{\mathbb{R}}^{2}]\backslash[(4I_{0})^{2}]
.

14.
Porousness and
{\mathcal{P}}_{I_{0}}

Recall, from §
2
, that
I_{0}=(-1,1)\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
.
The notation
{\mathcal{P}}_{I}
(for any
I\in{\mathcal{I}}
) is also defined in §
2
.
The main results of this section are Lemma
14.17
and Lemma
14.21
,
which, together, show that there exists
(P_{0},K_{0})\in{\mathcal{P}}_{I_{0}}

such that
P_{0}
is porous.

From the perspective of dynamical systems,
porousness is generic properness, while elements of
{\mathcal{P}}_{I_{0}}
display strong periodicity.
We are therefore interested in vector fields that combine properness and periodicity.
These two dynamical properties are in opposition to one another,
so their conflation is a challenge.
A water pump is metaphor for this challenge, because the handle of the pump
(or whatever mechanism gives power to the pump) moves in a periodic manner,
while the water flow is proper in the sense that the water moves a great distance.

Let
H
be the hyperbolic vector field of §
12
.
Let
R
be the racetrack of §
13
,
and let
Q
be the racetrack vector field of §
13
.
A generic orbit of
H
is proper, while
R
is a nonempty open set of
Q
-periodic orbits.
We will blend
H
and
Q
together in a number of ways.
For example, we can simply form the product,
P
, of
H
and
Q
.
That is, we can define a function
P:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by:   for all
\rho\,=\,(u,v)\,\in\,{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,=\,{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,

P(\rho)\,\,:=\,\,(\,H(u)\,,\,Q(v)\,)\,\,\in\,\,{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,\times\,{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,\,=\,\,{\mathbb{R}}^{4}.

The flow of the vector field represented by
P
can be described as a water pump, as follows.
The runners, driven by
Q
, go around racetrack
R
.
Their motion powers the pump, and
the water flows out along the hyperbolas that are the orbits of
H
.
The maps
P_{*}
,
P_{+}
and
P_{0}
, defined below, are variants of
P
.
They also fit into the water pump metaphor,
and one can invent stories to help visualize them.
For example, with
P_{*}
, the pump has a defect:
While the runners are inside
(3I_{0})^{2}
, the water flow stops,
but, when the runners are outside
(4I_{0})^{2}
,
the pump works at full strength.
Because the runners spend less than half of their time in
(4I_{0})^{2}
,
the problem is intermittent, and the water still flows, albeit with occasional delays.
With the pump defined by
P_{+}
, the defect just described still exists,
but, in addition, there’s another twist:
Once the water has flowed far enough,
the runners abandon the racetrack and
start running straight upward in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
at unit speed.
With
P_{0}
there is yet one more feature in the dynamics:
After the runners leave the racetrack,
the water eventually ceases to flow.
We now present details.

Recall that
R\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is the racetrack of §
13
.
As in §
13
, let

S:=(4I_{0})\times(12I_{0})\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\quad\hbox{and}\quad B:=(4I_{0})\times\{-12\}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}.

Recall, from §
2
, the definition of
\zeta_{I}
(for any
I\in{\mathcal{I}}
).
Define
\alpha:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

\alpha(y,z)=1-[\zeta_{3I_{0}}(y)][\zeta_{3I_{0}}(z)]
.
Then

•

0\leq\alpha\leq 1
,

•

\alpha=0
on
(\,\overline{3I_{0}}\,)^{2}
and

•

\alpha=1
on
[{\mathbb{R}}^{2}]\backslash[(4I_{0})^{2}]
.

Define
P_{*}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by:   for all
\rho\,=\,(u,v)\,\in\,{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,=\,{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,

P_{*}(\rho)\,\,:=\,\,(\,[\alpha(v)][H(u)]\,,\,Q(v)\,)\,\,\in\,\,{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,\,=\,\,{\mathbb{R}}^{4}.

Then
P_{*}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
is
C^{\infty}
and
P_{*}({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\subseteq{\overline{I}}_{0}^{4}
.
Then
P_{*}
is complete.

Fix
C^{\infty}
maps
\beta,\gamma:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that

•

0\leq\beta\leq 1
and
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
,

•

\beta=1
on
(\,\overline{100I_{0}}\,)^{4}
and
\beta=0
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\,\,\backslash\,\,[(200I_{0})^{4}]
and

•

\gamma=1
on
(\,\overline{300I_{0}}\,)^{4}
and
\gamma=0
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\,\,\backslash\,\,[(400I_{0})^{4}]
.

Let
J_{0}:=200I_{0}
and let
K_{0}:=400I_{0}
.
Then
\beta=0
on
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(J_{0}^{4})
and
\beta=\gamma=0
on
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(K_{0}^{4})
.
Also,
\beta=\gamma=1
on
(\,\overline{100I_{0}}\,)^{4}
.

Let
{\bf 0}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be the zero map defined by
{\bf 0}(w,x)=(0,0)
.
Let
Q_{0}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be the constant map defined by
Q_{0}(y,z)=(0,1)
.
Note that
V_{0}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
satisfies:   for all
\rho\,=\,(u,v)\,\in\,{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,=\,{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,

V_{0}(\rho)\quad=\quad(\,{\bf 0}(u)\,,\,Q_{0}(v)\,)\quad\in\quad{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,\times\,{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\quad=\quad{\mathbb{R}}^{4}.

Define
X:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by:   for all
\rho\,=\,(u,v)\,\in\,{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,=\,{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,

X(\rho)\quad:=\quad(\,H(u)\,,\,Q_{0}(v)\,)\quad\in\quad{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,\times\,{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\quad=\quad{\mathbb{R}}^{4}.

Then
X:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
is
C^{\infty}
and
X({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\subseteq{\overline{I}}_{0}^{4}
.
Let
P_{+}:=\beta P_{*}+(1-\beta)X
.
Then
P_{+}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
is
C^{\infty}
and
P_{+}({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\subseteq{\overline{I}}_{0}^{4}
.
Let
P_{0}:=\gamma P_{+}+(1-\gamma)V_{0}
.
Then
P_{0}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
is
C^{\infty}
and
P_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\subseteq{\overline{I}}_{0}^{4}
.
Then
P_{0}
is complete.

Define a
C^{\infty}
map
\omega:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by:
for all
\rho=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,

\omega(\rho)\quad:=\quad[\,\gamma(\rho)\,]\,\,[\,(\alpha(v))(\beta(\rho))\,+\,1\,-\,(\beta(\rho))\,].

For all
\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, we define
\Omega_{\rho}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

\displaystyle{\Omega_{\rho}(t)\>=\int_{0}^{t}\,\left[\omega\left(\Phi_{r}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\right)\right]\,dr}
.

Following the construction in §
13
,
fix an integer
m>24
such that

•

\forall v\in R
,
\Phi_{m}^{Q}(v)=v
and

•

\forall v\in B
,
\forall t\in(0,m)
,
[\Phi_{t}^{Q}(v)\in S]\Leftrightarrow[t<24]
.

Let
\displaystyle{T_{\times}:=m-\left[\int_{-4}^{4}\,(\zeta_{3I_{0}}(t))\,dt\right]}
.

Let
Z_{0}:=(\{0\}\times{\mathbb{R}})\cup({\mathbb{R}}\times\{0\})\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Let
Z:=Z_{0}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Then
Z_{0}
is nowhere dense in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, and so
Z
is nowhere dense in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.

Define
\Pi_{12}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and
\Pi_{34}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
by

\Pi_{12}(w,x,y,z)\,\,=\,\,(w,x)\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\Pi_{34}(w,x,y,z)\,\,=\,\,(y,z).

LEMMA 14.1
.

Let
\sigma=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Then

(i)

\omega(\sigma)\quad\geq\quad 0
and

(ii)

\Pi_{12}(P_{0}(\sigma))\quad=\quad[\omega(\sigma)]\,\,[H(u)]
.

Proof.

Let
p:=\alpha(v)
and
q:=\beta(\sigma)
and
r:=\gamma(\sigma)
.
Then
p,q,r\in[0,1]
.
Let
s:=pq+1-q
.
Since
pq\geq 0
and
1-q\geq 0
, we get
s\geq 0
.
By definition of
\omega
, we have
\omega(\sigma)=r\cdot[pq+1-q]
.
That is,
\omega(\sigma)=rs
.
So, as
r\geq 0
and
s\geq 0
, we get
\omega(\sigma)\geq 0
,
which proves (i).

As
\Pi_{12}(P_{*}(\sigma))=p\cdot[H(u)]

and
\Pi_{12}(X(\sigma))=1\cdot[H(u)]
, we get

\Pi_{12}(P_{+}(\sigma))=[qp+(1-q)(1)][H(u)]=[pq+1-q][H(u)]=s\cdot[H(u)]
.
Therefore, because
\Pi_{12}(V_{0}(\sigma))={\bf 0}(u)=0\cdot[H(u)]
,
it follows that
\Pi_{12}(P_{0}(\sigma))=[rs+(1-r)(0)][H(u)]=[rs][H(u)]=[\omega(\sigma)][H(u)]
,
which proves (ii).
∎

LEMMA 14.2
.

Let
\rho=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Define
f:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
f(t)=\Omega_{\rho}(t)
.
Then all of the following are true:

(i)

f(0)=0
.

(ii)

For all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have
f^{\prime}(t)=\omega(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))\geq 0
.

(iii)

f:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is nondecreasing.

(iv)

For all
t\geq 0
, we have
f(t)\geq 0
.

(v)

For all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have
\Pi_{12}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))=\Phi_{f(t)}^{H}(u)
.

Proof.

For all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\displaystyle{f(t)=\Omega_{\rho}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\,\left[\omega\left(\Phi_{r}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\right)\right]\,dr}
,
so
f(0)=0
, and, by the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus,
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have
f^{\prime}(t)=\omega(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))
;
also, by Lemma
14.1
(i),
\omega(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))\geq 0
.
This proves (i) and (ii).
By (ii),
f^{\prime}\geq 0
,
so, by the Mean Value Theorem,
f:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is nondecreasing,
which proves (iii).
Next, (iv) follows from (i) and (iii).
It remains to prove (v).
Define
\theta,\kappa:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
by

\theta(t)\,\,=\,\,\Pi_{12}(\,\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\,)\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\kappa(t)\,\,=\,\,\Phi_{f(t)}^{H}(u)
.

We wish to show, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, that

\theta(t)=\kappa(t)
.

For all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, define
V_{t}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
by
V_{t}(u)=[f^{\prime}(t)][H(u)]
.
Differentiating the definition of
\kappa
,
and using the Chain Rule, we conclude, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, that

\kappa^{\prime}(t)=[f^{\prime}(t)][H(\Phi_{f(t)}^{H}(u))]
.
Then, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have
\kappa^{\prime}(t)=[f^{\prime}(t)][H(\kappa(t))]=V_{t}(\kappa(t))
.
By (i), we have
f(0)=0
.
Then
\kappa(0)=u=\Pi_{12}(\rho)=\theta(0)
.
So, by uniqueness of solutions of (time-dependent) ODEs,
it suffices to show, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, that

\theta^{\prime}(t)=V_{t}(\theta(t))
.

Differentiating the definition of
\theta
,
and using linearity of
\Pi_{12}
, we see, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, that

\theta^{\prime}(t)=\Pi_{12}(P_{0}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho)))
.
Fix
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and let
\rho_{1}:=\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho)
.
We wish to show that
\Pi_{12}(P_{0}(\rho_{1}))=V_{t}(\theta(t))
.

Choose
u_{1},v_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
such that
\rho_{1}=(u_{1},v_{1})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
By Lemma
14.1
(ii),

\Pi_{12}(P_{0}(\rho_{1}))=[\omega(\rho_{1})][H(u_{1})]
.
By definition of
\theta
,
we have
\theta(t)=\Pi_{12}(\rho_{1})
,
so
\theta(t)=u_{1}
.
By definition of
V_{t}
, we see that
V_{t}(u_{1})=[f^{\prime}(t)][H(u_{1})]
.
By (ii), we have
f^{\prime}(t)=\omega(\rho_{1})
.
Then
\Pi_{12}(P_{0}(\rho_{1}))\!=\![\omega(\rho_{1})][H(u_{1})]\!=\![f^{\prime}(t)][H(u_{1})]\!=\!V_{t}(u_{1})\!=\!V_{t}(\theta(t))
.
∎

LEMMA 14.3
.

Let
\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Then

(i)

t\mapsto|\Pi_{1}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))|:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is nondecreasing   and

(ii)

t\mapsto|\Pi_{2}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))|:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is nonincreasing.

Proof.

We will only prove (i). The proof of (ii) is similar.

Choose
u,v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
such that
\rho=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Define
f:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
f(t)=\Omega_{\rho}(t)
.
By Lemma
14.2
(iii),
f:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is nondecreasing.
Let
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be the coordinate projection maps
defined by
\pi_{1}(w,x)=w
and
\pi_{2}(w,x)=x
.
By Lemma
14.2
(v),
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\Pi_{12}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))=\Phi_{f(t)}^{H}(u)
,
so
\Pi_{1}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))\,\,=\,\,\pi_{1}(\Phi_{f(t)}^{H}(u))
.
We therefore wish to prove:
t\mapsto|\pi_{1}(\Phi_{f(t)}^{H}(u))|:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is nondecreasing.

By Lemma
12.3
(iii),
we know that
t\mapsto|\pi_{1}(\Phi_{t}^{H}(u))|:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is nondecreasing.
So, since
f:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is also nondecreasing,
the composite
t\mapsto|\pi_{1}(\Phi_{f(t)}^{H}(u))|:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}

is nondecreasing, as desired.
∎

LEMMA 14.4
.

Let
\rho=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Then, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have
\Pi_{34}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{*}}(\rho))=\Phi_{t}^{Q}(v)
.

Proof.

Define
\lambda:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}

by
\lambda(t)=\Pi_{34}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{*}}(\rho))
.
We wish to show, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, that
\lambda(t)=\Phi_{t}^{Q}(v)
.
As
\lambda(0)=\Pi_{34}(\rho)=v=\Phi_{0}^{Q}(v)
,
by uniqueness of solutions of ODEs, it suffices to prove,
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, that
\lambda^{\prime}(t)=Q(\lambda(t))
.
Differentiating the definition of
\lambda
, and using linearity of
\Pi_{34}
,
we get: for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\lambda^{\prime}(t)=\Pi_{34}(P_{*}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{*}}(\rho)))
.
Fix
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and let
\rho_{1}:=\Phi_{t}^{P_{*}}(\rho)
.
We wish to prove that
\Pi_{34}(P_{*}(\rho_{1}))=Q(\lambda(t))
.

Fix
u_{1},v_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
such that
\rho_{1}=(u_{1},v_{1})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
By definition of
\lambda
, we have
\lambda(t)=\Pi_{34}(\rho_{1})
, so
\lambda(t)=v_{1}
.
By definition of
P_{*}
, we have
\Pi_{34}(P_{*}(\rho_{1}))=Q(v_{1})
.
Then
\Pi_{34}(P_{*}(\rho_{1}))=Q(v_{1})=Q(\lambda(t))
.
∎

LEMMA 14.5
.

All of the following are true.

(i)

P_{*}=P_{0}
on
(\,\overline{100I_{0}}\,)^{4}
.

(ii)

P_{0}=V_{0}
on
(\,\overline{3I_{0}}\,)^{4}
.

(iii)

\omega=\alpha\circ\Pi_{34}
on
(\,\overline{100I_{0}}\,)^{4}
.

(iv)

\omega=1
on
[J_{0}^{4}]\,\backslash\,[J_{0}^{2}\times(4I_{0})^{2}]
.

(v)

\forall\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times S
,
\Pi_{34}(P_{0}(\rho))=(0,1)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

(vi)

\forall\rho\in[{\mathbb{R}}^{4}]\,\backslash\,[(200I_{0})^{2}\times(50I_{0})^{2}]
,

\Pi_{34}(P_{0}(\rho))=(0,1)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

(vii)

Z
is
P_{0}
-invariant.

(viii)

{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times R
is
P_{*}
-invariant.

Proof.

Let
w:=(0,1)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
For all
v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, we have
Q_{0}(v)=w
.
So, for all
\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, we have
\Pi_{34}(V_{0}(\rho))=\Pi_{34}(X(\rho))=w

and, moreover, by the construction of
Q
in §
13
, we have both

[\,\,\forall v\in S,\,\,Q(v)=w\,\,]\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad[\,\,\forall v\in[{\mathbb{R}}^{2}]\backslash[(50I_{0})^{2}],\,\,Q(v)=w\,\,].

By definition of
P_{*}
, for all
\rho=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,
we have

P_{*}(\rho)\,\,=\,\,(\,[\alpha(v)][H(u)]\,,\,Q(v)\,)\,\,\in\,\,{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,\,=\,\,{\mathbb{R}}^{4}.

so
\Pi_{34}(P_{*}(\rho))=Q(v)
.

Proof of (i):

On
(\,\overline{100I_{0}}\,)^{4}\,
,
because
\beta=1
and
\gamma=1
, we get
P_{*}=P_{+}
and
P_{+}=P_{0}
.
So, on
(\,\overline{100I_{0}}\,)^{4}\,
,

P_{*}=P_{+}=P_{0}
.

End of proof of (i).

Proof of (ii):

Fix
\rho=(u,v)\in(\,\overline{3I_{0}}\,)^{2}\times(\,\overline{3I_{0}}\,)^{2}=(\,\overline{3I_{0}}\,)^{4}
.
We wish to show that
P_{0}(\rho)=(0,0,0,1)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.

As
v\in(\,\overline{3I_{0}}\,)^{2}
,
we have
\alpha(v)=0
.
As
v\in(\,\overline{3I_{0}}\,)^{2}\subseteq(4I_{0})\times(12I_{0})=S
,
we see that
Q(v)=w
.
As
\rho\in(\,\overline{3I_{0}}\,)^{4}\subseteq(\,\overline{100I_{0}}\,)^{4}
,
by (i),
P_{*}(\rho)=P_{0}(\rho)
.
Therefore
P_{0}(\rho)=P_{*}(\rho)=([\alpha(v)][H(u)],Q(v))=(0\cdot[H(u)],w)
,
and so
P_{0}(\rho)=(0,0,0,1)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, as desired.

End of proof of (ii).

Proof of (iii):

Let
\rho=(u,v)\in(\,\overline{100I_{0}}\,)^{2}\times(\,\overline{100I_{0}}\,)^{2}=(\,\overline{100I_{0}}\,)^{4}
.
We wish to show that
\omega(\rho)=\alpha(\Pi_{34}(\rho))
,

i.e.
, that
\omega(\rho)=\alpha(v)
.

As
\rho\in(\,\overline{100I_{0}}\,)^{4}
,
we get
\beta(\rho)=\gamma(\rho)=1
.
Then, by definition of
\omega
,

\omega(\rho)=[1][(\alpha(v))(1)+1-(1)]=\alpha(v)
, as desired.

End of proof of (iii).

Proof of (iv):

Let
\rho=(u,v)\in J_{0}^{2}\times J_{0}^{2}=J_{0}^{4}

and assume that
v\notin(4I_{0})^{2}
.
We wish to prove that
\omega(\rho)=1
.

Since
v\in[{\mathbb{R}}^{2}]\backslash[(4I_{0})^{2}]
, we have
\alpha(v)=1
.
Since
\rho\in J_{0}^{4}\subseteq(\,\overline{300I_{0}}\,)^{4}
, we have
\gamma(\rho)=1
.
Then, from the definition of
\omega
, we conclude that

\omega(\rho)=[1][(1)(\beta(\rho))+1-(\beta(\rho))]=1
, as desired.

End of proof of (iv).

Proof of (v):

Fix
u\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and
v\in S
.
Let
\rho=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
We wish to show that
\Pi_{34}(P_{0}(\rho))=w
.

Because
v\in S
, we get
Q(v)=w
.
Then
\Pi_{34}(P_{*}(\rho))=Q(v)=w
.
So, since we have both
P_{+}=\beta P_{*}+(1-\beta)X
and
\Pi_{34}(X(\rho))=w
,
we see that
\Pi_{34}(P_{+}(\rho))=w
.
So, since we have both
P_{0}=\gamma P_{+}+(1-\gamma)V_{0}
and
\Pi_{34}(V_{0}(\rho))=w
,
we see that
\Pi_{34}(P_{0}(\rho))=w
.

End of proof of (v).

Proof of (vi):

Fix
\rho=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{4}

and assume that
\rho\notin(200I_{0})^{2}\times(50I_{0})^{2}
.
We wish to prove that
\Pi_{34}(P_{0}(\rho))=w
.
We have
\Pi_{34}(V_{0}(\rho))=w
,
so, because
P_{0}=\gamma P_{+}+(1-\gamma)V_{0}
,
it suffices to prove that
\Pi_{34}(P_{+}(\rho))=w
.

If
u\notin J_{0}^{2}
,
then
\rho=(u,v)\notin J_{0}^{4}
, and so
\beta(\rho)=0
, and so,
since
P_{+}=\beta P_{*}+(1-\beta)X
, we conclude that
P_{+}(\rho)=X(\rho)
,
which yields
\Pi_{34}(P_{+}(\rho))=\Pi_{34}(X(\rho))=w
,
as desired.
We may therefore assume
u\in J_{0}^{2}
,
i.e.
,
u\in(200I_{0})^{2}
.
So, since
(u,v)=\rho\notin(200I_{0})^{2}\times(50I_{0})^{2}
, we get
v\notin(50I_{0})^{2}
.
Then
Q(v)=w
.
Then
\Pi_{34}(P_{*}(\rho))=Q(v)=w
.
So, since we have both
P_{+}=\beta P_{*}+(1-\beta)X
and
\Pi_{34}(X(\rho))=w
,
we see that
\Pi_{34}(P_{+}(\rho))=w
, as desired.

End of proof of (vi).

Let
Z^{\prime}:={\mathbb{R}}\times\{0\}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{4}

and let
Z^{\prime\prime}:=\{0\}\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.

Claim 1:

P_{0}(Z^{\prime})\subseteq Z^{\prime}
.

Proof of Claim 1:

Fix
\rho\in Z^{\prime}
.
We wish to prove that
P_{0}(\rho)\in Z^{\prime}
.

Fix
u,v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
such that
\rho=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Let
c:=\omega(\rho)
.
By Lemma
14.1
(ii),

\Pi_{12}(P_{0}(\rho))=c\cdot[H(u)]
.
Since
\rho\in Z^{\prime}
, it follows that
u\in{\mathbb{R}}\times\{0\}
.
Then, by Lemma
12.2
(i),
we have
H(u)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times\{0\}
.
Then
\Pi_{12}(P_{0}(\rho))=c\cdot[H(u)]\in{\mathbb{R}}\times\{0\}
,
so
P_{0}(\rho)\in\Pi_{12}^{-1}({\mathbb{R}}\times\{0\})=Z^{\prime}
, as desired.

End of proof of Claim 1.

Claim 2:

P_{0}(Z^{\prime\prime})\subseteq Z^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof of Claim 2:

Similiar to the proof of Claim 1.

End of proof of Claim 2.

Proof of (vii):

Both
Z^{\prime}
and
Z^{\prime\prime}
are vector subspaces of
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
So, by Claim 1,
Z^{\prime}
is
P_{0}
-invariant,
and, by Claim 2,
Z^{\prime\prime}
is
P_{0}
-invariant.
So, since
Z=(Z^{\prime})\cup(Z^{\prime\prime})
,

Z
is
P_{0}
-invariant as well.

End of proof of (vii).

Proof of (viii):

Fix
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
We wish to prove
\Phi_{t}^{P_{*}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times R)\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times R
.
Fix
\rho=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, assume that
v\in R

and let
\rho_{1}:=\Phi_{t}^{P_{*}}(\rho)
.
We wish to show that
\rho_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times R
,

i.e.
, that
\Pi_{34}(\rho_{1})\in R
.

By the construction of
Q
in §
13
,
R
is
Q
-invariant.
So, as
v\in R
, we get
\Phi_{t}^{Q}(v)\in R
.
By Lemma
14.4
,
we have
\Pi_{34}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{*}}(\rho))=\Phi_{t}^{Q}(v)
.
Then
\Pi_{34}(\rho_{1})=\Pi_{34}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{*}}(\rho))=\Phi_{t}^{Q}(v)\in R
.

End of proof of (viii).

∎

LEMMA 14.6
.

Let
\rho=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Let
J\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
be an interval.
Assume that
0\in J
and that
\Phi_{J}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq(\,\overline{100I_{0}}\,)^{4}
.
Then, for all
t\in J
, we have

\Pi_{34}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))=\Phi_{t}^{Q}(v)
.

Proof.

Fix
t\in J
.
By Lemma
14.4
, we have

\Pi_{34}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{*}}(\rho))=\Phi_{t}^{Q}(v)
,
so it suffices to show that
\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho)=\Phi_{t}^{P_{*}}(\rho)
.

By Lemma
14.5
(i),
we have
P_{*}=P_{0}
on
(\,\overline{100I_{0}}\,)^{4}\,
.
So, since
0\in J
and
\Phi_{J}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq(\,\overline{100I_{0}}\,)^{4}
,
by Lemma
6.1
,
we get
\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho)=\Phi_{t}^{P_{*}}(\rho)
.
∎

LEMMA 14.7
.

Let
\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
. Then both of the following are true:

(i)

Say
\Pi_{4}(\rho)\geq a_{J_{0}}
.
Then, for all
t\geq 0
,
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))\geq a_{J_{0}}
.

(ii)

Say
\Pi_{4}(\rho)\leq-a_{J_{0}}
.
Then, for all
t\leq 0
,
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))\leq-a_{J_{0}}
.

Proof.

We will only prove (i). The proof of (ii) is similar.

Define
\lambda:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
\lambda(t)=\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))
.
Fix
r\in[0,\infty)
and assume, for a contradiction, that
\lambda(r)<a_{J_{0}}
.

Recall that
J_{0}=200I_{0}
, so
a_{J_{0}}=200a_{I_{0}}=200
.
Choose
b\geq 50
such that
\lambda(r)<b<a_{J_{0}}
.
Let
A:=\{t\in[0,\infty)|\lambda(t)\geq b\}
.
Since
\lambda(r)<b
, we have
r\notin A
.
Let
t_{0}:=\inf[0,\infty)\backslash A
.
Then
[0,t_{0})\subseteq A
.
Moreover, for all
\delta>0
,
we have
[t_{0},t_{0}+\delta)\not\subseteq A
.
Let
\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\ldots
be a sequence in
(0,\infty)

such that
\delta_{j}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
.
For all integers
j\geq 1
, choose
u_{j}\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\delta_{j})

such that
u_{j}\notin A
.
Then
u_{j}\to t_{0}
as
j\to\infty
, and, for all integers
j\geq 1
,
we have
\lambda(u_{j})<b
.
Taking the limit as
j\to\infty
,
and using continuity of
\lambda
,
we conclude that
\lambda(t_{0})\leq b
.
By definition of
\lambda
,
\lambda(0)=\Pi_{4}(\rho)
.
By assumption,
a_{J_{0}}\leq\Pi_{4}(\rho)
.
Then
\lambda(t_{0})\leq b<a_{J_{0}}\leq\Pi_{4}(\rho)=\lambda(0)
,
so
\lambda(t_{0})<\lambda(0)
.
Then
t_{0}\neq 0
.
So, since
t_{0}\in[0,\infty)
, we conclude that
t_{0}>0
.
Then, by the Mean Value Theorem, fix
t_{1}\in(0,t_{0})
such that

\lambda^{\prime}(t_{1})=[(\lambda(t_{0}))-(\lambda(0))]/t_{0}
.
From this, because
\lambda(t_{0})<\lambda(0)
and because
t_{0}>0
,
it follows that
\lambda^{\prime}(t_{1})<0
.

Because
t_{1}\in(0,t_{0})\subseteq[0,t_{0})\subseteq A
,
we conclude that
\lambda(t_{1})\geq b
.
We define
\rho_{1}:=\Phi_{t_{1}}^{P_{0}}(\rho)
.
Then, by definition of
\lambda
, we have
\lambda(t_{1})=\Pi_{4}(\rho_{1})
.
Therefore
\Pi_{4}(\rho_{1})=\lambda(t_{1})\geq b\geq 50
,
and so
\Pi_{4}(\rho_{1})\notin 50I_{0}
,
and so
\rho_{1}\in[{\mathbb{R}}^{4}]\backslash[(200I_{0})^{2}\times(50I_{0})^{2}]
.
Then, by Lemma
14.5
(vi),
we see that
\Pi_{34}(P_{0}(\rho_{1}))=(0,1)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, and so
\Pi_{4}(P_{0}(\rho_{1}))=1
.

Differentiating the definition of
\lambda
, and using linearity of
\Pi_{4}
,
we see, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, that
\lambda^{\prime}(t)=\Pi_{4}(P_{0}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho)))
.
Then
\lambda^{\prime}(t_{1})=\Pi_{4}(P_{0}(\rho_{1}))
.
Then
1=\Pi_{4}(P_{0}(\rho_{1}))=\lambda^{\prime}(t_{1})<0
, contradiction.
∎

COROLLARY 14.8
.

Let
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
. Then both of the following are true:

(i)

Say
\Pi_{4}(\sigma)>-a_{J_{0}}
.
Then, for all
t\geq 0
,
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\sigma))>-a_{J_{0}}
.

(ii)

Say
\Pi_{4}(\sigma)<a_{J_{0}}
.
Then, for all
t\leq 0
,
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\sigma))<a_{J_{0}}
.

Proof.

We will only prove (i). The proof of (ii) is similar.
Fix
t\geq 0
and let
\rho:=\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\sigma)
.
Assume, for a contradiction, that
\Pi_{4}(\rho)\leq-a_{J_{0}}
.

Then, by Lemma
14.7
(ii),

\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{-t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))\leq-a_{J_{0}}
.
So, as
\Phi_{-t}^{P_{0}}(\rho)=\sigma
,

\Pi_{4}(\sigma)\leq-a_{J_{0}}
,
contradicting the assumption that
\Pi_{4}(\sigma)>-a_{J_{0}}
.
∎

LEMMA 14.9
.

Let
\sigma\in\Pi_{3}^{-1}({\mathbb{R}}\backslash J_{0})
.
Then, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have
\Pi_{3}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\sigma))=\Pi_{3}(\sigma)
.

Proof.

Let
a:=\Pi_{3}(\sigma)
.
Then
a\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash J_{0}
.
Define
F,G:{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by

F(w,x,z)\,\,=\,\,(w,x,a,z)\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad G(w,x,z)\,\,=\,\,(w,x,0,z).

Let
X:=\Pi_{3}^{-1}(a)=F({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{4}

and
Y:=\Pi_{3}^{-1}(0)=G({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Define
\pi:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
by
\pi(w,x,y,z)=(w,x,z)
.
For all
\tau\in X
, we have
F(\pi(\tau))=\tau
.
For all
\tau\in Y
, we have
G(\pi(\tau))=\tau
.
We wish to show, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
that
\Pi_{3}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\sigma))=a
,
i.e.
,
that
\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\sigma)\in X
.

Since
\Pi_{3}(\sigma)=a
, we get
\sigma\in\Pi_{3}^{-1}(a)=X
, so
F(\pi(\sigma))=\sigma
.
Let
\rho:=\pi(\sigma)
.
Then
F(\rho)=F(\pi(\sigma))=\sigma
.
Define
V:{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
by
V(\phi)=\pi(P_{0}(F(\phi)))
.
Then
V({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\subseteq\pi(P_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{4}))\subseteq\pi(\,{\overline{I}}_{0}^{4}\,)={\overline{I}}_{0}^{3}
.
So, since
V
is
C^{\infty}
, we see that
V
is complete.
Define
\gamma:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by
\gamma(t)=F(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\rho))
.
Then, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have
\gamma(t)\in F({\mathbb{R}}^{3})=X
.
It therefore suffices to show, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, that
\gamma(t)=\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\sigma)
.
We have
\gamma(0)=F(\rho)=\sigma=\Phi_{0}^{P_{0}}(\sigma)
.
So, by uniqueness of solutions of ODEs,
it suffices to show, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, that
\gamma^{\prime}(t)=P_{0}(\gamma(t))
.
For all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\gamma^{\prime}(t)\,\,=\,\,[d/dt][F(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\rho))]\,\,=\,\,G([d/dt][\Phi_{t}^{V}(\rho)])\,\,=\,\,G(V(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\rho))).

Fix
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and define
\lambda:=\Phi_{t}^{V}(\rho)
.
Then we have both
\gamma^{\prime}(t)=G(V(\lambda))
and
P_{0}(\gamma(t))=P_{0}(F(\Phi_{t}^{V}(\rho)))=P_{0}(F(\lambda))
,
and so we wish to prove that
G(V(\lambda))=P_{0}(F(\lambda))
.

Let
\mu:=F(\lambda)
.
Then
\mu\in F({\mathbb{R}}^{3})=\Pi_{3}^{-1}(a)
, so
\Pi_{3}(\mu)=a
.
Then
\Pi_{3}(\mu)=a\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash J_{0}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}\backslash(50I_{0})
,
so
\mu\in[{\mathbb{R}}^{4}]\backslash[(200I_{0})^{2}\times(50I_{0})^{2}]
.
So, by Lemma
14.5
(vi), we have
\Pi_{34}(P_{0}(\mu))=(0,1)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
so
\Pi_{3}(P_{0}(\mu))=0
.
Let
\nu:=P_{0}(\mu)=P_{0}(F(\lambda))
.
Then
\Pi_{3}(\nu)=\Pi_{3}(P_{0}(\mu))=0
,
and so
\nu\in\Pi_{3}^{-1}(0)=Y
.
Then
G(\pi(\nu))=\nu
.
By definition of
V
, we have
V(\lambda)=\pi(P_{0}(F(\lambda)))
.
That is,
V(\lambda)=\pi(\nu)
.

Then
G(V(\lambda))=G(\pi(\nu))=\nu=P_{0}(F(\lambda))
, as desired.
∎

COROLLARY 14.10
.

The set
\Pi_{3}^{-1}(J_{0})
is
P_{0}
-invariant.

Proof.

For all
\sigma\in\Pi_{3}^{-1}({\mathbb{R}}\backslash J_{0})
,
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
by Lemma
14.9
,
we get
\Pi_{3}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\sigma))=\Pi_{3}(\sigma)
,
so
\Pi_{3}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\sigma))\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash J_{0}
,

i.e.
,
\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\sigma)\in\Pi_{3}^{-1}({\mathbb{R}}\backslash J_{0})
.
This shows
\Pi_{3}^{-1}({\mathbb{R}}\backslash J_{0})
is
P_{0}
-invariant.
Then
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\backslash[\Pi_{3}^{-1}({\mathbb{R}}\backslash J_{0})]
is also
P_{0}
-invariant.
So, since
\Pi_{3}^{-1}(J_{0})={\mathbb{R}}^{4}\backslash[\Pi_{3}^{-1}({\mathbb{R}}\backslash J_{0})]
,
we are done.
∎

LEMMA 14.11
.

Let
\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,
t_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then both of the following hold:

(i)

Say
\Phi_{[t_{0},\infty)}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq[{\mathbb{R}}^{4}]\backslash[J_{0}^{4}]
.
Then
\displaystyle{\lim_{t\to\infty}\,\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))=\infty}
.

(ii)

Say
\Phi_{(-\infty,t_{0}]}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq[{\mathbb{R}}^{4}]\backslash[J_{0}^{4}]
.
Then
\displaystyle{\lim_{t\to-\infty}\,\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))=-\infty}
.

Proof.

We will only prove (i). The proof of (ii) is similar.

Define
\lambda:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
\lambda(t)=\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))
.
We wish to show that
\displaystyle{\lim_{t\to\infty}\,\lambda(t)=\infty}
.
Let
c:=(\lambda(t_{0}))-t_{0}
.
Because
\displaystyle{\lim_{t\to\infty}\,(c+t)=\infty}
,
it suffices to show, for all
t\geq t_{0}
, that
\lambda(t)=c+t
.
Since
\lambda(t_{0})=c+t_{0}
, it suffices to show, for all
t\geq t_{0}
, that
\lambda^{\prime}(t)=1
.
Differentiating the definition of
\lambda
, and using linearity of
\Pi_{4}
,
we conclude, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, that
\lambda^{\prime}(t)=\Pi_{4}(P_{0}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho)))
.
Fix
t\geq t_{0}
and let
\rho_{1}:=\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho)
.
We wish to show that
\Pi_{4}(P_{0}(\rho_{1}))=1
.

Since
t\geq t_{0}
,
\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\in\Phi_{[t_{0},\infty)}^{P_{0}}(\rho)
.
That is,
\rho_{1}\in\Phi_{[t_{0},\infty)}^{P_{0}}(\rho)
.
Then

\rho_{1}\,\,\in\,\,\Phi_{[t_{0},\infty)}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\,\,\subseteq\,\,[{\mathbb{R}}^{4}]\backslash[J_{0}^{4}]\,\,\subseteq\,\,[{\mathbb{R}}^{4}]\,\backslash\,[(200I_{0})^{2}\times(50I_{0})^{2}].

So, by Lemma
14.5
(vi), we conclude that
\Pi_{34}(P_{0}(\rho_{1}))=(0,1)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Then
\Pi_{4}(P_{0}(\rho_{1}))=1
, as desired.
∎

LEMMA 14.12
.

Let
a>0
.
Let
I:=(-a,a)\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let
s,u\in{\mathbb{R}}
and assume that
s<u
.
Let
\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Assume that
\Phi_{s}^{P_{0}}(\rho),\Phi_{u}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\in I^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Then
\Phi_{[s,u]}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq I^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

Proof.

Fix
t\in[s,u]
.
We wish to prove
\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\in I^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
That is, we wish to prove
both
\Pi_{1}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))\in I

and
\Pi_{2}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))\in I
.
We will only prove the former.
The proof of the latter is similar.

Since
t\leq u
, by Lemma
14.3
(i),

|\Pi_{1}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))|\leq|\Pi_{1}(\Phi_{u}^{P_{0}}(\rho))|
.
Since
\Phi_{u}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\in I^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
we have
\Pi_{1}(\Phi_{u}^{P_{0}}(\rho))\in I
,
so
|\Pi_{1}(\Phi_{u}^{P_{0}}(\rho))|<a
.
Then
|\Pi_{1}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))|\leq|\Pi_{1}(\Phi_{u}^{P_{0}}(\rho))|<a
,
so
\Pi_{1}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))\in I
, as desired.
∎

We record the special cases
a=1
and
a=200
of Lemma
14.12
:

COROLLARY 14.13
.

Let
s,u\in{\mathbb{R}}
and say
s<u
.
Let
\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Assume that
\Phi_{s}^{P_{0}}(\rho),\Phi_{u}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\in I_{0}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Then
\Phi_{[s,u]}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq I_{0}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

COROLLARY 14.14
.

Let
s,u\in{\mathbb{R}}
and say
s<u
.
Let
\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Assume that
\Phi_{s}^{P_{0}}(\rho),\Phi_{u}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\in J_{0}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Then
\Phi_{[s,u]}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq J_{0}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

LEMMA 14.15
.

Let
s,u\in{\mathbb{R}}
and assume that
s<u
.
Let
\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Assume that
\Phi_{s}^{P_{0}}(\rho),\Phi_{u}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times J_{0}^{2}
.
Then
\Phi_{[s,u]}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times J_{0}^{2}
.

Proof.

Fix
t\in[s,u]
.
Say, for a contradiction, that

\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\notin{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times J_{0}^{2}
.

Let
\sigma:=\Phi_{s}^{P_{0}}(\rho)

and
\tau:=\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho)

and
\mu:=\Phi_{u}^{P_{0}}(\rho)
.
Then
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times J_{0}^{2}

and
\tau\notin{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times J_{0}^{2}

and
\mu\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times J_{0}^{2}
.
Let
q:=t-s
and let
r:=u-t
.
Then
q\geq 0
and
r\geq 0
.
Moreover, we have
\tau=\Phi_{q}^{P_{0}}(\sigma)

and
\mu=\Phi_{r}^{P_{0}}(\tau)
.

As
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times J_{0}^{2}
,
we get
\Pi_{3}(\sigma)\in J_{0}
,

i.e.
,
\sigma\in\Pi_{3}^{-1}(J_{0})
.
By Corollary
14.10
,
the set
\Pi_{3}^{-1}(J_{0})
is
P_{0}
-invariant.
So, because
\sigma\in\Pi_{3}^{-1}(J_{0})
,
we get
\Phi_{q}^{P_{0}}(\sigma)\in\Pi_{3}^{-1}(J_{0})
,

i.e.
, we get
\tau\in\Pi_{3}^{-1}(J_{0})
.
Then
\Pi_{3}(\tau)\in J_{0}
.
So, since
\tau\notin{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times J_{0}^{2}
,
we see that
\Pi_{4}(\tau)\notin J_{0}
.

As
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times J_{0}^{2}
,
we get
\Pi_{4}(\sigma)\in J_{0}=(-a_{J_{0}},a_{J_{0}})
,
so
\Pi_{4}(\sigma)>-a_{J_{0}}
.
So, by Corollary
14.8
(i),

\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{q}^{P_{0}}(\sigma))>-a_{J_{0}}
,

i.e.
,
\Pi_{4}(\tau)>-a_{J_{0}}
.
So, as
\Pi_{4}(\tau)\notin J_{0}=(-a_{J_{0}},a_{J_{0}})
,
we get
\Pi_{4}(\tau)\geq a_{J_{0}}
.
So, by Lemma
14.7
(i),

\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{r}^{P_{0}}(\tau))\geq a_{J_{0}}
,

i.e.
,
\Pi_{4}(\mu)\geq a_{J_{0}}
.
Then
\Pi_{4}(\mu)\notin(-a_{J_{0}},a_{J_{0}})=J_{0}
.
However
\mu\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times J_{0}^{2}
, so
\Pi_{4}(\mu)\in J_{0}
,
contradiction.
∎

LEMMA 14.16
.

Let
s,u\in{\mathbb{R}}
and assume that
s<u
.
Let
\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Assume that
\Phi_{s}^{P_{0}}(\rho),\Phi_{u}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\in J_{0}^{4}
.
Then
\Phi_{[s,u]}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq J_{0}^{4}
.

Proof.

As
J_{0}^{4}\subseteq J_{0}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and
J_{0}^{4}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times J_{0}^{2}
,
by Corollary
14.14
and Lemma
14.15
,
we get
\Phi_{[s,u]}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq[J_{0}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}]\cap[{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times J_{0}^{2}]=J_{0}^{4}
.
∎

LEMMA 14.17
.

The map
P_{0}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
is porous.

Proof.

As
Z
is nowhere dense in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,
it suffices to prove

({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash Z\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}(P_{0})
.
Fix
\rho\in({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash Z
.
We wish to prove that
\rho\in{\mathcal{U}}(P_{0})
.

By Lemma
8.3
(c
\Rightarrow
a),
it suffices to prove that

\displaystyle{\lim_{t\to\infty}\,[\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))]=\infty}

and that

\displaystyle{\lim_{t\to-\infty}\,[\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))]=-\infty}
.
We will prove the former; the proof of the latter is similar.
By Lemma
14.11
(i),
it suffices to show, for some
t_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
that
\Phi_{[t_{0},\infty)}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq[{\mathbb{R}}^{4}]\backslash[J_{0}^{4}]
.
We therefore assume, for a contradiction, that, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
we have
[\Phi_{[t,\infty)}^{P_{0}}(\rho)]\cap[J_{0}^{4}]\neq\emptyset
.

We then fix a sequence
t_{1},t_{2},\ldots
in
{\mathbb{R}}

such that
t_{1}<t_{2}<\cdots
,
such that
t_{1},t_{2},\ldots\to\infty
,
and such that
\Phi_{t_{1}}^{P_{0}}(\rho),\Phi_{t_{2}}^{P_{0}}(\rho),\ldots\in J_{0}^{4}
.
Then, by Lemma
14.16
,
we have
\Phi_{[t_{1},t_{2}]}^{P_{0}}(\rho),\Phi_{[t_{2},t_{3}]}^{P_{0}}(\rho),\cdots\subseteq J_{0}^{4}
.
Taking the union,
\Phi_{[t_{1},\infty)}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq J_{0}^{4}
.
Let
\sigma:=\Phi_{t_{1}}^{P_{0}}(\rho)
.
Then
\Phi_{[0,\infty)}^{P_{0}}(\sigma)=\Phi_{[t_{1},\infty)}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq J_{0}^{4}
.

By Lemma
14.5
(vii),
we see that
Z
is
P_{0}
-invariant,
and it follows that
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash Z
is
P_{0}
-invariant.
So, since
\rho\in({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash Z
, we have
\sigma\in({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash Z
.
Choose
u,v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
such that
\sigma=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Since
\sigma\notin Z
, we get
u\notin Z_{0}
.
Then
u\notin\{0\}\times{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be the coordinate projection maps
defined by
\pi_{1}(w,x)=w
and
\pi_{2}(w,x)=x
.
By Lemma
12.3
(i), we have
|\pi_{1}(\Phi_{t}^{H}(u))|\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
.
Then fix a compact set
K\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
such that,

(*)

for all
t\in[0,\infty)\backslash K
,
|\pi_{1}(\Phi_{t}^{H}(u))|\geq a_{J_{0}}
.

Define
f:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
f(t)=\Omega_{\sigma}(t)
.
By Lemma
14.2
(ii and iv),
for all
t\geq 0
, we have both
f^{\prime}(t)\geq 0
and
f(t)\geq 0
.
For all
t\geq 0
, we have

\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\sigma)\in\Phi_{[0,\infty)}^{P_{0}}(\sigma)\subseteq J_{0}^{4}
,
so
\Pi_{1}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\sigma))\in J_{0}
,
so
|\Pi_{1}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\sigma))|<a_{J_{0}}
.
By Lemma
14.2
(v),
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have
\Pi_{12}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\sigma))=\Phi_{f(t)}^{H}(u)
,
so
\Pi_{1}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\sigma))=\pi_{1}(\Phi_{f(t)}^{H}(u))
.
Then, for all
t\geq 0
, we have

|\,\pi_{1}(\Phi_{f(t)}^{H}(u))\,|\quad=\quad|\,\Pi_{1}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\sigma))\,|\quad<\quad a_{J_{0}},

so, by
(*)
,
f(t)\in K
.
Then
f([0,\infty))\subseteq K
.
Then, by Lemma
3.1
(with
a:=8
,
b:=1
),
fix
r\geq 0
such that both
f^{\prime}(r)<1
and
f^{\prime}(r+8)<1
.

By Lemma
14.2
(ii),
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have
f^{\prime}(t)=\omega(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\sigma))
.
Let
\sigma_{0}:=\Phi_{r}^{P_{0}}(\sigma)
and
let
\sigma_{1}:=\Phi_{8}^{P_{0}}(\sigma_{0})=\Phi_{r+8}^{P_{0}}(\sigma)
.
Then

•

\sigma_{0},\,\sigma_{1}\quad\in\quad\Phi_{[0,\infty)}^{P_{0}}(\sigma)\quad\subseteq\quad J_{0}^{4}
and

•

\omega(\sigma_{0})\quad=\quad\omega(\Phi_{r}^{P_{0}}(\sigma))\quad=\quad f^{\prime}(r)\quad<\quad 1
and

•

\omega(\sigma_{1})\quad=\quad\omega(\Phi_{r+8}^{P_{0}}(\sigma))\quad=\quad f^{\prime}(r+8)\quad<\quad 1
.

Let
S_{1}:=J_{0}^{2}\times(4I_{0})^{2}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
By Lemma
14.5
(iv), we have
\omega=1
on
[J_{0}^{4}]\backslash[S_{1}]
.
So, since
\omega(\sigma_{0})<1
and
\sigma_{0}\in J_{0}^{4}
, we see that
\sigma_{0}\in S_{1}
.
Similarly, since
\omega(\sigma_{1})<1
and
\sigma_{1}\in J_{0}^{4}
, we see that
\sigma_{1}\in S_{1}
.

Let
v_{0}:=\Pi_{34}(\sigma_{0})
and
v_{1}:=\Pi_{34}(\sigma_{1})
.
Since
\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1}\in S_{1}
, we get
v_{0},v_{1}\in(4I_{0})^{2}
.
Fix
y,z\in 4I_{0}
such that
v_{0}=(y,z)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}={\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
We have
z\in 4I_{0}=(-4,4)
.
Then
z>-4
, so
z+8>4
.
Then
z+8\notin 4I_{0}
.
Then
\Phi_{8}^{Q_{0}}(v_{0})=(y,z+8)\notin(4I_{0})^{2}
.
Define
\lambda:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
by
\lambda(t)=\Pi_{34}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\sigma_{0}))
,
and let
A:=\{t\in[0,8]\,|\,\lambda(t)=\Phi_{t}^{Q_{0}}(v_{0})\}
.

Since
v_{1}\in(4I_{0})^{2}
and
\Phi_{8}^{Q_{0}}(v_{0})\notin(4I_{0})^{2}
,
we see that
v_{1}\neq\Phi_{8}^{Q_{0}}(v_{0})
.
Then
\lambda(8)=\Pi_{34}(\Phi_{8}^{P_{0}}(\sigma_{0}))=\Pi_{34}(\sigma_{1})=v_{1}\neq\Phi_{8}^{Q_{0}}(v_{0})
,
and so
8\notin A
.
Let
s:=\inf[0,8]\backslash A
.
Then
[0,s)\subseteq A
and, moreover,

(**)

for all
\delta>0
, we have
[s,s+\delta)\not\subseteq A
.

As
\lambda(0)=\Pi_{34}(\sigma_{0})=v_{0}=\Phi_{0}^{Q_{0}}(v_{0})
, we get
0\in A
.
So, since
A
is closed in
[0,8]
and
[0,s)\subseteq A
,
we have
s\in A
,
i.e.
, we have
\lambda(s)=\Phi_{s}^{Q_{0}}(v_{0})
.

As
z\in(-4,4)
and
s\in[0,8]
,
we get
z+s\in(-4,12)\subseteq(-12,12)
,
so
z+s\in 12I_{0}
.
Then
\Phi_{s}^{Q_{0}}(v_{0})=(y,z+s)\in(4I_{0})\times(12I_{0})=S
.
By definition of
\lambda
, we have
\lambda(s)=\Pi_{34}(\Phi_{s}^{P_{0}}(\sigma_{0}))
.
Then

\Pi_{34}(\Phi_{s}^{P_{0}}(\sigma_{0}))\quad=\quad\lambda(s)\quad=\quad\Phi_{s}^{Q_{0}}(v_{0})\quad\in\quad S,

so
\Phi_{s}^{P_{0}}(\sigma_{0})\in\Pi_{34}^{-1}(S)={\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times S
.
Then, because
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times S
is open in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, by continuity, choose
\delta_{1}>0

such that
\Phi_{[s,s+\delta_{1})}^{P_{0}}(\sigma_{0})\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times S
.

Claim:

For all
t\in[s,s+\delta_{1})
, we have
\lambda(t)=\Phi_{t}^{Q_{0}}(v_{0})
.

Proof of claim:

Since
\lambda(s)=\Phi_{s}^{Q_{0}}(v_{0})
,
by uniqueness of solutions of ODEs,
it suffices to prove, for all
t\in[s,s+\delta_{1})
,
that
\lambda^{\prime}(t)=Q_{0}(\lambda(t))
.

Differentiating the definition of
\lambda
, and using linearity of
\Pi_{34}
,
we see, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, that
\lambda^{\prime}(t)=\Pi_{34}(P_{0}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\sigma_{0})))
.
Let
w:=(0,1)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Then, by definition of
Q_{0}
, for all
\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, we have
Q_{0}(\tau)=w
.
Fix
t\in[s,s+\delta_{1})
.
We wish to prove that
\Pi_{34}(P_{0}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\sigma_{0})))=w
.

We have
\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\sigma_{0})\in\Phi_{[s,s+\delta_{1})}^{P_{0}}(\sigma_{0})\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times S
.
Then, by Lemma
14.5
(v),
we see that
\Pi_{34}(P_{0}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\sigma_{0})))=w
, as desired.

End of proof of claim.

Since
s\in A
and
8\notin A
, we see that
s\neq 8
.
So, as
s\in[0,8]
, we get
s<8
.
Let
\delta_{0}:=\min\{\delta_{1},8-s\}
.
Then
\delta_{0}>0
.
Also,
s+\delta_{0}\leq 8
.
Then
[s,s+\delta_{0})\subseteq[0,8]
.
As
\delta_{0}\leq\delta_{1}
, by the claim,
we see, for all
t\in[s,s+\delta_{0})
, that
\lambda(t)=\Phi_{t}^{Q_{0}}(v_{0})
.
Then
[s,s+\delta_{0})\subseteq A
, contradicting
(**)
.
∎

Recall that
\displaystyle{T_{\times}:=m-\left[\int_{-4}^{4}\,(\zeta_{3I_{0}}(t))\,dt\right]}
.

LEMMA 14.18
.

Let
\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times(3I_{0})^{2}
.
Assume
\Phi_{[0,m]}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq(\,\overline{100I_{0}}\,)^{4}
.
Then
\Pi_{12}(\Phi_{m}^{P_{0}}(\rho))=\Phi_{T_{\times}}^{H}(\Pi_{12}(\rho))

and
\Pi_{34}(\Phi_{m}^{P_{0}}(\rho))=\Phi_{m}^{Q}(\Pi_{34}(\rho))
.

Proof.

Define
f:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
f(t)=\Omega_{\rho}(t)
.
Choose
u_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and
v_{0}\in(3I_{0})^{2}
such that
\rho=(u_{0},v_{0})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Let
J:=[0,m]
.
Then we have
\Phi_{J}^{P_{0}}(\rho)=\Phi_{[0,m]}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq(\,\overline{100I_{0}}\,)^{4}
.

By Lemma
14.2
(v) and
Lemma
14.6
,
for all
t\in J
, we have both

\Pi_{12}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))=\Phi_{f(t)}^{H}(u_{0})\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\Pi_{34}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))=\Phi_{t}^{Q}(v_{0}).

Therefore we have both
\Pi_{12}(\Phi_{m}^{P_{0}}(\rho))=\Phi_{f(m)}^{H}(u_{0})=\Phi_{f(m)}^{H}(\Pi_{12}(\rho))

and
\Pi_{34}(\Phi_{m}^{P_{0}}(\rho))=\Phi_{m}^{Q}(v_{0})=\Phi_{m}^{Q}(\Pi_{34}(\rho))
.
We need only prove
f(m)=T_{\times}
.

For all
t\in J
,
\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\in\Phi_{J}^{P_{0}}(\rho)\subseteq(\,\overline{100I_{0}}\,)^{4}
,
so, by Lemma
14.5
(iii),
we conclude that
\omega(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))=\alpha(\Pi_{34}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho)))
.
Then, for all
t\in J
,
we have
\omega(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))=\alpha(\Pi_{34}(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho)))=\alpha(\Phi_{t}^{Q}(v_{0}))
.
Then

f(m)\,\,=\,\,\Omega_{\rho}(m)\,\,=\,\,\int_{0}^{m}\,[\omega(\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\rho))]\,dt\,\,=\,\,\int_{0}^{m}\,[\alpha(\Phi_{t}^{Q}(v_{0}))]\,dt.

Define
g:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
g(t)=1-[\alpha(\Phi_{t}^{Q}(v_{0}))]
.
Then

f(m)\quad=\quad\int_{0}^{m}\,\left[1-(g(t))\right]\,dt\quad=\quad m\,\,-\,\,\left[\int_{0}^{m}\,(g(t))\,dt\right].

Choose
y_{0},z_{0}\in 3I_{0}
such that
v_{0}=(y_{0},z_{0})\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}={\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Since
v_{0}\in(3I_{0})^{2}\subseteq(4I_{0})\times(12I_{0})=S\subseteq R
,
by definition of
m
, we get
\Phi_{m}^{Q}(v_{0})=v_{0}
.
Then, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\Phi_{t+m}^{Q}(v_{0})=\Phi_{t}^{Q}(\Phi_{m}^{Q}(v_{0}))=\Phi_{t}^{Q}(v_{0})
,
so
g(t+m)=g(t)
.
That is,
g:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is
m
-periodic.
It follows that

the integral of
g
over any interval in
{\mathbb{R}}
of length
m

is equal to

the integral of
g
over any other interval in
{\mathbb{R}}
of length
m
.

In particular,

\displaystyle{\int_{0}^{m}\,(g(t))\,dt=\int_{-4-z_{0}}^{m-4-z_{0}}\,(g(t))\,dt}
.
By invariance of integration under translation,

\displaystyle{\int_{-4-z_{0}}^{m-4-z_{0}}\,(g(t))\,dt=\int_{-4}^{m-4}\,(g(t-z_{0}))\,dt}
.

We have
y_{0}\in 3I_{0}\subseteq 4I_{0}
and
z_{0}\in 3I_{0}\subseteq 12I_{0}
and
v_{0}=(y_{0},z_{0})
.
So, for all
t\in[4,m-4]
,
by Corollary
13.4
,
\Phi_{t-z_{0}}^{Q}(v_{0})\in({\mathbb{R}}^{2})\backslash[(4I_{0})^{2}]
,
and so
\alpha(\Phi_{t-z_{0}}^{Q}(v_{0}))=1
,
and so
g(t-z_{0})=1-1=0
.
We conclude that
\displaystyle{\int_{4}^{m-4}\,(g(t-z_{0}))\,dt=0}
.
Then
\displaystyle{\int_{-4}^{m-4}\,(g(t-z_{0}))\,dt=\int_{-4}^{4}\,(g(t-z_{0}))\,dt}
.

Recall that
\alpha:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is defined by

\alpha(y,z)=1-[\zeta_{3I_{0}}(y)][\zeta_{3I_{0}}(z)]
.
As
y_{0}\in 3I_{0}
, we have
\zeta_{3I_{0}}(y_{0})=1
.
For all
t\in[-4,4]
,
by Lemma
13.1
(i),
we have
\Phi_{t-z_{0}}^{Q}(v_{0})=\Phi_{t-z_{0}}^{Q_{0}}(v_{0})=(y_{0},t)
,
and so

\alpha(\,\Phi_{t-z_{0}}^{Q}(v_{0})\,)\,\,\,=\,\,\,1\,-\,[\zeta_{3I_{0}}(y_{0})][\zeta_{3I_{0}}(t)]\,\,\,=\,\,\,1\,-\,[1][\zeta_{3I_{0}}(t)],

and so
g(t-z_{0})=\zeta_{3I_{0}}(t)
.
Then
\displaystyle{\int_{-4}^{4}\,(g(t-z_{0}))\,dt=\int_{-4}^{4}\,(\zeta_{3I_{0}}(t))\,dt}
.

Putting all these observations together, we conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{m}(g(t))\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-4-z_{0}}^{m-4-z_{0}}\,(g(t))\,dt\,=\,\int_{-4}^{m-4}\,(g(t-z_{0}))\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-4}^{4}\,(g(t-z_{0}))\,dt\,=\,\int_{-4}^{4}\,(\zeta_{3I_{0}}(t))\,dt.

Then

\displaystyle{f(m)=m-\left[\int_{0}^{m}\,(g(t))\,dt\right]=m-\left[\int_{-4}^{4}\,(\zeta_{3I_{0}}(t))\,dt\right]=T_{\times}}
.
∎

LEMMA 14.19
.

\Phi_{m}^{P_{0}}
agrees with
\hbox{Id}_{4}
to all orders at
\xi_{I_{0}}
.

Proof.

It suffices to show both of the following:

(i)

\Pi_{12}\circ\Phi_{m}^{P_{0}}
agrees with
\Pi_{12}
to all orders at
\xi_{I_{0}}
and

(ii)

\Pi_{34}\circ\Phi_{m}^{P_{0}}
agrees with
\Pi_{34}
to all orders at
\xi_{I_{0}}
.

Recall that
R\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is the racetrack of §
13

and that
{\bf 0}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is the zero map defined by
{\bf 0}(w,x)=(0,0)
.
Recall that the racetrack
R
is an open subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}

and that
(4I_{0})\times(12I_{0})=S\subseteq R\subseteq(50I_{0})^{2}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

Let
R_{1}:=\{(0,0)\}\times R\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Because
(0,-1)\in S\subseteq R
and
\xi_{I_{0}}=(0,0,0,-1)
,
we get
\xi_{I_{0}}\in R_{1}
.
Also, because
R\subseteq(50I_{0})^{2}
,
we conclude that
R_{1}\subseteq(50I_{0})^{4}\subseteq(100I_{0})^{4}\subseteq(\,\overline{100I_{0}}\,)^{4}
.

Define
Q_{1}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by:   for all
\rho=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,

Q_{1}(u,v)\quad=\quad(\,\,{\bf 0}(u)\,\,,\,\,Q(v)\,\,)\quad\in\quad{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,\times\,{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\quad=\quad{\mathbb{R}}^{4}.

From the construction in §
13
,
R
is
Q
-invariant,
so
R_{1}
is
Q_{1}
-invariant.
So, since
\xi_{I_{0}}\in R_{1}
, we conclude that
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{Q_{1}}(\xi_{I_{0}})\subseteq R_{1}
.

For
\rho=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,
if
\rho\in R_{1}
, then
u=(0,0)
, so
H_{0}(u)=(0,0)
,
so
H(u)=(0,0)
, so
[\alpha(v)][H(u)]=(0,0)={\bf 0}(u)
, so

P_{*}(\rho)\,\,\,=\,\,\,(\,\,[\alpha(v)][H(u)]\,\,,\,\,Q(v)\,\,)\,\,\,=\,\,\,(\,\,{\bf 0}(u)\,\,,\,\,Q(v)\,\,)\,\,\,=\,\,\,Q_{1}(\rho).

Thus
P_{*}=Q_{1}
on
R_{1}
.
Also, because
R_{1}\subseteq(\,\overline{100I_{0}}\,)^{4}\,
,
we see, by Lemma
14.5
(i),
that
P_{0}=P_{*}
on
R_{1}
.
Then
P_{0}=P_{*}=Q_{1}
on
R_{1}
.
So, as
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{Q_{1}}(\xi_{I_{0}})\subseteq R_{1}
,
by Lemma
6.1
, we get
\Phi_{[0,m]}^{P_{0}}(\xi_{I_{0}})=\Phi_{[0,m]}^{Q_{1}}(\xi_{I_{0}})
.
Then
\Phi_{[0,m]}^{P_{0}}(\xi_{I_{0}})=\Phi_{[0,m]}^{Q_{1}}(\xi_{I_{0}})\subseteq R_{1}\subseteq(100I_{0})^{4}
.
So, by continuity, fix an open neighborhood
N_{0}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
of
\xi_{I_{0}}

such that
\Phi_{[0,m]}^{P_{0}}(N_{0})\subseteq(100I_{0})^{4}
.
Let
N:=N_{0}\cap[{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times(3I_{0})^{2}]
.
Then
N
is also an open neighborhood in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
of
\xi_{I_{0}}

and, moreover,
\Phi_{[0,m]}^{P_{0}}(N)\subseteq\Phi_{[0,m]}^{P_{0}}(N_{0})\subseteq(100I_{0})^{4}
.
Then, by Lemma
14.18
, we see, for all
\rho\in N
,
that
\Pi_{12}(\Phi_{m}^{P_{0}}(\rho))=\Phi_{T_{\times}}^{H}(\Pi_{12}(\rho))

and that
\Pi_{34}(\Phi_{m}^{P_{0}}(\rho))=\Phi_{m}^{Q}(\Pi_{34}(\rho))
.
That is, on
N
, we have both

\Pi_{12}\circ\Phi_{m}^{P_{0}}\,=\,\Phi_{T_{\times}}^{H}\circ\Pi_{12}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\Pi_{34}\circ\Phi_{m}^{P_{0}}\,=\,\Phi_{m}^{Q}\circ\Pi_{34}.

So, since
N
is an open neighborhood of
\xi_{I_{0}}
, we have both

(*)

\Pi_{12}\,\circ\,\Phi_{m}^{P_{0}}
agrees with
\Phi_{T_{\times}}^{H}\,\circ\,\Pi_{12}
to all orders at
\xi_{I_{0}}

and

(**)

\Pi_{34}\,\circ\,\Phi_{m}^{P_{0}}
agrees with
\Phi_{m}^{Q}\,\circ\,\Pi_{34}
to all orders at
\xi_{I_{0}}
.

By Lemma
12.4
,
\Phi_{T_{\times}}^{H}
agrees with
\hbox{Id}_{2}
to all orders at
(0,0)
.
So, since
\Pi_{12}(\xi_{I_{0}})=(0,0)
, we see that

\Phi_{T_{\times}}^{H}\circ\Pi_{12}
agrees with
\Pi_{12}
to all orders at
\xi_{I_{0}}
.
So (i) follows from
(*)
.
It remains to prove (ii).

Recall that
(0,-1)\in R
.
Then
R
is an open neighborhood in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
of
(0,-1)
.
By definition of
m
, for all
v\in R
, we have
\Phi_{m}^{Q}(v)=v
.
That is,
\Phi_{m}^{Q}=\hbox{Id}_{2}
on
R
.
Then
\Phi_{m}^{Q}
agrees with
\hbox{Id}_{2}
to all orders at
(0,-1)
.
So, since
\Pi_{34}(\xi_{I_{0}})=(0,-1)
, we conclude that

\Phi_{m}^{Q}\circ\Pi_{34}
agrees with
\Pi_{34}
to all orders at
\xi_{I_{0}}
.
So (ii) follows from
(**)
.
∎

LEMMA 14.20
.

Let
\tau\in B_{\circ}(I_{0})
and let
s\in(0,m)
.
Assume that
\Phi_{s}^{P_{0}}(\tau)\in I_{0}^{4}
.
Then
s<2
.

Proof.

From the construction of
S
and
R
in §
13
,
we have

(4I_{0})\,\times\,(12I_{0})\quad=\quad S\quad\subseteq\quad R\quad\subseteq\quad(50I_{0})^{2}.

Then
I_{0}\times\{-1\}\subseteq(4I_{0})\times(12I_{0})\subseteq R
, so

I_{0}^{3}\times\{-1\}\subseteq I_{0}^{2}\times R
.
Let
C
be the closure in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
of
I_{0}^{2}\times R
.
Then
B_{\circ}(I_{0})=I_{0}^{3}\times\{-1\}\subseteq I_{0}^{2}\times R\subseteq C
.
Then
\tau\in B_{\circ}(I_{0})\subseteq C
.
Because
I_{0}^{2}\times R\subseteq I_{0}^{2}\times(50I_{0})^{2}\subseteq(50I_{0})^{4}
,
we get
C\subseteq(\,\overline{50I_{0}}\,)^{4}\subseteq(100I_{0})^{4}
.
Then
C\cup[(100I_{0})^{4}]=(100I_{0})^{4}
.

By assumption,
\tau\in B_{\circ}(I_{0})
and
\Phi_{s}^{P_{0}}(\tau)\in I_{0}^{4}
.
Then

\Phi_{0}^{P_{0}}(\tau)\quad=\quad\tau\quad\in\quad B_{\circ}(I_{0})\quad\subseteq\quad I_{0}^{2}\,\times\,R\quad\subseteq\quad I_{0}^{2}\,\times\,{\mathbb{R}}^{2}

and
\Phi_{s}^{P_{0}}(\tau)\in I_{0}^{4}\subseteq I_{0}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
So, by Corollary
14.13
,

\Phi_{[0,s]}^{P_{0}}(\tau)\subseteq I_{0}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

Claim:

\Phi_{[0,s]}^{P_{0}}(\tau)\subseteq C
.

Proof of claim:
Define

A\quad:=\quad\{\,\,t\in[0,s]\,\,|\,\,\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\tau)\in C\,\,\}.

Assume, for a contradiction, that
A\subsetneq[0,s]
.

Let
t_{0}:=\inf[0,s]\backslash A
.
Then
[0,t_{0})\subseteq A
and,

(*)

for all
\delta>0
, we have
[t_{0},t_{0}+\delta)\not\subseteq A
.

Because
\tau\in C
, we see that
0\in A
.
So, since
A
is closed in
[0,s]
and since
[0,t_{0})\subseteq A
,
we get
[0,t_{0}]\subseteq A
.
Then
[0,t_{0}]\subseteq A\subsetneq[0,s]
.
Then
t_{0}<s
.
As
t_{0}\in[0,t_{0}]\subseteq A
,
we conclude that
\Phi_{t_{0}}^{P_{0}}(\tau)\in C
.
Then
\Phi_{t_{0}}^{P_{0}}(\tau)\in C\subseteq(100I_{0})^{4}
.
By continuity, fix
\delta_{1}>0
such that

\Phi_{[t_{0},t_{0}+\delta_{1})}^{P_{0}}(\tau)\subseteq(100I_{0})^{4}
.
We have
[0,t_{0}]\subseteq A
, so
\Phi_{[0,t_{0}]}^{P_{0}}(\tau)\subseteq C
.
Then
\Phi_{[0,t_{0}+\delta_{1})}^{P_{0}}(\tau)=[\Phi_{[0,t_{0})}^{P_{0}}(\tau)]\cup[\Phi_{[t_{0},t_{0}+\delta_{1})}^{P_{0}}(\tau)]\subseteq C\cup[(100I_{0})^{4}]=(100I_{0})^{4}
.
By Lemma
14.5
(i), we have
P_{*}=P_{0}
on
(100I_{0})^{4}
.
Then, by Lemma
6.1
, we have

\Phi_{[0,t_{0}+\delta_{1})}^{P_{*}}(\tau)=\Phi_{[0,t_{0}+\delta_{1})}^{P_{0}}(\tau)
.

By Lemma
14.5
(viii),
we see that
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{P_{*}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times R)={\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times R
.
Also,
\tau\in I_{0}^{2}\times R\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times R
.
Let
\delta_{0}:=\min\{\delta_{1},s-t_{0}\}
.
Then
\delta_{0}>0
and

\Phi_{[t_{0},t_{0}+\delta_{0})}^{P_{0}}(\tau)\subseteq\Phi_{[0,t_{0}+\delta_{1})}^{P_{0}}(\tau)=\Phi_{[0,t_{0}+\delta_{1})}^{P_{*}}(\tau)\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{P_{*}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times R)={\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times R
.

Recall that
\Phi_{[0,s]}^{P_{0}}(\tau)\subseteq I_{0}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Also,
[t_{0},t_{0}+\delta_{0})\subseteq[0,s]
.
Then
\Phi_{[t_{0},t_{0}+\delta_{0})}^{P_{0}}(\tau)\subseteq\Phi_{[0,s]}^{P_{0}}(\tau)\subseteq I_{0}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Then

\Phi_{[t_{0},t_{0}+\delta_{0})}^{P_{0}}(\tau)\,\,\,\subseteq\,\,\,[I_{0}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}]\,\cap\,[{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times R]\,\,\,=\,\,\,I_{0}^{2}\,\times\,R\,\,\,\subseteq\,\,\,C.

Then
[t_{0},t_{0}+\delta_{0})\subseteq A
, contradicting
(*)
.

End of proof of claim.

By assumption,
\tau\in B_{\circ}(I_{0})
and
\Phi_{s}^{P_{0}}(\tau)\in I_{0}^{4}
.
Fix
u,v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
such that
\tau=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
By the claim,
\Phi_{[0,s]}^{P_{0}}(\tau)\subseteq C
.
Then
\Phi_{[0,s]}^{P_{0}}(\tau)\subseteq C\subseteq(100I_{0}\,)^{4}
.
Then, by Lemma
14.6
,
we have
\Pi_{34}(\Phi_{s}^{P_{0}}(\tau))=\Phi_{s}^{Q}(v)
.
Then
\Phi_{s}^{Q}(v)=\Pi_{34}(\Phi_{s}^{P_{0}}(\tau))\in\Pi_{34}(I_{0}^{4})=I_{0}^{2}
.
Also,
v=\Pi_{34}(\tau)\in\Pi_{34}(B_{\circ}(I_{0}))=\Pi_{34}(I_{0}^{3}\times\{-1\})=I_{0}\times\{-1\}
.
So let
z:=-1
and choose
y\in I_{0}
such that
v=(y,z)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}={\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

Let
q:=s-1
.
Then
\Phi_{q-z}^{Q}(v)=\Phi_{q+1}^{Q}(v)=\Phi_{s}^{Q}(v)\in I_{0}^{2}
.
By Corollary
13.3
, we know,
for all
t\in[1,m-1]
, that
\Phi_{t-z}^{Q}(v)\notin I_{0}^{2}
.
Then
q\notin[1,m-1]
,
so
s\notin[2,m]
.
So, since
s\in(0,m)
, we get
s<2
.
∎

LEMMA 14.21
.

We have
(P_{0},K_{0})\in{\mathcal{P}}_{I_{0}}
.

Proof.

Recall that
P_{0}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
is
C^{\infty}
and
P_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\subseteq{\overline{I}}_{0}^{4}
.
Then
P_{0}\in{\mathcal{C}}
.
On
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(K_{0}^{4})
,
we have
\gamma=0
, so
P_{0}=\gamma P_{+}+(1-\gamma)V_{0}=V_{0}
.
Then
(P_{0},K_{0})\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
By Lemma
14.5
(ii),

P_{0}=V_{0}
on
(\,\overline{3I_{0}}\,)^{4}
.
Then
P_{0}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{I_{0}}
.
So, since
4I_{0}\subseteq 400I_{0}=K_{0}
,
we get
(P_{0},K_{0})\in{\mathcal{D}}_{I_{0}}^{\times}
.
As
m>24
, we get
m>2=2a_{I_{0}}
.
By Lemma
14.19
,
\Phi_{m}^{P_{0}}
agrees with
\hbox{Id}_{4}
to all orders at
\xi_{I_{0}}
.
It remains to show, for all
\tau\in B_{\circ}(I_{0})
, for all
t\in(0,m)
, that

[\,\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\tau)\in I_{0}^{4}\,]\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad[\,t<2\,].

Lemma
14.20
yields
\Rightarrow
,
and it remains to prove
\Leftarrow
.
Fix
\tau\in B_{\circ}(I_{0})
.
We wish to prove that
\Phi_{(0,2)}^{P_{0}}(\tau)\subseteq I_{0}^{4}
.

As
\tau\in B_{\circ}(I_{0})=I_{0}^{3}\times\{-1\}
, we get

\Phi_{(0,2)}^{V_{0}}(\tau)\subseteq I_{0}^{3}\times(-1,1)=I_{0}^{4}
and

\Phi_{[0,2]}^{V_{0}}(\tau)\quad\subseteq\quad I_{0}^{3}\,\times\,[-1,1]\quad\subseteq\quad{\overline{I}}_{0}^{4}\quad\subseteq\quad(\,\overline{3I_{0}}\,)^{4}.

So, since
P_{0}=V_{0}
on
(\,\overline{3I_{0}}\,)^{4}
,
by Lemma
6.1
, for all
t\in[0,2]
,
we have
\Phi_{t}^{P_{0}}(\tau)=\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}(\tau)
.
Then
\Phi_{(0,2)}^{P_{0}}(\tau)=\Phi_{(0,2)}^{V_{0}}(\tau)\subseteq I_{0}^{4}
, as desired.
∎

15.
Results about the exchange operation
{\mathcal{X}}_{I}(P,V)

Recall, from §
9
,
the definitions of
DF_{I}^{V}
,
UF_{I}^{V}
and
TF_{I}^{V}
.
Recall, from §
11
,
the definitions of
{\mathcal{D}}_{+}
,
{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{+}^{\#}
and
{\mathcal{D}}_{*}
.
The definitions of
{\mathcal{D}}_{I}^{\times}
,
{\mathcal{X}}_{I}(P,v)
