and
{\mathcal{P}}_{I}

are all found in §
2
.

The following fact will be used repeatedly throughout this section:
If
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
, if
(P,K)\in{\mathcal{D}}_{I}^{\times}
and if
X={\mathcal{X}}_{I}(P,V)
, then

•

4I\subseteq K
,

•

P=V_{0}
on
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(K^{4})
,

•

X=P
on
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
,

•

V=V_{0}
on
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
,

•

P=V_{0}
on
(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}
and

•

X=V
on
(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}
,

and, consequently,

•

X=P=V=V_{0}
on
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(K^{4})
and

•

X=P=V=V_{0}
on
[(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}]\backslash[I^{4}]
.

LEMMA 15.1
.

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Let
(P,K)\in{\mathcal{D}}_{I}^{\times}
.
Let
X:={\mathcal{X}}_{I}(P,V)
.
Then
(X,K)\in{\mathcal{M}}(V,I)
.

Proof.

On
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(K^{4})
, we have
X=V_{0}
.
Then
(X,K)\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
We have
4I\subseteq K
, so
a_{4I}\leq a_{K}
.
Then
a_{I}<4a_{I}=a_{4I}\leq a_{K}
.
Also, we have
X=V
on
{\overline{I}}^{4}
.
Then
(X,K)\in{\mathcal{M}}(V,I)
, as desired.
∎

LEMMA 15.2
.

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}_{+}

and let
(P,K)\in{\mathcal{D}}_{I}^{\times}
.
Assume that both
V
and
P
are porous.
Let
X:={\mathcal{X}}_{I}(P,V)
.
Then
X
is porous.

Proof.

Let

•

S_{1}:=({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash({\mathcal{U}}(P))
,
U_{1}:=({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(\,{\overline{I}}^{4}\,)
and

•

S_{2}:=({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash({\mathcal{U}}(V))
,
U_{2}:=(2I)^{4}
.

Then

•

(
S_{1}
is
P
-invariant )  and  (
X=P
on
U_{1}
)   and

•

(
S_{2}
is
V
-invariant )  and  (
X=V
on
U_{2}
).

Then, by Lemma
7.1
,

Z_{1}:=S_{1}\cap U_{1}
and
Z_{2}:=S_{2}\cap U_{2}

are both locally
X
-invariant.
Since both
V
and
P
are porous, by Corollary
8.5
,
we see that both
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are meager in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Then both
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
are meager in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Then, by Lemma
7.2
,
both
Z^{\prime}_{1}:=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(Z_{1})
and
Z^{\prime}_{2}:=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(Z_{2})
are meager in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Then
Z^{\prime}:=Z^{\prime}_{1}\cup Z^{\prime}_{2}
is
X
-invariant and meager in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.

By Corollary
8.5
,
we wish to show that
{\mathcal{U}}(X)
is comeager in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
It therefore suffices to prove that
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash Z^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}(X)
.
Fix
\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, and assume that
\rho\notin Z^{\prime}
.
We wish to show that
\rho\in{\mathcal{U}}(X)
.

Claim 1:

\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho)\not\subseteq{\overline{I}}^{4}
.

Proof of Claim 1:

Assume, for a contradiction, that
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho)\subseteq{\overline{I}}^{4}
.
Then, since
V=X
on
{\overline{I}}^{4}
, by Lemma
6.1
,
we get
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\rho)=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho)
.
Then
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\rho)\subseteq{\overline{I}}^{4}
,
so
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\rho))\subseteq\Pi_{4}(\,{\overline{I}}^{4}\,)={\overline{I}}
.

We have
\rho\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho)\subseteq{\overline{I}}^{4}\subseteq(2I)^{4}=U_{2}
.
Because
\rho\notin Z^{\prime}
, we know that
\rho\notin Z^{\prime}_{2}
,
so
\rho\notin Z_{2}=S_{2}\cap U_{2}
.
Then
\rho\notin S_{2}
.
That is,
\rho\in{\mathcal{U}}(V)
.
Then
{\mathbb{R}}=\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\rho))\subseteq{\overline{I}}
, contradiction.

End of proof of Claim 1.

By Claim 1, fix
\rho_{0}\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho)
such that
\rho_{0}\notin{\overline{I}}^{4}
.
Since
Z^{\prime}
is
X
-invariant, since
\rho\notin Z^{\prime}
and since
\rho_{0}\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho)
,
we see that
\rho_{0}\notin Z^{\prime}
.
Since
{\mathcal{U}}(X)
is
X
-invariant,
it suffices to show that
\rho_{0}\in{\mathcal{U}}(X)
.

On
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(K^{4})
, we have
X=V_{0}
.
Therefore
(X,K)\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Then, by Lemma
8.3
(b
\Rightarrow
a),
it suffices to show that

(-\infty,-a_{K})\,\cap\,[\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0}))]\,\,\neq\,\,\emptyset\,\,\neq\,\,(a_{K},\infty)\,\cap\,[\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0}))].

Because
\Pi_{4}(I^{4})=I\subseteq 4I\subseteq K=(-a_{K},a_{K})
, we get

(-\infty,-a_{K})\,\cap\,[\Pi_{4}(I^{4})]\quad=\quad\emptyset\quad=\quad(a_{K},\infty)\,\cap\,[\Pi_{4}(I^{4})].

Let
R:=(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0}))\cup(I^{4})
.
Then
\Pi_{4}(R)=[\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0}))]\cup[\Pi_{4}(I^{4})]
, so

\displaystyle(-\infty,-a_{K})\,\cap\,[\Pi_{4}(R)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-\infty,-a_{K})\,\cap\,[\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0}))]\qquad\hbox{and}

\displaystyle(a_{K},\infty)\,\cap\,[\Pi_{4}(R)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(a_{K},\infty)\,\cap\,[\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0}))].

It therefore suffices to prove that

(-\infty,-a_{K})\,\cap\,[\Pi_{4}(R)]\quad\neq\quad\emptyset\quad\neq\quad(a_{K},\infty)\,\cap\,[\Pi_{4}(R)].

We have
\rho_{0}\notin Z^{\prime}
, so
\rho_{0}\notin Z^{\prime}_{1}
, so
\rho_{0}\notin Z_{1}=S_{1}\cap U_{1}
.
So, since
\rho_{0}\in({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(\,{\overline{I}}^{4}\,)=U_{1}
, we see that
\rho_{0}\notin S_{1}
.
That is,
\rho_{0}\in{\mathcal{U}}(P)
.
Then
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{P}(\rho_{0}))={\mathbb{R}}
, and it follows that

(-\infty,-a_{K})\,\cap\,[\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{P}(\rho_{0}))]\,\,\neq\,\,\emptyset\,\,\neq\,\,(a_{K},\infty)\,\cap\,[\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{P}(\rho_{0}))].

It therefore suffices to show that
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{P}(\rho_{0})\subseteq R
.
We will only prove that
\Phi_{[0,\infty)}^{P}(\rho_{0})\subseteq R
;
a similar argument proves that
\Phi_{(-\infty,0]}^{P}(\rho_{0})\subseteq R
.
We define
Q:=\{t\geq 0\,|\,\Phi_{t}^{P}(\rho_{0})\in R\}
.
We wish to show that
Q=[0,\infty)
.
Assume, for a contradiction, that
Q\neq[0,\infty)
.

Since
Q\subsetneq[0,\infty)
,
we get
[0,\infty)\backslash Q\neq\emptyset
.
Let
t_{1}:=\inf\,[0,\infty)\backslash Q
.

Claim 2:

t_{1}>0
.

Proof of Claim 2:

Since
\rho_{0}\in({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(\,{\overline{I}}^{4}\,)
,
by continuity of
\Phi_{\bullet}^{P}(\rho_{0})
,
fix
\gamma>0
such that

\Phi_{[0,\gamma)}^{P}(\rho_{0})\subseteq({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
.
Then, because
X=P
on
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
,
by Lemma
6.1
,

\Phi_{[0,\gamma)}^{X}(\rho_{0})=\Phi_{[0,\gamma)}^{P}(\rho_{0})
.

Then
\Phi_{[0,\gamma)}^{P}(\rho_{0})\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0})\subseteq R
.
It follows that
[0,\gamma)\subseteq Q
.
Therefore
t_{1}=\inf\,[0,\infty)\backslash Q\geq\gamma>0
.

End of proof of Claim 2.

Because
\inf[0,\infty)\backslash Q=t_{1}
,
we get
[0,t_{1})\subseteq Q
and, for all
\delta>0
, we have
[t_{1},t_{1}+\delta)\not\subseteq Q
.
Because
[0,t_{1})\subseteq Q
, it follows that

(+)

\Phi_{[0,t_{1})}^{P}(\rho_{0})\,\,\subseteq\,\,R\,\,=\,\,(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0}))\,\cup\,(I^{4})
.

Let
\rho_{1}:=\Phi_{t_{1}}^{P}(\rho_{0})
.
For all
\delta>0
, we have
[t_{1},t_{1}+\delta)\not\subseteq Q
,
from which it follows that
\Phi_{[t_{1},t_{1}+\delta)}^{P}(\rho_{0})\not\subseteq R
.
That is,

(*)

for all
\delta>0
,
\Phi_{[0,\delta)}^{P}(\rho_{1})\,\,\not\subseteq\,\,R\,\,=\,\,(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0}))\,\cup\,(I^{4})
.

Claim 3:

\rho_{1}\notin I^{4}
.

Proof of Claim 3:

Suppose that
\rho_{1}\in I^{4}
.
We wish to obtain a contradiction.

By continuity of
\Phi_{\bullet}^{P}(\rho_{1})
,
fix
\delta>0
such that

\Phi_{[0,\delta)}^{P}(\rho_{1})\subseteq I^{4}
.
Then
\Phi_{[0,\delta)}^{P}(\rho_{1})\subseteq I^{4}\subseteq R
.
This contradicts
(*)
.

End of proof of Claim 3.

Claim 4:
Let
N
be an interval in
{\mathbb{R}}

such that
\inf N<0
and
0\in N
.
Assume that
\Phi_{N}^{P}(\rho_{1})\subseteq({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
.
Then
\Phi_{N}^{P}(\rho_{1})\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0})
.

Proof of Claim 4:

Because
X=P
on
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
,
it follows, from Lemma
6.1
,
that, for all
t\in N
,
we have
\Phi_{t}^{X}(\rho_{1})=\Phi_{t}^{P}(\rho_{1})
.
Then
\Phi_{N}^{X}(\rho_{1})=\Phi_{N}^{P}(\rho_{1})
.
By Claim 2,
t_{1}>0
.
Choose
t_{*}\in(0,t_{1}]
such that
-t_{*}\in N
.
Let
\rho_{*}:=\Phi_{-t_{*}}^{X}(\rho_{1})=\Phi_{-t_{*}}^{P}(\rho_{1})
.
Then
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{*})=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{1})
.
It follows that
\Phi_{N}^{P}(\rho_{1})=\Phi_{N}^{X}(\rho_{1})\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{1})=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{*})
.

We have
t_{1}-t_{*}\in[0,t_{1})
.
So, by
(+)
, we get
\Phi_{t_{1}-t_{*}}^{P}(\rho_{0})\in R
.
Since
\rho_{1}=\Phi_{t_{1}}^{P}(\rho_{0})
,
we see that
\Phi_{-t_{*}}^{P}(\rho_{1})=\Phi_{t_{1}-t_{*}}^{P}(\rho_{0})
.
Then

\rho_{*}\,\,\,=\,\,\,\Phi_{-t_{*}}^{P}(\rho_{1})\,\,\,=\,\,\,\Phi_{t_{1}-t_{*}}^{P}(\rho_{0})\,\,\,\in\,\,\,R\,\,\,=\,\,\,(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0}))\,\cup\,(I^{4}).

So, since
\rho_{*}=\Phi_{-t_{*}}^{P}(\rho_{1})\in\Phi_{N}^{P}(\rho_{1})\subseteq({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
,
we see that
\rho_{*}\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0})
.
Then
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{*})=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0})
.
Then
\Phi_{N}^{P}(\rho_{1})\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{*})=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0})
.

End of proof of Claim 4.

Claim 5:

\rho_{1}\in{\overline{I}}^{4}
.

Proof of Claim 5:

Suppose that
\rho_{1}\notin{\overline{I}}^{4}
.
We wish to obtain a contradiction.

By continuity of
\Phi_{\bullet}^{P}(\rho_{1})
,
fix
\delta>0
such that
\Phi_{(-\delta,\delta)}^{P}(\rho_{1})\subseteq({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
.
Then, by Claim 4, we have

\Phi_{(-\delta,\delta)}^{P}(\rho_{1})\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0})
.
Then

\Phi_{[0,\delta)}^{P}(\rho_{1})\quad\subseteq\quad\Phi_{(-\delta,\delta)}^{P}(\rho_{1})\quad\subseteq\quad\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0})\quad\subseteq\quad R.

This contradicts
(*)
.

End of proof of Claim 5.

Claim 6:

\rho_{1}\notin B_{\circ}(I)
.

Proof of Claim 6:

Assume, for a contradiction, that
\rho_{1}\in B_{\circ}(I)
.
Then, by Lemma
4.2
(v),
\Phi_{[-1,1]}^{V_{0}}(\rho_{1})\subseteq(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}
.
Since
P=V_{0}
on
(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}
,
it follows, from Lemma
6.1
,
that, for all
t\in[-1,1]
,
we have
\Phi_{t}^{P}(\rho_{1})=\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}(\rho_{1})
.

Because
\rho_{1}\in B_{\circ}(I)
,
by Lemma
4.2
(iv),

\Phi_{[-1,0]}^{V_{0}}(\rho_{1})\subseteq[(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}]\backslash[I^{4}]
.
Then
\Phi_{[-1,0]}^{P}(\rho_{1})=\Phi_{[-1,0]}^{V_{0}}(\rho_{1})\subseteq[(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}]\backslash[I^{4}]\subseteq({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
.
Then, by Claim 4, we get

\Phi_{[-1,0]}^{P}(\rho_{1})\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0})
.
Then
\Phi_{[-1,0]}^{P}(\rho_{1})\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0})\subseteq R
.

Because
\rho_{1}\in B_{\circ}(I)
,
by Lemma
4.2
(i),
\Phi_{(0,1]}^{V_{0}}(\rho_{1})\subseteq I^{4}
.
Then

\Phi_{(0,1]}^{P}(\rho_{1})\quad=\quad\Phi_{(0,1]}^{V_{0}}(\rho_{1})\quad\subseteq\quad I^{4}\quad\subseteq\quad R.

Then
\Phi_{[0,1)}^{P}(\rho_{1})\subseteq\Phi_{[-1,1]}^{P}(\rho_{1})=(\Phi_{[-1,0]}^{P}(\rho_{1}))\cup(\Phi_{(0,1]}^{P}(\rho_{1}))\subseteq R
.
This contradicts
(*)
.

End of proof of Claim 6.

Claim 7:

\rho_{1}\in T_{\circ}(I)
.

Proof of Claim 7:

By Claim 5 and Claim 3, we have
\rho_{1}\in(\,{\overline{I}}^{4}\,)\backslash(I^{4})
.
Let
S:=(\,{\overline{I}}^{3}\,)\backslash(I^{3})
.
Then

\rho_{1}\quad\in\quad(\,{\overline{I}}^{4}\,)\backslash(I^{4})\quad=\quad(B_{\circ}(I))\,\cup\,(S\times{\overline{I}}\,)\,\cup\,(T_{\circ}(I)).

So, by Claim 6, we have

\rho_{1}\in(S\times{\overline{I}}\,)\cup(T_{\circ}(I))
,
and it suffices to show that
\rho_{1}\notin S\times{\overline{I}}
.
Assume, for a contradiction, that
\rho_{1}\in S\times{\overline{I}}
.

By Lemma
4.2
(vi),

\Phi_{[-1,1]}^{V_{0}}(\rho_{1})\subseteq[(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}]\backslash[I^{4}]\subseteq(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}
.
Then, since
P=V_{0}
on
(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}
,
by Lemma
6.1
,

\Phi_{[-1,1]}^{P}(\rho_{1})=\Phi_{[-1,1]}^{V_{0}}(\rho_{1})
.
Then
\Phi_{[-1,1]}^{P}(\rho_{1})=\Phi_{[-1,1]}^{V_{0}}(\rho_{1})\subseteq[(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}]\backslash[I^{4}]\subseteq({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
.
Then, by Claim 4,
\Phi_{[-1,1]}^{P}(\rho_{1})\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0})
.
Then
\Phi_{[0,1)}^{P}(\rho_{1})\subseteq\Phi_{[-1,1]}^{P}(\rho_{1})\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0})\subseteq R
.
This contradicts
(*)
.

End of proof of Claim 7.

By Claim 7 and Lemma
4.2
(ii),
we see that
\Phi_{(-2a_{I},0)}^{V_{0}}(\rho_{1})\subseteq I^{4}
.
So, since
P=V_{0}
on
I^{4}
,
it follows, from Corollary
6.2
, that

(**)

for all
t\in[-2a_{I},0]
, we have
\Phi_{t}^{P}(\rho_{1})=\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}(\rho_{1})
.

Then, for all
t\in(-2a_{I},0)
,

\Phi_{t}^{P}(\rho_{1})=\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}(\rho_{1})\in\Phi_{(-2a_{I},0)}^{V_{0}}(\rho_{1})\subseteq I^{4}\subseteq{\overline{I}}^{4}
.
Also,
\Phi_{-t_{1}}^{P}(\rho_{1})=\rho_{0}\notin{\overline{I}}^{4}
.
Then
-t_{1}\notin(-2a_{I},0)
,
i.e.
,
t_{1}\notin(0,2a_{I})
.
So, by Claim 2, it follows that
t_{1}\geq 2a_{I}
.
Then
0\leq t_{1}-2a_{I}<t_{1}
.

By Claim 7,
\rho_{1}\in T_{\circ}(I)
.
Let
\rho^{\prime}:=\Phi_{-2a_{I}}^{V_{0}}(\rho_{1})
.
By Lemma
4.3
,
we have
\rho^{\prime}\in B_{\circ}(I)
and
\rho_{1}=SU_{I}(\rho^{\prime})
.
By
(**)
,
\Phi_{-2a_{I}}^{P}(\rho_{1})=\Phi_{-2a_{I}}^{V_{0}}(\rho_{1})
.
Then

\rho^{\prime}\,\,=\,\,\Phi_{-2a_{I}}^{V_{0}}(\rho_{1})\,\,=\,\,\Phi_{-2a_{I}}^{P}(\rho_{1})\,\,=\,\,\Phi_{-2a_{I}}^{P}(\Phi_{t_{1}}^{P}(\rho_{0}))\,\,=\,\,\Phi_{t_{1}-2a_{I}}^{P}(\rho_{0}).

So, since
t_{1}-2a_{I}\in[0,t_{1})
, by
(+)
,
we see that
\rho^{\prime}\in(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0}))\cup(I^{4})
.
Since
\rho^{\prime}\in B_{\circ}(I)
and
(B_{\circ}(I))\cap(I^{4})=\emptyset
,
we have
\rho^{\prime}\notin I^{4}
.
Then
\rho^{\prime}\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0})
.
Then, because
Z^{\prime}
is
X
-invariant and
\rho_{0}\notin Z^{\prime}
,
we see that
\rho^{\prime}\notin Z^{\prime}
.
Then
\rho^{\prime}\notin Z^{\prime}_{2}
, so
\rho^{\prime}\notin Z_{2}=S_{2}\cap U_{2}
.
So, since
\rho^{\prime}\in B_{\circ}(I)\subseteq{\overline{I}}^{4}\subseteq(2I)^{4}=U_{2}
,
we conclude that
\rho^{\prime}\notin S_{2}
.
That is,
\rho^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{U}}(V)
.
Then

\rho^{\prime}\,\,\in\,\,(B_{\circ}(I))\,\,\cap\,\,({\mathcal{U}}(V))\,\,\subseteq\,\,(B(I))\cap({\mathcal{U}}(V))\,\,=\,\,{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V,I).

So, since
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}_{+}
, we get
SU_{I}(\rho^{\prime})=UF_{I}^{V}(\rho^{\prime})
.
Let
t_{0}:=TF_{I}^{V}(\rho^{\prime})
.
We have
\rho^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V,I)
, so
\rho^{\prime}=DF_{I}^{V}(\rho^{\prime})
.
Then
\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\rho^{\prime})=UF_{I}^{V}(\rho^{\prime})
.
By Lemma
9.2
,

\Phi_{(0,t_{0})}^{V}(\rho^{\prime})\subseteq I^{4}
.
Also,
X=V
on
I^{4}
.
Then, by Corollary
6.2
,

\Phi_{t_{0}}^{X}(\rho^{\prime})=\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\rho^{\prime})
.
Then
UF_{I}^{V}(\rho^{\prime})=\Phi_{t_{0}}^{V}(\rho^{\prime})=\Phi_{t_{0}}^{X}(\rho^{\prime})
.
Then
\rho_{1}=SU_{I}(\rho^{\prime})=UF_{I}^{V}(\rho^{\prime})=\Phi_{t_{0}}^{X}(\rho^{\prime})\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho^{\prime})
.
Also, recall that
\rho^{\prime}\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0})
.
We conclude that
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{1})=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho^{\prime})=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0})
.

By Claim 7 and Lemma
4.2
(iii),

\Phi_{[0,1]}^{V_{0}}(\rho_{1})\subseteq[(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}]\backslash[I^{4}]
.
So, since
X=V_{0}=P
on
[(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}]\backslash[I^{4}]
,
by Lemma
6.1
, we have

\Phi_{[0,1]}^{X}(\rho_{1})\quad=\quad\Phi_{[0,1]}^{V_{0}}(\rho_{1})\quad=\quad\Phi_{[0,1]}^{P}(\rho_{1}).

Then

\Phi_{[0,1)}^{P}(\rho_{1})\subseteq\Phi_{[0,1]}^{P}(\rho_{1})=\Phi_{[0,1]}^{X}(\rho_{1})\subseteq\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{1})=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho_{0})\subseteq R
.
This contradicts
(*)
.
∎

LEMMA 15.3
.

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}_{*}
.
Let
(P,K)\in{\mathcal{P}}_{I}
.
Let
X:={\mathcal{X}}_{I}(P,V)
.
Then
(X,\xi_{I})
is periodic to all orders.

Proof.

Since
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}_{*}
,
fix an integer
j\geq 1
and an open neighborhood
N
in
{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V,I)

of
\xi_{I}
such that
TF_{I}^{V}=j
on
N
.
Then, by definition of
UF_{I}^{V}
, we see that
UF_{I}^{V}=\Phi_{j}^{V}
on
N
.
Because
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}_{*}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}_{+}
, it follows that
UF_{I}^{V}=SU_{I}
on
{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V,I)
.
We have
P=V_{0}
on
I^{4}
.
By Lemma
4.2
(i), we have
\Phi_{(0,2a_{I})}^{V_{0}}(B_{\circ}(I))=I^{4}
.
Then, by Corollary
6.2
,
on
B(I)
, we have
\Phi_{2a_{I}}^{P}=\Phi_{2a_{I}}^{V_{0}}
.
Also, by Lemma
9.1
(ii), on
B(I)
, we have
SU_{I}=\Phi_{2a_{I}}^{V_{0}}
.
Because
X=V
on
I^{4}
,
by Lemma
9.3
,

\Phi_{j}^{X}=\Phi_{j}^{V}
on
{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V,I)
.
We have
N\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V,I)\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(V,I)\subseteq B(I)
.
Then, on
N
, we have

\Phi_{j}^{X}\,\,=\,\,\Phi_{j}^{V}\,\,=\,\,UF_{I}^{V}\,\,=\,\,SU_{I}\,\,=\,\,\Phi_{2a_{I}}^{V_{0}}\,\,=\,\,\Phi_{2a_{I}}^{P}.

For all
t\in(0,2a_{I})
, let
L_{t}:=\Phi_{-t}^{V_{0}}(N)
.

Claim:
Let
t\in(0,2a_{I})
. Then, on
L_{t}
,

\Phi_{j+2a_{I}}^{X}=\Phi_{4a_{I}}^{V_{0}}=\Phi_{4a_{I}}^{P}
.

Proof of claim:

We have
X=V_{0}=P
on
[(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}]\backslash[I^{4}]

and
2a_{I}-t\in(0,2a_{I})
.
By Lemma
4.2
(iii), we have

\Phi_{(0,2a_{I})}^{V_{0}}(T_{\circ}(I))\subseteq[(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}]\backslash[I^{4}]
.
Therefore, by Lemma
6.1
,
on
T_{\circ}(I)
, we have
\Phi_{2a_{I}-t}^{X}=\Phi_{2a_{I}-t}^{V_{0}}=\Phi_{2a_{I}-t}^{P}
.

We have
X=V_{0}=P
on
[(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}]\backslash[I^{4}]
.
By Lemma
4.2
(iv), we have

\Phi_{(-2a_{I},0)}^{V_{0}}(B_{\circ}(I))\subseteq[(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}]\backslash[I^{4}]
.
Also,
N\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V,I)\subseteq B_{\circ}(I)
.
Then

\Phi_{(0,t)}^{V_{0}}(L_{t})\,\,=\,\,\Phi_{(-t,0)}^{V_{0}}(N)\subseteq\,\,\Phi_{(-2a_{I},0)}^{V_{0}}(B_{\circ}(I))\,\,\subseteq\,\,[(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}]\backslash[I^{4}].

So, by Corollary
6.2
,
on
L_{t}
, we have
\Phi_{t}^{X}=\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}=\Phi_{t}^{P}
.
We also have
\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}(L_{t})=N
.
Then, since
\Phi_{j}^{X}=\Phi_{2a_{I}}^{V_{0}}=\Phi_{2a_{I}}^{P}
on
N
,
we see that, on
L_{t}
,

\Phi_{j}^{X}\circ\Phi_{t}^{X}\quad=\quad\Phi_{2a_{I}}^{V_{0}}\circ\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}\quad=\quad\Phi_{2a_{I}}^{P}\circ\Phi_{t}^{P}.

By Lemma
9.1
(ii),

\Phi_{2a_{I}}^{V_{0}}(B_{\circ}(I))=SU_{I}(B_{\circ}(I))
.
Then

\Phi_{2a_{I}}^{V_{0}}(\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}(L_{t}))\,=\,\Phi_{2a_{I}}^{V_{0}}(N)\,\subseteq\,\Phi_{2a_{I}}^{V_{0}}(B_{\circ}(I))\,=\,SU_{I}(B_{\circ}(I))\,=\,T_{\circ}(I).

So, since
\Phi_{2a_{I}-t}^{X}=\Phi_{2a_{I}-t}^{V_{0}}=\Phi_{2a_{I}-t}^{P}
on
T_{\circ}(I)
,
we see that, on
L_{t}
,

\Phi_{2a_{I}-t}^{X}\circ\Phi_{j}^{X}\circ\Phi_{t}^{X}\,\,=\,\,\Phi_{2a_{I}-t}^{V_{0}}\circ\Phi_{2a_{I}}^{V_{0}}\circ\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}\,\,=\,\,\Phi_{2a_{I}-t}^{P}\circ\Phi_{2a_{I}}^{P}\circ\Phi_{t}^{P}.

That is, on
L_{t}
, we have
\Phi_{j+2a_{I}}^{X}=\Phi_{4a_{I}}^{V_{0}}=\Phi_{4a_{I}}^{P}
.

End of proof of claim.

Since
(P,K)\in{\mathcal{P}}_{I}
,
fix an integer
m>2a_{I}
such that

(*)

\Phi_{m}^{P}
agrees with
\hbox{Id}_{4}
at
\xi_{I}
to all orders   and

(**)

for all
\tau\in B_{\circ}(I)
,   for all
t\in(0,m)
,

[\,\Phi_{t}^{P}(\tau)\in I^{4}\,]\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad[\,t<2a_{I}\,].

Let
U:=\displaystyle{\bigcup_{t\in(0,2a_{I})}L_{t}=\Phi_{(-2a_{I},0)}^{V_{0}}(N)}
.
The claim shows that, on
U
, we have

\Phi_{j+2a_{I}}^{X}=\Phi_{4a_{I}}^{V_{0}}=\Phi_{4a_{I}}^{P}
.

We have
P=V_{0}
on
(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}
and,
from Lemma
4.2
(v),
we conclude that

\Phi_{[0,4a_{I}]}^{V_{0}}(B_{\circ}(I))\subseteq(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}
.
Then, by Corollary
6.2
,
for all
t\in[0,4a_{I}]
, on
B(I)
,
\Phi_{t}^{P}=\Phi_{t}^{V_{0}}
.
Let
U_{1}:=\Phi_{(2a_{I},4a_{I})}^{P}(B_{\circ}(I))=\Phi_{(2a_{I},4a_{I})}^{V_{0}}(B_{\circ}(I))
.
We have

\Phi_{(0,m-4a_{I})}^{P}(U_{1})\qquad\subseteq\qquad\Phi_{(2a_{I},m)}^{P}(B_{\circ}(I)),

and, by
(**)
, we have
\Phi_{(2a_{I},m)}^{P}(B_{\circ}(I))\subseteq({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
.
Then

\Phi_{(0,m-4a_{I})}^{P}(U_{1})\quad\subseteq\quad({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4}).

Also,
X=P
on
({\mathbb{R}}^{4})\backslash(I^{4})
.
Then, by Corollary
6.2
,
on
U_{1}
, we have
\Phi_{m-4a_{I}}^{X}=\Phi_{m-4a_{I}}^{P}
.
Recall that, on
U
,
\Phi_{j+2a_{I}}^{X}=\Phi_{4a_{I}}^{P}
.
Also,

\Phi_{4a_{I}}^{V_{0}}(U)\,\,=\,\,\Phi_{(2a_{I},4a_{I})}^{V_{0}}(N)\,\,\subseteq\,\,\Phi_{(2a_{I},4a_{I})}^{V_{0}}(B_{\circ}(I))\,\,=\,\,U_{1}.

Then, on
U
, we have

\Phi_{m-4a_{I}}^{X}\circ\Phi_{j+2a_{I}}^{X}=\Phi_{m-4a_{I}}^{P}\circ\Phi_{4a_{I}}^{P}
.
That is, on
U
, we have
\Phi_{m-2a_{I}+j}^{X}=\Phi_{m}^{P}
.

We have
X=V_{0}=P
on
[(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}]\backslash[I^{4}]
.
Also,
\xi_{I}\in B_{\circ}(I)
, so, and, by Lemma
4.2
(iv),
we get
\Phi_{[-2a_{I},0]}^{V_{0}}(\xi_{I})\subseteq[(\,\overline{3I}\,)^{4}]\backslash[I^{4}]
.
Then, by Lemma
6.1
, we have

\Phi_{-a_{I}}^{X}(\xi_{I})=\Phi_{-a_{I}}^{V_{0}}(\xi_{I})=\Phi_{-a_{I}}^{P}(\xi_{I})
.

Let
\rho:=\Phi_{-a_{I}}^{V_{0}}(\xi_{I})=\Phi_{-a_{I}}^{X}(\xi_{I})=\Phi_{-a_{I}}^{P}(\xi_{I})
.
Because
\xi_{I}\in N
, it follows that
\rho\in\Phi_{(-2a_{I},0)}^{V_{0}}(N)=U
.
By Corollary
8.4
(iii),
{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V,I)
is open in
B_{\circ}(I)
.
So, as
N
is open in
{\mathcal{U}}_{B}^{\circ}(V,I)
, we see that
N
is open in
B_{\circ}(I)
.
Then, by Lemma
4.7
,
U
is an open subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Then
U
is an open neighborhood of
\rho
.
So, since
\Phi_{m-2a_{I}+j}^{X}=\Phi_{m}^{P}
on
U
, we see that

\Phi_{m-2a_{I}+j}^{X}
agrees to all orders with
\Phi_{m}^{P}
at
\rho
.
Since
\rho\in\Phi^{P}_{\mathbb{R}}(\xi_{I})
, it follows, from
(*)
,
that
\Phi_{m}^{P}
agrees to all orders with
\hbox{Id}_{4}
at
\rho
.
Then
\Phi_{m-2a_{I}+j}^{X}
agrees to all orders with
\hbox{Id}_{4}
at
\rho
.
We have
m>2a_{I}
and
j\geq 1
, so
m-2a_{I}+j\neq 0
.
Then
(X,\rho)
is periodic to all orders.
So, as
\xi_{I}=\Phi_{a_{I}}^{X}(\rho)\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\rho)
, we see that
(X,\xi_{I})
is periodic to all orders.
∎

16.
The iteration

Recall, from §
9
,
the definitions of
DF_{I}^{V}
,
UF_{I}^{V}
and
TF_{I}^{V}
.

Recall, from §
11
,
the definitions of
{\mathcal{D}}_{+}
,
{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{+}^{\#}
and
{\mathcal{D}}_{*}
.
The definition of
{\mathcal{D}}_{I}^{\times}
appears in §
2
.

Let
\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
We define
{\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by
{\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}(\sigma)=\sigma+\rho
.
For any
V\in{\mathcal{C}}
,
we define
{\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}V:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by
({\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}V)(\sigma)=V(\sigma-\rho)
;
then
{\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}V\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}V)={\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}({\mathcal{U}}(V))
,
so:   [
V
is porous ]    iff    [
{\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}V
is porous ].

For all
\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, we have
{\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}V_{0}=V_{0}
.
Also, for all
\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, for all
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for all
V\in{\mathcal{V}}(a)
,
we have
{\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}V\in{\mathcal{V}}(a+[\Pi_{4}(\rho)])
.

Let
J\in{\mathcal{I}}
.
Let
\rho\in J^{4}
.
Then, for all
I\in{\mathcal{I}}
, we have
{\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}(I^{4})\subseteq(I+J)^{4}
.
Moreover, for all
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
, we have
({\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}V,I+J)\in{\mathcal{D}}
.

For all
V\in{\mathcal{C}}
, for all
\rho,\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, we have:
if
(V,\sigma)
is periodic to all orders,
then
({\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}V,\sigma+\rho)
is periodic to all orders.

LEMMA 16.1
.

Let
(V,I)\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Assume
V
is porous.
Let
\sigma^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{U}}(V)
.
Then there exists
(V^{\prime},I^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{M}}(V,I)

such that
V^{\prime}
is porous and
such that
(V^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
is periodic to all orders.

Proof.

Choose
J_{0}\in{\mathcal{I}}
such that
{\overline{I}}\subseteq J_{0}
and such that
\sigma^{\prime}\in J_{0}^{4}
.
Then
a_{I}<a_{J_{0}}
and
(V,J_{0})\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Also,
\sigma^{\prime}\in({\mathcal{U}}(V))\cap(J_{0}^{4})={\mathcal{U}}_{\circ}(V,J_{0})
.
Since
(V,J_{0})\in{\mathcal{D}}
, it follows that
V\in{\mathcal{V}}(a_{J_{0}})
.

Let
{\widecheck{\sigma}}:=DF_{J_{0}}^{V}(\sigma^{\prime})
.
Then, by Lemma
9.5
, we have
{\widecheck{\sigma}}\in B_{\circ}(J_{0})
.
Let
\tau_{1}:=\xi_{J_{0}}-{\widecheck{\sigma}}
.
Then
{\mathcal{T}}_{\tau_{1}}({\widecheck{\sigma}})=\xi_{J_{0}}

and
{\mathcal{T}}_{-\tau_{1}}(\xi_{J_{0}})={\widecheck{\sigma}}
.

For all
\rho\in B_{\circ}(J_{0})
,
\xi_{J_{0}}-\rho\in J_{0}^{3}\times\{0\}
.
Then
\tau_{1}\in J_{0}^{3}\times\{0\}
,
so
\tau_{1}\in J_{0}^{4}
and
\Pi_{4}(\tau_{1})=0
.
Let
W:={\mathcal{T}}_{\tau_{1}}V
.
Then
V={\mathcal{T}}_{-\tau_{1}}W
.
Let
J:=J_{0}+J_{0}=2J_{0}
.
Then
(W,J)\in{\mathcal{D}}
and
W
is porous.

Let
s_{0}:=a_{J_{0}}
.
Then
(0,0,0,-s_{0})=\xi_{J_{0}}
and
\Phi_{-s_{0}}^{V_{0}}({\widecheck{\sigma}})={\widecheck{\sigma}}+\xi_{J_{0}}
.
We have
{\widecheck{\sigma}}\in B_{\circ}(J_{0})\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times\{-a_{J_{0}}\}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-\infty,-a_{J_{0}}]
.
By Lemma
8.1
(iii), we have

\Phi_{-s_{0}}^{V}({\widecheck{\sigma}})=\Phi_{-s_{0}}^{V_{0}}({\widecheck{\sigma}})
.
Then
\Phi_{-s_{0}}^{V}({\widecheck{\sigma}})={\widecheck{\sigma}}+\xi_{J_{0}}
.

We have
{\widecheck{\sigma}}=DF_{J_{0}}^{V}(\sigma^{\prime})\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma^{\prime})
,
and
{\widecheck{\sigma}}+\xi_{J_{0}}=\Phi_{-s_{0}}^{V}({\widecheck{\sigma}})\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}({\widecheck{\sigma}})
.
Then
{\widecheck{\sigma}}+\xi_{J_{0}}\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}(\sigma^{\prime})
.
So, since
\sigma^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{U}}(V)

and
{\mathcal{U}}(V)
is
V
-invariant,
we see that
{\widecheck{\sigma}}+\xi_{J_{0}}\in{\mathcal{U}}(V)
.
Then
{\mathcal{T}}_{\tau_{1}}({\widecheck{\sigma}}+\xi_{J_{0}})\in{\mathcal{U}}(W)
.
So, since

{\mathcal{T}}_{\tau_{1}}({\widecheck{\sigma}}+\xi_{J_{0}})=2\xi_{J_{0}}=\xi_{2J_{0}}=\xi_{J}
,
we get
\xi_{J}\in{\mathcal{U}}(W)
.
Then

\xi_{J}\quad\in\quad({\mathcal{U}}(W))\,\cap\,(B(J))\quad=\quad{\mathcal{U}}_{B}(W,J).

Then
(W,J)\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}
.
By Lemma
11.3
,
fix
(V_{*},I_{*})\in({\mathcal{M}}_{*}(W,J))\cap({\mathcal{D}}_{*})

such that
V_{*}
is porous.
Then
(V_{*},I_{*})\in{\mathcal{D}}_{*}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}
.
Also,

(V_{*},I_{*})\quad\in\quad{\mathcal{M}}_{*}(W,J)\quad\subseteq\quad{\mathcal{M}}(W,J),

so
a_{J}<a_{I_{*}}
and
V_{*}=W
on
{\overline{J}}^{4}
.
As
a_{J}<a_{I_{*}}
, we get
{\overline{J}}\subseteq I_{*}
.
Moreover, because
(V_{*},I_{*})\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}(W,J)
,
it follows that
V_{*}\in{\mathcal{V}}(a_{J})
.

Let
P_{0}
and
K_{0}
be as in §
14
.
By Lemma
14.17
,
P_{0}
is porous.
By Lemma
14.21
,
(P_{0},K_{0})\in{\mathcal{P}}_{I_{0}}

Let
K:=a_{I_{*}}K_{0}
and
define
P:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by
P(\sigma)=P_{0}(\sigma/a_{I_{*}})
.
Then
P
is porous and
(P,K)\in{\mathcal{P}}_{I_{*}}
.

Since
(P,K)\in{\mathcal{P}}_{I_{*}}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}_{I_{*}}^{\times}
,
it follows that
4I_{*}\subseteq K
,
so
a_{4I_{*}}\leq a_{K}
.
Then
a_{I_{*}}<4a_{I_{*}}=a_{4I_{*}}\leq a_{K}
.

Let
X:={\mathcal{X}}_{I_{*}}(P,V_{*})
.
Then
X=V_{*}
on
I_{*}^{4}
, and, therefore, on
{\overline{J}}^{4}
.
Then
X=V_{*}=W
on
{\overline{J}}^{4}
.
Since

(V_{*},I_{*})\,\,\in\,\,{\mathcal{D}}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad(P,K)\,\,\in\,\,{\mathcal{D}}_{I_{*}}^{\times},

we see, by Lemma
15.1
, that
(X,K)\in{\mathcal{M}}(V_{*},I_{*})
.
Therefore we have
(X,K)\in{\mathcal{M}}(V_{*},I_{*})\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}
.
Because

(V_{*},I_{*})\,\,\in\,\,{\mathcal{D}}_{*}\,\,\subseteq\,\,{\mathcal{D}}_{+}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad(P,K)\,\,\in\,\,{\mathcal{D}}_{I_{*}}^{\times},

and because
V_{*}
and
P
are porous,
we see, by Lemma
15.2
, that
X
is porous.
Because

(V_{*},I_{*})\,\,\in\,\,{\mathcal{D}}_{*}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad(P,K)\,\,\in\,\,{\mathcal{P}}_{I_{*}},

we see, by Lemma
15.3
,
that
(X,\xi_{I_{*}})
is periodic to all orders.

Let
V^{\prime}:={\mathcal{T}}_{-\tau_{1}}X
.
Then
V^{\prime}
is porous.
Since
\tau_{1}\in J_{0}^{4}
, we get
-\tau_{1}\in J_{0}^{4}
.
Let
I^{\prime}:=K+J_{0}
.
Then
(V^{\prime},I^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{D}}
.

Because
(X,K)\in{\mathcal{M}}(V_{*},I_{*})
and
(V_{*},I_{*})\in{\mathcal{M}}(W,J)
,
it follows that
(X,K)\in{\mathcal{M}}(W,J)
.
Then
X=W
on
{\overline{J}}^{4}
.
Then, on
{\mathcal{T}}_{-\tau_{1}}(\,{\overline{J}}^{4}\,)
, we have

V^{\prime}\,\,=\,\,{\mathcal{T}}_{-\tau_{1}}X\,\,=\,\,{\mathcal{T}}_{-\tau_{1}}W\,\,=\,\,V.

We have
{\mathcal{T}}_{\tau_{1}}(J_{0}^{4})\subseteq(J_{0}+J_{0})^{4}=J^{4}\subseteq{\overline{J}}^{4}
,
so
J_{0}^{4}\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{-\tau_{1}}(\,{\overline{J}}^{4}\,)
.
Therefore
V^{\prime}=V
on
J_{0}^{4}
.
So, since
{\overline{I}}\subseteq J_{0}
, we see that
V^{\prime}=V
on
{\overline{I}}^{4}
.
Also,

a_{I}<a_{J_{0}}<2a_{J_{0}}=a_{2J_{0}}=a_{J}<a_{I_{*}}<a_{K}<a_{K}+a_{J_{0}}=a_{K+J_{0}}=a_{I^{\prime}}.

Then
(V^{\prime},I^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{M}}(V,I)
.
It remains only to prove that
(V^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
is periodic to all orders.

Because
V_{*}\in{\mathcal{V}}(a_{J})
,
we have
V_{*}=V_{0}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-\infty,-a_{J}]
.
Since
X={\mathcal{X}}_{I_{*}}(P,V_{*})
, we have
X=V_{*}
on
{\overline{I}}_{*}^{4}
.
Then

(i)

X=V_{*}=V_{0}
on
\{(0,0,0)\}\times[-a_{I_{*}},-a_{J}]
.

We have
W={\mathcal{T}}_{\tau_{1}}V
and
\Pi_{4}(\tau_{1})=0
.
So, since
V\in{\mathcal{V}}(a_{J_{0}})
, we have
W\in{\mathcal{V}}(a_{J_{0}})
as well.
That is,
W=V_{0}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(-\infty,-a_{J_{0}}]
.
Then, because
X=W
on
{\overline{J}}^{4}
,
we see that

(ii)

X=W=V_{0}
on
\{(0,0,0)\}\times[-a_{J},-a_{J_{0}}]
.

Let
s_{1}:=a_{I_{*}}-a_{J_{0}}
.
By Lemma
4.4
(i),
\Phi_{s_{1}}^{V_{0}}(\xi_{I_{*}})=\xi_{J_{0}}
.
By (i) and (ii), we have
X=V_{0}
on
\{(0,0,0)\}\times[-a_{I_{*}},-a_{J_{0}}]
.
By Lemma
4.4
(ii),

\Phi_{[0,s_{1}]}^{V_{0}}(\xi_{I_{*}})\subseteq\{(0,0,0)\}\times[-a_{I_{*}},-a_{J_{0}}]
.
Then, by Lemma
6.1
, we have

\Phi_{s_{1}}^{X}(\xi_{I_{*}})=\Phi_{s_{1}}^{V_{0}}(\xi_{I_{*}})
.
Then
\Phi_{s_{1}}^{X}(\xi_{I_{*}})=\xi_{J_{0}}
.
Then
\xi_{J_{0}}\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{X}(\xi_{I_{*}})
, so,
because
(X,\xi_{I_{*}})
is periodic to all orders,
we see that
(X,\xi_{J_{0}})
is periodic to all orders.
Then, because
{\mathcal{T}}_{-\tau_{1}}X=V^{\prime}
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{-\tau_{1}}(\xi_{J_{0}})={\widecheck{\sigma}}
,
we conclude that
(V^{\prime},{\widecheck{\sigma}})
is periodic to all orders.

Since
{\widecheck{\sigma}}=DF_{J_{0}}^{V}(\sigma^{\prime})
, it follows that
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V}({\widecheck{\sigma}})
.
So choose
s_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\Phi_{s_{0}}^{V}({\widecheck{\sigma}})=\sigma^{\prime}
.
Recall that
V^{\prime}=V
on
J_{0}^{4}
.
Also,
(V,J_{0})\in{\mathcal{D}}
and
J_{0}\subseteq 2J_{0}=J
.
Then
(V,J)\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Also,

{\widecheck{\sigma}}\in B_{\circ}(J_{0})\subseteq{\overline{J}}_{0}^{4}\subseteq(2J_{0})^{4}=J^{4}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\Phi_{s_{0}}^{V}({\widecheck{\sigma}})=\sigma^{\prime}\in J_{0}^{4}\subseteq J^{4}.

Then, by Lemma
8.7
,

\Phi_{s_{0}}^{V^{\prime}}({\widecheck{\sigma}})=\Phi_{s_{0}}^{V}({\widecheck{\sigma}})
.
It follows that

\sigma^{\prime}\quad=\quad\Phi_{s_{0}}^{V}({\widecheck{\sigma}})\quad=\quad\Phi_{s_{0}}^{V^{\prime}}({\widecheck{\sigma}})\quad\in\quad\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V^{\prime}}({\widecheck{\sigma}}).

So, since
(V^{\prime},{\widecheck{\sigma}})
is periodic to all orders,
it follows that
(V^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
is periodic to all orders as well.
∎

17.
Results about cyclic groups

LEMMA 17.1
.

Let
C
be a cyclic subgroup of a locally compact (Hausdorff) topological group
P
.
Then either
C
is a closed subset of
P

or the closure in
P
of
C
is compact.

Proof.

Let
{\overline{C}}
denote the closure in
P
of
C
.
Give
{\overline{C}}
the relative topology inherited from
P
.
Assume that
{\overline{C}}
is noncompact.
We wish to show that
C
is closed in
P
.

By Theorem 2.3.2, p. 39 of
[
Rud62
]
,
since
{\overline{C}}
is monothetic and noncompact,
it follows that
{\overline{C}}
is discrete.
Then every subset of
{\overline{C}}
is closed, and
in particular,
C
is closed in
{\overline{C}}
.
So, since
{\overline{C}}
is closed in
P
,
we see that
C
is closed in
P
.
∎

LEMMA 17.2
.

Let
P
and
Q
be locally compact (Hausdorff) topological groups
and let
h:P\to Q
be a surjective (continuous) homomorphism.
Let
Q_{0}
be an infinite cyclic closed subgroup of
Q
.
Then there exists an infinite cyclic closed subgroup
P_{0}
of
P

such that
h(P_{0})=Q_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
q_{0}
be a generator of
Q_{0}
.
Since
h:P\to Q
is surjective, fix
p_{0}\in P
such that
h(p_{0})=q_{0}
.
Let
P_{0}
be the cyclic subgroup of
P
generated by
p_{0}
.
Then
P_{0}
is a cyclic subgroup of
P
and
h(P_{0})=Q_{0}
.
Since
Q_{0}
is infinite and since
h(P_{0})=Q_{0}
, it follows that
P_{0}
is infinite as well.
It remains to prove that
P_{0}
is a closed subset of
P
.

Let
{\overline{P}}_{0}
be the closure in
P
of
P_{0}
.
Because
Q_{0}
is closed in
Q
and because
h:P\to Q
is continuous,
it follows that
h^{-1}(Q_{0})
is closed in
P
.
So, since
P_{0}\subseteq h^{-1}(Q_{0})
,
we conclude that
{\overline{P}}_{0}\subseteq h^{-1}(Q_{0})
,

i.e.
, that
h(\,{\overline{P}}_{0})\subseteq Q_{0}
.
Then
Q_{0}=h(P_{0})\subseteq h(\,{\overline{P}}_{0})\subseteq Q_{0}
.
Then
h(\,{\overline{P}}_{0})=Q_{0}
.

Give
Q_{0}
its relative topology inherited from
Q
.
Then
Q_{0}
is a countable, locally compact, Hausdorff topological space.
Every point in a
T_{1}
topological space is either open or nowhere dense.
Then, by the Baire Category Theorem,
Q_{0}
has an open point.
Then
Q_{0}
is a topological group with an open point, so
Q_{0}
is discrete.
So, since
Q_{0}
is infinite,
Q_{0}
is noncompact.
Then, since
h(\,{\overline{P}}_{0}\,)=Q_{0}
, we see that
{\overline{P}}_{0}
is also noncompact.
Then, by Lemma
17.1
,

P_{0}
is a closed subset of
P
.
∎

LEMMA 17.3
.

If
G
is a connected real Lie group whose center
Z(G)
is noncompact,
then
Z(G)
has an infinite cyclic closed subgroup.

Proof.

Let
Z:=Z(G)
and let
Z_{\circ}
be the identity component of
Z
.
Let
Z_{*}:=Z/(Z_{\circ})
have the discrete topology.

By Lemma 3.1 of
[
AO
]
,

Z_{*}
is finitely generated.
If
Z_{*}
is infinite,
then the Structure Theorem for Finitely Generated Abelian Groups
implies that
Z_{*}
contains an infinite cyclic subgroup,
so, by Lemma
17.2
, we are done.
We therefore assume that
Z_{*}=Z/(Z_{\circ})
is finite.
Then, since
Z
is noncompact, we see that
Z_{\circ}
is noncompact.

By Theorem 1 of §2.21.1
on p. 104 of
[
MG64
]
,
let
K
be a maximal compact subgroup of
Z_{\circ}
.
Let
A:=(Z_{\circ})/K
have its quotient topology
from the canonical map
Z_{\circ}\to A
.
Because
Z_{\circ}
is noncompact,
K\neq Z_{\circ}
,
so fix
a\in A\backslash\{1_{A}\}
.
Let
C
be the cyclic subgroup of
A
generated by
a
.
Let
{\overline{C}}
be the closure in
A
of
C
.
By maximality of
K
, any nontrivial closed subgroup of
A
is noncompact,
so
{\overline{C}}
is noncompact.
It follows, from Lemma
17.1
,
that
C
is a closed subgroup of
A
.
Moreover, as
{\overline{C}}
is noncompact,
we know that
C
is infinite.
Thus
C
is an infinite cyclic closed subgroup of
A=(Z_{\circ})/K
.
By Lemma
17.2
, there exists an infinite cyclic closed subgroup
C_{1}
of
Z_{\circ}
.
As
Z_{\circ}
is an open subgroup of
Z
,
Z_{\circ}
is a closed subset of
Z
.
Then
C_{1}
is a closed subset of
Z
.
∎

18.
The counterexamples

Note that Theorem
18.1
below applies
when
G
is discrete and isomorphic to the additive group
{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Lemma
17.3
shows that Theorem
18.1
also applies
when
G
is a connected real Lie group whose center
Z(G)
is noncompact.
Also note, by (ii) below that, if

\hbox{either}\qquad G\hbox{ is connected }\qquad\hbox{or}\qquad G=Z,

then
M
is connected.

THEOREM 18.1
.

Let
G
be a real Lie group.
Assume the center
Z(G)
of
G

admits an infinite cyclic closed subgroup
Z
.
Then there is a
C^{\infty}
manifold
M
and
a fixpoint rare
C^{\infty}
action of
G
on
M

such that:

(i)

for any integer
k\geq 0
,
there is a dense subset
D
of
F_{k}M
such that,
for all
\delta\in D
, the stabilizer
\hbox{Stab}_{Z}(\delta)
is infinite   and

(ii)

the number of connected components of
M
and
G/Z
are equal.

Proof.

We have
(V_{0},I_{0})\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Also,
{\mathcal{U}}(V_{0})={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, so
V_{0}
is porous.

Let
|\bullet|:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to[0,\infty)
be a norm on
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Let
\{\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3},\ldots\}
be a countable dense subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Choose
\sigma_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
such that
|\sigma_{1}-\omega_{1}|<1
.
Then
\sigma_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}={\mathcal{U}}(V_{0})
.

By Lemma
16.1
,
choose
(V_{1},I_{1})\in{\mathcal{M}}(V_{0},I_{0})

such that
V_{1}
is porous and
such that
(V_{1},\sigma_{1})
is periodic to all orders.
Because
V_{1}
is porous,
{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})
is dense in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,
so fix
\sigma_{2}\in{\mathcal{U}}(V_{1})
such that
|\sigma_{2}-\omega_{2}|<1/2
.

By Lemma
16.1
,
choose
(V_{2},I_{2})\in{\mathcal{M}}(V_{1},I_{1})

such that
V_{2}
is porous and
such that
(V_{2},\sigma_{2})
is periodic to all orders.
Because
V_{2}
is porous,
{\mathcal{U}}(V_{2})
is dense in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,
so fix
\sigma_{3}\in{\mathcal{U}}(V_{2})
such that
|\sigma_{3}-\omega_{3}|<1/3
.

By Lemma
16.1
,
choose
(V_{3},I_{3})\in{\mathcal{M}}(V_{2},I_{2})

such that
V_{3}
is porous and
such that
(V_{3},\sigma_{3})
is periodic to all orders.
Because
V_{3}
is porous,
{\mathcal{U}}(V_{3})
is dense in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,
so fix
\sigma_{4}\in{\mathcal{U}}(V_{3})
such that
|\sigma_{4}-\omega_{4}|<1/4
.

Continuing yields a countable dense subset

\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\ldots\}
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,
and a sequence
(V_{1},I_{1}),(V_{2},I_{2}),(V_{3},I_{3}),\ldots
in
{\mathcal{D}}
.
For each integer
j\geq 1
,

•

a_{I_{j+1}}>a_{I_{j}}
,

•

V_{j+1}=V_{j}
on
I_{j}^{4}
,

•

V_{j}
is porous   and

•

(V_{j},\sigma_{j})
is periodic to all orders.

We have
a_{I_{1}}<a_{I_{2}}<a_{I_{3}}<\cdots
and
a_{I_{1}},a_{I_{2}},a_{I_{3}},\ldots\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
It follows both that
I_{1}\subseteq I_{2}\subseteq I_{3}\subseteq\cdots

and that
I_{1}\cup I_{2}\cup I_{3}\cup\cdots={\mathbb{R}}
.
Then
I_{1}^{4}\subseteq I_{2}^{4}\subseteq I_{3}^{4}\subseteq\cdots

and
I_{1}^{4}\cup I_{2}^{4}\cup I_{3}^{4}\cup\cdots={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Define
V_{\infty}\in{\mathcal{C}}
by the rule:
For all integers
j\geq 1
,
V_{\infty}=V_{j}
on
I_{j}^{4}
.
Let
\displaystyle{X:=\bigcap_{k=1}^{\infty}({\mathcal{U}}(V_{k}))}
.

Claim 1:
For all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}
,
for all
\sigma\in X
, we have

\Phi_{t}^{V_{\infty}}(\sigma)\neq\sigma
.

Proof of Claim 1:

Let
t\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}
and let
\sigma\in X
.
Assume, for a contradiction, that we have
\Phi_{t}^{V_{\infty}}(\sigma)=\sigma
.

Since
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{\infty}}(\sigma)=\Phi_{[0,t]}^{V_{\infty}}(\sigma)
,
we see that
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{\infty}}(\sigma)
is compact.
Then, since
I_{1}^{4}\subseteq I_{2}^{4}\subseteq I_{3}^{4}\subseteq\cdots

and
I_{1}^{4}\cup I_{2}^{4}\cup I_{3}^{4}\cup\cdots={\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,
fix an integer
j\geq 1
such that

\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{\infty}}(\sigma)\subseteq I_{j}^{4}
.
We have
V_{j}=V_{\infty}
on
I_{j}^{4}
.
Then, by Lemma
6.1
,

\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{j}}(\sigma)=\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{\infty}}(\sigma)
.
Then

\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{j}}(\sigma))=\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{\infty}}(\sigma))\subseteq\Pi_{4}(I_{j}^{4})=I_{j}
.
However, because
\sigma\in X\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}(V_{j})
,
it follows that
\Pi_{4}(\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{j}}(\sigma))={\mathbb{R}}
.
Thus we have
{\mathbb{R}}\subseteq I_{j}
, contradiction.

End of proof of Claim 1.

Claim 2:
For all integers
j\geq 1
,
(V_{\infty},\sigma_{j})
is periodic to all orders.

Proof of Claim 2:

Fix an integer
j\geq 1
.
We wish to show that
(V_{\infty},\sigma_{j})
is periodic to all orders.

Because
(V_{j},I_{j})\in{\mathcal{D}}
and
(V_{j},\sigma_{j})
is periodic,
we conclude, from Lemma
8.13
,
that
\Phi_{\mathbb{R}}^{V_{j}}(\sigma_{j})\subseteq I_{j}^{4}
.
For all
\tau\in I_{j}^{4}
,
because
V_{\infty}
and
V_{j}
agree on
I_{j}^{4}
,
which is an open neighborhood of
\tau
,
it follows that they agree to all orders at
\tau
.
So, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

V_{\infty}
and
V_{j}
agree to all orders at
\Phi_{t}^{V_{j}}(\sigma_{j})
.
Then, by Lemma
6.3
, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\Phi_{t}^{V_{\infty}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
and
\Phi_{t}^{V_{j}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
agree to all orders at
\sigma_{j}
.
So, as
(V_{j},\sigma_{j})
is periodic to all orders,
it follows
(V_{\infty},\sigma_{j})
is periodic to all orders as well.

End of proof of Claim 2.

Because
Z
is infinite cyclic,
it follows that
Z
is isomorphic to the additive discrete group
{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Let
f:Z\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be an isomorphism.
Define a
Z
-action on
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by:
for all
z\in Z
, for all
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,
z\sigma=\Phi_{f(z)}^{V_{\infty}}(\sigma)
.
Let
M:=G\times_{Z}{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Because the
Z
-action on
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
is
C^{\infty}
,
it follows that the
G
-action on
M
is
C^{\infty}
as well.
By construction,
M
is (the total space of) a fiber bundle over
G/Z
with fiber
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,
so, because
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
is connected,

M
has the same number of connected components as does
G/Z
.

By Corollary
8.4
(i),
{\mathcal{U}}(V)
is an open subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
For all integers
k\geq 1
,
V_{k}
is porous.
Then
{\mathcal{U}}(V_{k})
is a dense open subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
Then, because

\displaystyle{X=\bigcap_{k=1}^{\infty}({\mathcal{U}}(V_{k}))}
,
we see, by the Baire Category Theorem,
that
X
is dense in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
By Claim 1, for all
z\in Z\backslash\{1_{Z}\}
, for all
\sigma\in X
,
we have
\Phi_{f(z)}^{V_{\infty}}(\sigma)\neq\sigma
,

i.e.
, we have
z\sigma\neq\sigma
.
Thus the
Z
-action on
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
is fixpoint rare.
Then the
G
-action on
M
is also fixpoint rare.

Let
p:G\times{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to G\times_{Z}{\mathbb{R}}^{4}=M
be the canonical map.
Define an injection
\iota:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to M
by
\iota(\sigma)=p(1_{G},\sigma)
.
Let
\Sigma:=\iota(\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\ldots\})
.
Because
\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\ldots\}
is dense in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,
A:=G\Sigma
is dense in
M
.

Fix an integer
k\geq 0
,
and let
\pi:=\pi_{k}^{M}:F_{k}M\to M
be the
k
th order frame bundle of
M
.
Let
D:=\pi^{-1}(A)
.
Since
A
is dense in
M
, and since
\pi:F_{k}M\to M
is open,
it follows that
D
is dense in
F_{k}M
.
Fix
\delta\in D
.
We wish to show that
\hbox{Stab}_{Z}(\delta)
is infinite.

Let
S:=\hbox{Stab}_{G}(\delta)
.
Then
\hbox{Stab}_{Z}(\delta)=S\cap Z
.
We therefore wish to show that
S\cap Z
is infinite.

Since
\pi(\delta)\in A=G\Sigma
,
fix
g_{0}\in G
,
\tau_{0}\in\Sigma
such that
\pi(\delta)=g_{0}\tau_{0}
.
Let
\delta_{0}:=g_{0}^{-1}\delta
.
Then
\pi(\delta_{0})=\tau_{0}
,

i.e.
,
\delta_{0}\in\pi^{-1}(\tau_{0})
.
Let
S_{0}:=\hbox{Stab}_{G}(\delta_{0})
.
Then
S=g_{0}S_{0}g_{0}^{-1}
.
As
Z\subseteq Z(G)
, we have
Z=g_{0}Zg_{0}^{-1}
.
Then
S\cap Z=g_{0}(S_{0}\cap Z)g_{0}^{-1}
,
so it suffices to show that
S_{0}\cap Z
is infinite.

Recall that
\hbox{Id}_{4}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
is the identity map defined by
\hbox{Id}_{4}(\sigma)=\sigma
.
Let
\hbox{Id}:M\to M
be the identity map defined by
\hbox{Id}(\rho)=\rho
.

Since
\tau_{0}\in\Sigma=\iota(\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\ldots\})
, fix an integer
j\geq 1

such that
\tau_{0}=\iota(\sigma_{j})
.
By Claim 2, fix
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\backslash\{0\}

such that
\Phi_{n_{0}}^{V_{\infty}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
agrees with the identity
\hbox{Id}_{4}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}

to all orders at
\sigma_{j}
.

Let
z_{0}:=f^{-1}(n_{0})
.
Then
z_{0}\in Z\backslash\{1_{Z}\}
,
and, for all
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, we have
z_{0}\sigma=\Phi_{n_{0}}^{V_{\infty}}(\sigma)
.
Then the map
\sigma\mapsto z_{0}\sigma:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}

is equal to
\Phi_{n_{0}}^{V_{\infty}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
,
and therefore agrees with
\hbox{Id}_{4}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
to all orders at
\sigma_{j}
.
Then, since
Z\subseteq Z(G)
and since
\iota(\sigma_{j})=\tau_{0}
,
it follows that the map
\rho\mapsto z_{0}\rho:M\to M

agrees with
\hbox{Id}:M\to M
at
\tau_{0}
to all orders.
In particular,
\rho\mapsto z_{0}\rho:M\to M

agrees with
\hbox{Id}:M\to M
at
\tau_{0}
to order
k
.
Then, for all
\rho\in\pi^{-1}(\tau_{0})
, we have
z_{0}\rho=\rho
.
So, since
\delta_{0}\in\pi^{-1}(\tau_{0})
, we get
z_{0}\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}
.
That is,
z_{0}\in\hbox{Stab}_{Z}(\delta_{0})=S_{0}\cap Z
.
Let
C_{0}
be the cyclic subgroup of
S_{0}\cap Z
generated by
z_{0}
.
Every nontrivial subgroup of an infinite cyclic group is infinite,
so
C_{0}
is infinite.
So, because
C_{0}\subseteq S_{0}\cap Z
,
it follows that
S_{0}\cap Z
is infinite, as desired.
∎
